Quantum S ​ L + ​ ( N , ℝ ) SL^{+}(N,\mathbb{R}) as a locally compact quantum group
K. De Commer, G. Schrader, A. Shapiro, C. Voigt

Abstract
. We construct the first examples of purely continuous, q q -deformed Lie type locally compact quantum groups in higher rank. They arise from Drinfeld–Jimbo quantization, at unimodular deformation parameter, of the totally positive part of higher rank split real Lie groups in type A A . Our techniques are based on quantum cluster theory, in particular as developed through the work of Fock and Goncharov.



Introduction

Positive representations arise in the representation theory of split real quantum groups, and are deeply connected with quantum cluster algebras and higher quantum Teichmüller theory. They were first studied by Ponsot–Teschner
[
PT99
,
PT01
]
in the rank
1
case in connection with Liouville theory, and later on in general type by Ip and Frenkel–Ip
[
Ip12
,
FI14
,
Ip15
]
. A key feature of these representations is that the standard generators of the quantized universal enveloping algebra act by positive self-adjoint (unbounded) operators. This resembles classical Lie algebra representations arising from unitary representations of split semisimple Lie groups, except that, in this analogy, the positivity requirement corresponds to looking only at the totally positive part of the underlying groups.

The main result of the present paper is the construction of a quantization
SL^{+}_{\hbar}(N,\mathbb{R})
of the semigroup
SL^{+}(N,\mathbb{R})
of totally positive matrices in
SL(N,\mathbb{R})
as a locally compact quantum group in the sense of Kustermans–Vaes
[
KV00
,
KV03
]
. Here
\hbar
is a nonzero real parameter, related to the usual
q
in the algebraic setting by
q=e^{\pi i\hbar}
. In the above analogy, this provides the analytic foundations for a global approach to the study of positive representations of quantum groups, and allows one to bring the powerful machinery of operator algebras into the picture. At the same time, it yields the first examples of Drinfeld–Jimbo-type higher rank locally compact quantum groups which are genuinely outside the realm of algebraic constructions.

Historically, the theory of locally compact quantum groups originated from attempts to generalize Pontryagin duality to the nonabelian setting, see
[
Kus05
]
for an overview. To a large part, the structure of a locally compact quantum group is governed by a
multiplicative unitary
, encoding the analogue of the regular representation of a locally compact group. The concept of a multiplicative unitary was studied earlier on by Baaj–Skandalis
[
BS93
]
, and developed further by Woronowicz
[
Wor96
]
, who introduced the notion of
manageability
, later generalized to
modularity
in
[
SW01
]
. The key insight of Kustermans and Vaes was that requiring the existence of Haar weights allows one to build an elegant and extremely versatile theory of locally compact quantum groups from a simple set of axioms, closed under Pontryagin duality and encompassing all known examples at the time. They showed in particular that the
Kac–Takesaki operator
of a locally compact quantum group is a manageable multiplicative unitary, thus linking their approach to the work of Woronowicz.

However, apart from constructions of an essentially algebraic nature, only a few isolated examples of genuine locally compact quantum groups of
q
-deformation type were hitherto known, each involving subtle analytic considerations. These examples are limited to low rank
[
VD01
,
WZ02
,
Wor03
,
KK03
]
, with case by case techniques.

One of these examples is the quantum ‘
ax+b
’-group introduced by Woronowicz–Zakrzewski
[
WZ02
]
. For the analysis of this quantum group, Woronowicz studied the quantum exponential function
[
Wor00
]
, which is closely related to Faddeev’s noncompact quantum dilogarithm
[
Fad95
,
Kas01
,
FG09
]
. Most of the intricacies of the quantum ‘
ax+b
’-group are due to the fact that the operators involved are only assumed to be self-adjoint, possibly with negative parts in their spectrum.

It was first realised by Ip
[
Ip13
]
that the work of Woronowicz–Zakrzewski implicitly contains all ingredients needed for a
totally positive
version of the quantum ‘
ax+b
’-group, see also
[
BT03
]
. This is surprising in at least two ways: firstly, the classical counterpart of the totally positive ‘
ax+b
’-quantum group is only a semigroup, and not a group, and secondly, working with positive self-adjoint operators simplifies the analysis significantly. Ip showed that one obtains a modular multiplicative unitary in this situation, and one also has Haar weights, thus giving a locally compact quantum group. One should view this positive ‘
ax+b
’-quantum group as corresponding to a Borel in the associated totally positive quantum
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-group, obtained via the Drinfeld double construction.

This is the starting point of our work.
The main algebraic ingredient we use to go beyond the rank 1 case is
quantum cluster theory.
Cluster algebras were first introduced in the commutative setting by Fomin and Zelevinsky in the context of (dual) canonical bases in Lie theory
[
Lus93
]
, and the theory was shown to admit a natural
q
-deformation by Berenstein and Zelevinsky in
[
BZ05
]
, as well as by Fock and Goncharov
[
FG09
]
as part of their work on the quantization of higher Teichmüller spaces. Starting from the data of a
quiver
, a quantum cluster algebra is defined as the intersection of a collection of mildly non-commutative algebras known as
quantum tori
or skew-Laurent polynomial rings. The precise form of the intersection is governed by the combinatorics of
cluster mutation
, with the intersection being taken over all quivers
mutation-equivalent
to the original. A quantum cluster algebra contains a distinguished class of elements known as
global monomials
(or ‘cluster monomials’). For a large class of quivers, it is proven in
[
DM21
]
(and
[
GHKK18
]
at
q=1
) that the expansion of a global monomial relative to the quantum tori of an arbitrary quiver is a skew-Laurent polynomial whose coefficients are elements of
\mathbb{N}[q^{\pm 1}]
invariant under the bar involution
q\mapsto q^{-1}
. In the cases relevant for our purposes, we have in fact an explicit combinatorial description of these expansion coefficients in terms of partition functions of paths in a directed graph.

The setting of quantum tori and
q
-Laurent positivity is particularly attractive from an analytic point of view. On the one hand, there is in this setting a well-developed operator-algebraic deformation theory due to Rieffel
[
Rie93
]
. On the other, Laurent positivity guarantees that each summand in the expansion of a global monomial corresponds to a positive self-adjoint operator. By taking
form sums
of such summands, we are able to make sense of the global monomials corresponding to PBW generators of the quantum group in various clusters as rather explicit positive operators. This perspective seems to be new, and we hope it will find further applications in the analytic theory of quantum cluster algebras initiated by Fock and Goncharov in
[
FG09
]
.

In
[
FG09
]
, it is explained that to each cluster mutation one can associate a unitary known as a quantum mutation operator. It is these quantum mutation operators that we will use for the construction of the multiplicative unitary for
SL^{+}_{\hbar}(N,\mathbb{R})
. In a little more detail, the particular cluster algebras we use in this paper are those considered by Fock and Goncharov in their work
[
FG06
]
on moduli spaces of framed
SL(N,\mathbb{C})
local systems on surfaces. In this dictionary, the cluster algebra through which we describe the quantum Borel corresponds to a ‘triangle’, i.e. a disk with three marked points on its boundary. Fock and Goncharov construct an explicit composite of cluster mutations realizing the ‘flip’ intertwining the cluster charts corresponding to the two ways of gluing a disk with four marked points from a pair of triangles along a single edge, and it is the corresponding quantum mutation operator that will serve as our multiplicative unitary.

Using the form sum construction, we are able to show that the same operator can be obtained via functional calculus from the general factorized form of the
R
-matrix for the quantum group, interpreted as an element of the tensor square of the Heisenberg double of the quantum Borel. On the other hand, the cluster-theoretic description of our multiplicative unitary is highly amenable to explicit computation, a feature which we exploit heavily in verifying its compatibility with the locally compact quantum group axioms.

We would like to point out that Ip pioneered the study of positive quantum groups, also in higher rank. In
[
Ip12
,
Ip13
,
Ip17
]
, he proposed the theory of
multiplier Hopf algebras
due to Van Daele
[
VD98
]
to approach this problem. Multiplier Hopf algebras with integrals, or more precisely algebraic quantum groups in the sense of
[
KVD97
]
, give rise to a well-behaved class of locally compact quantum groups. These exhibit many features of the general theory, but are still close to the purely algebraic theory of Hopf algebras. However, the analytic side of discussion in
[
Ip12
,
Ip13
,
Ip17
]
lacks mathematical rigor, and some of the claims in these papers are only valid on a heuristic level. As we will show, the scaling constant of the Borel part of
SL^{+}_{\hbar}(N,\mathbb{R})
is different from
1
, which means that it cannot be obtained as an algebraic quantum group
[
DCVD10
]
.

Our construction of
SL^{+}_{\hbar}(N,\mathbb{R})
as a locally compact quantum group opens several avenues for further exploration, some of which we now outline.
Since the inception of the theory, it has been very desirable to define a meaningful ‘category of positive representations’ arising from cluster-type realizations of quantum groups.
1
1

1

An attempt at describing algebraic structures that may underlie such a definition has been made in
[
SS17
]
, a preprint with an overly optimistic title.

We regard the construction of the locally compact quantum group
SL^{+}_{\hbar}(N,\mathbb{R})
presented here as the first step towards addressing this issue. In future work, we hope to make aspects of the representation categories of
SL^{+}_{\hbar}(N,\mathbb{R})
more concrete, including the Plancherel formula and an explicit description of the reduced unitary dual.

Another direction for further exploration consists of determining to which extent the representation theory of
SL^{+}_{\hbar}(N,\mathbb{R})
can be ‘integrated’ over more general surfaces
[
BZBJ18
]
to define an analog of a topological quantum field theory. This question seems closely related to the infinite dimensional analog of a modular functor arising in the work
[
FG09
]
of Fock and Goncharov on quantized higher Teichmüller theory.

Finally, we would like to draw the reader’s attention to the fact that all our constructions work for all
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, in particular they remain valid when
q=e^{\pi i\hbar}
is a root of unity. We note that the constructions are sensitive to the value of
\hbar
rather than that of
q
, in particular we obtain a locally compact quantum group at
\hbar=2
which corresponds to
q=1
. On a related note, in the algebraic study of positive representations, the notion of a
modular double
plays a prominent role, going back to work of Faddeev
[
Fad95
]
. This encompasses two formally commuting copies of the quantized universal enveloping algebra, at dual deformation parameters
q=e^{i\pi\hbar}
and
\check{q}=e^{\pi i\hbar^{-1}}
. Modular duality is a mathematical reflection of the duality properties of Liouville theory and its generalizations. In the analytic setting, this is already built into the construction of the locally compact quantum group, and presents itself in our context as an isomorphism between
SL^{+}_{\hbar}(n,\mathbb{R})
and
SL^{+}_{\hbar^{-1}}(n,\mathbb{R})
, identifying the corresponding multiplicative unitaries.

Let us now come to the contents of this paper.

In the
first section
, we present some basic facts on vector spaces with a skew-symmetric bilinear form, and study their associated representation theory.

In the
second section
, we construct the multiplicative unitary that will turn out to be associated to a locally compact quantum group, namely the quantization
\mathbb{B}_{N}^{+}
of the Borel part of
SL^{+}(N,\mathbb{R})
. We present two formulas for this multiplicative unitary: one based directly on the formula for the universal
R
-matrix in quantum group theory, and one constructed from a particular Laurent polynomial chart for (a reduction of) the Heisenberg algebra associated to the quantum group.

In the
third section
, we prove the fundamental fact that our multiplicative unitary is
modular
, which already unlocks many of the desired features for the associated representation theory. Our strategy is to decompose the multiplicative unitary, viewed as a representation of the quantum group, as a tensor product of ‘rank one’-representations, for which the associated modularity is already known.

In the
fourth section
, we determine the von Neumann algebras underlying the above modular multiplicative unitary, which are to be seen as the quantized measureable spaces underlying our quantum group. These von Neumann algebras are easily described as twisted group von Neumann algebras, but to get at this result we need access to the more refined C
∗
-algebraic theory, using results of Rieffel’s deformation theory
[
Rie93
]
. At the end of this section, we also briefly comment on the phenomenon of modular duality.

In the
fifth section
, we obtain our main result, showing that our modular multiplicative unitary leads to quantum groups admitting Haar measures. The crucial idea here is based on fundamental insights of Woronowicz and Van Daele
[
VD01
,
Wor03
]
, together with a flexibility to use irreducible representations of the (degenerate) Heisenberg algebra when studying our multiplicative unitary.

In the
sixth section
, we give a complete description of the modular data associated to our quantum group, such as modular elements, modular operators and modular conjugations.

In the
seventh section
, we consider the duality theory for our quantum group, and determine a formula for the associated left regular multiplicative unitary, or equivalently, the Kac–Takesaki operator, in terms of the multiplicative unitary that we started with.

Finally, in the
eighth section
we show how one can apply the Drinfeld double construction for the quantized Borel group, to arrive (via the results of
[
BV05
]
) at the desired quantization
SL^{+}_{\hbar}(N,\mathbb{R})
as a locally compact quantum group, of the semi-group
SL^{+}(N,\mathbb{R})
of totally positive special linear matrices.

The paper is concluded with a long list of appendices, gathering some known results that might be less accessible to readers from one of the different communities addressed by this paper (locally compact quantum groups and quantum cluster algebras).

In
Appendix A
, we gather some analytic information on the quantum dilogarithm function that is crucial for the development of the whole theory.

In
Appendix B
, we shortly explain the proof of the pentagon equation associated to the quantum dilogarithm.

In
Appendix C
, we comment on the proof of modularity for the rank
1
case (‘
ax+b
’-group), following
[
WZ02
]
.

In
Appendix D
, we explain some technical concepts relating to C
∗
-algebras (multipliers and affiliated elements).

In
Appendix E
we explain how C
∗
-algebras can be deformed when acted on by a vector space with a skew-bilinear form, following
[
Rie93
]
.

In
Appendix F
, we give a brief overview of the theory of von Neumann algebras and their associated weight theory.

Finally, in
Appendix G
we present a direct algebraic proof of the pentagon equation for the multiplicative unitary constructed in Section
2
.

Conventions and notations
:
For a complex-valued function
f
with domain
X
, we write

\overline{f}(x)=\overline{f(x)},\qquad x\in X.

For
X
a locally compact Hausdorff space, we write
C_{b}(X)
for the C
∗
-algebra of bounded continuous complex-valued functions on
X
with pointwise algebra structure and
*
-operation
f\mapsto\overline{f}
. We write
C_{0}(X)\subseteq C_{b}(X)
for its C
∗
-subalgebra of functions vanishing at infinity.

For
A
a complex algebra,
M
a left
A
-module and
V\subseteq M
a linear subspace, we write

AV=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}v_{i}\mid n\geq 1,a_{i}\in A,v_{i}\in V\right\}\subseteq M.

We denote by
[-]
the norm-closure of the linear span of a set inside a normed space, or
[-]^{\tau}
if we take the closure with respect to another linear topology
\tau
.

We take inner products for Hilbert spaces anti-linear in the first factor. We write
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
for the von Neumann algebra of bounded operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
for the C
∗
-algebra of compact operators. Then for
\mathcal{H}
a Hilbert space and
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}
, we write
\omega_{\xi,\eta}
for the vector functional

(0.1)

\omega_{\xi,\eta}\colon\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\qquad\omega_{\xi,\eta}(x)=\langle\xi,x\eta\rangle,\qquad x\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}),

and we denote by
\theta_{\xi,\eta}
the rank
1
-operator

(0.2)

\theta_{\xi,\eta}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}),\qquad\theta_{\xi,\eta}(\zeta)=\langle\eta,\zeta\rangle\xi,\qquad\zeta\in\mathcal{H}.

We denote by

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}=\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})^{*}=[\omega_{\xi,\eta}\mid\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}]\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})^{*}

the space of normal functionals on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. We also adapt these notations to spaces of operators between different Hilbert spaces.

We generically use boldface symbols to indicate unbounded operators on Hilbert spaces. For
\mathbf{T}
an unbounded operator on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, we denote by
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{T})\subseteq\mathcal{H}
its domain, which by default we assume dense in
\mathcal{H}
. When
\mathbf{S},\mathbf{T}
are two unbounded operators with
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{S})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{T})
dense in
\mathcal{H}
, we denote by
\mathbf{S}+\mathbf{T}
the sum operator on
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{S})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{T})
. When
\mathbf{S},\mathbf{T}
are closed and
\mathbf{S}+\mathbf{T}
is closeable, we write
\mathbf{S}\dotplus\mathbf{T}
for the closure of
\mathbf{S}+\mathbf{T}
.

A self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space is by default allowed to be unbounded (with dense domain). By a
positive operator
we will always mean a self-adjoint operator with spectrum contained in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and by a
strictly positive operator
an invertible positive operator (i.e. with
0
not in the point spectrum). We frequently use functional calculus applied to self-adjoint operators, see e.g.
[
Sch12
, Chapter 5]
for an exposition.

We denote by
\otimes
the tensor product of Hilbert spaces, or the minimal tensor product of C
∗
-algebras. We denote by
\bar{\otimes}
the spatial tensor product of von Neumann algebras. We write
\odot
for algebraic tensor products of vector spaces when disambiguation is needed. For example, we denote by
\mathbf{H}_{1}\odot\mathbf{H}_{2}
the algebraic tensor product of self-adjoint operators
\mathbf{H}_{i}
, whose domain is the algebraic tensor product
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{H}_{1})\odot\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{H}_{2})
, while we denote by
\mathbf{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{H}_{2}
the closure of
\mathbf{H}_{1}\odot\mathbf{H}_{2}
, which is then again self-adjoint, resp. (strictly) positive if both
\mathbf{H}_{i}
are (
[
RS81
, Section VIII.10]
).

Between tensor products of vector spaces or Hilbert spaces, we use
\Sigma
to denote the flip map:

\Sigma\colon v\otimes w\mapsto w\otimes v.

We indicate standard leg numbering notation for operators
X
between Hilbert spaces by
X_{ij...}
, so e.g. if
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3}
are Hilbert spaces and
X\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{3})
, then

X_{23}=1\otimes X\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{3}),\qquad X_{32}=1\otimes\Sigma X\Sigma\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{3}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}),\qquad\textrm{etc.}

Fellowship.

1.
Skew-symmetric spaces and their representations

1.1.
Skew-symmetric spaces

Definition 1.1
.

A
skew-symmetric space

(V,\epsilon)
is a finite-dimensional real vector space
V
, equipped with a skew-symmetric bilinear form

\epsilon\colon V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\qquad\epsilon(v,w)=-\epsilon(w,v),\qquad v,w\in V.

We call
V

symplectic
if
\epsilon
is non-degenerate, and
trivial
if
\epsilon=0
.

The collection of skew-symmetric spaces is closed under taking subspaces, finite direct sums and conjugates

V=(V,\epsilon)\mapsto\overline{V}=(V,-\epsilon).

Formally, it is convenient to change the notation for the underlying vector space of
\overline{V}
by writing its elements as
\overline{v}
, with
\overline{v}
corresponding to
v\in V
.

We drop
\epsilon
from the notation if it is clear from the context, i.e. we write

(v,w)=\epsilon(v,w),\qquad v,w\in V.

To any skew-symmetric space
V
we can associate its
radical

\mathrm{Rad}(V)=\{v\in V\mid(v,-)=0\}\subseteq V.

Then
\mathrm{Rad}(V)
is a trivial skew-symmetric subspace of
V
, and
\epsilon
passes to the quotient

(1.1)

V_{\mathrm{red}}=V/\mathrm{Rad}(V),

with
V_{\mathrm{red}}
symplectic. We call
V_{\mathrm{red}}
the
reduction
of
V
.

If
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
is any subspace complementary to
\mathrm{Rad}(V)
, then
V^{\prime}
is a symplectic subspace of
V
, isomorphic to
V_{\mathrm{red}}
through the quotient map. We can in particular decompose
V
as an internal direct sum of skew-symmetric subspaces

(1.2)

V=\mathrm{Rad}(V)\oplus V^{\prime}.

A
Lagrangian subspace
of the symplectic space
V^{\prime}
is any trivially skew-symmetric subspace
W\subseteq V^{\prime}
of maximal dimension. For such a subspace one can select a Lagrangian complementary subspace
\hat{W}
in
V^{\prime}
, called a
dual
for
W
, so that in particular the pairing between
W
and
\hat{W}
is non-degenerate. Then associated to a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}\}
of
W
is a unique
dual
basis
\{\hat{e}_{1},\ldots,\hat{e}_{m}\}
of
\hat{W}
such that

(\hat{e}_{i},e_{j})=\delta_{ij},\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq m.

Writing
Z_{i}=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{e_{i},\hat{e}_{i}\}
, the
Z_{i}\subseteq V^{\prime}
are two-dimensional symplectic vector subspaces, and (
1.2
) can be further refined into an internal direct sum of skew-symmetric subspaces

(1.3)

V=\mathrm{Rad}(V)\oplus V^{\prime}=\mathrm{Rad}(V)\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}Z_{i}.

1.2.
Representations of skew-symmetric spaces

Definition 1.2
.

Let
V
a skew-symmetric space, and view
(V,+)
as a locally compact group.

For
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}=\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, a
unitary
\hbar
-representation of
V
is a strongly continuous unitary
\Omega
-projective representation
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
of
(V,+)
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, where
\Omega
is the unitary
2
-cocycle

\Omega(v,w)=\Omega_{\hbar}(v,w)=e^{-\pi i\hbar(v,w)},\qquad v,w\in V.

Spelled out, this means that
\pi(v)
is a unitary operator on
\mathcal{H}
for each
v\in V
, that for each
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
the map
v\mapsto\pi(v)\xi
is norm-continuous, and that

(1.4)

\pi(v+w)=e^{-\pi i\hbar(v,w)}\pi(v)\pi(w),\qquad v,w\in V,

from which we get in particular that

(1.5)

\pi(v)\pi(w)=e^{2\pi i\hbar(v,w)}\pi(w)\pi(v),\qquad v,w\in V.

Remark 1.3
.

Note that in principle one can restrict to the case
\hbar=1
by subsuming
\hbar
into the skew-symmetric bilinear form. However, in certain contexts it is convenient to explicitly have
\hbar
around as a deformation parameter.

Remark 1.4
.

When
V
is trivial as a skew-symmetric space, Definition
1.2
becomes independent of
\hbar
, and just gives back the notion of (strongly continuous) unitary
V
-representation.

Subrepresentations, direct sums and irreducibility of unitary
\hbar
-representations are defined in the obvious way.

Example 1.5
.

Endowing
V
with Lebesgue measure,

L^{2}(V)
carries the
standard
unitary
\hbar
-representation
\pi=\pi_{\mathrm{st}}
of
V
, determined by

(1.6)

(\pi(v)g)(w)=e^{\pi i\hbar(v,w)}g(w-v),\qquad v,w\in V,g\in L^{2}(V).

Example 1.6
.

Assume
V
is symplectic, and choose complementary Lagrangian subspaces
W,\hat{W}\subseteq V
. Then
L^{2}(W)
carries a unique unitary
\hbar
-representation
\pi=\pi_{\mathrm{Heis}}
of
V
, the
Heisenberg
\hbar
-representation
(with respect to the polarisation
(W,\hat{W})
), such that

(\pi(w)f)(w^{\prime})=f(w^{\prime}-w),\quad(\pi(\hat{w})f)(w^{\prime})=e^{2\pi i\hbar(\hat{w},w^{\prime})}f(w^{\prime}),\\
f\in L^{2}(W),w,w^{\prime}\in W,\hat{w}\in\hat{W}.

Theorem 1.7

(Stone-von Neumann theorem)
.

If
V
is symplectic, all its Heisenberg
\hbar
-representations are irreducible and unitarily equivalent. Any other unitary
\hbar
-representation is a (possibly infinite) direct sum of Heisenberg
\hbar
-representations.

If
V
is a skew-symmetric space and
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
a unitary
\hbar
-representation, the one-parameter group of unitaries

\pi_{v}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}),\qquad t\mapsto\pi(tv),\qquad v\in V

has an infinitesimal generator

\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}_{\hbar}=\mathbf{v}_{\pi},\qquad v\in V,

so
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{v})
consists of those vectors
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
for which
t\mapsto\pi(tv)\xi
is norm-differentiable, with

\mathbf{v}\xi=-i\frac{d}{dt}(\pi(tv)\xi)_{\mid t=0},\qquad\xi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{v}).

The operator
\mathbf{v}
is self-adjoint, and we write

(1.7)

\mathbf{E}(v)=\mathbf{E}_{\hbar}(v)=\mathbf{E}_{\pi}(v)

for the strictly positive operator which is its exponential (using functional calculus):

(1.8)

\mathbf{E}(v)=e^{\mathbf{v}},\qquad\pi(tv)=\mathbf{E}(v)^{it}=e^{it\mathbf{v}},\qquad v\in V,t\in\mathbb{R}.

Then we can rewrite (
1.4
) as

(1.9)

\mathbf{E}(v+w)^{it}=e^{-\pi i\hbar t^{2}(v,w)}\mathbf{E}(v)^{it}\mathbf{E}(w)^{it},\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},v,w\in V.

In the following, we use the shorthand

(1.10)

\mathbf{E}(v)\star\mathbf{E}(w):=\mathbf{E}(v+w),\qquad v,w\in V.

We conclude this section with the following two operations for unitary
\hbar
-representations:

Definition 1.8
.

If
V_{1},V_{2}
are skew-symmetric spaces with unitary
\hbar
-representations
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
on respective Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}
, their
product
is the unitary
\hbar
-representation

(1.11)

\pi_{\otimes}\colon V_{1}\oplus V_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}),\qquad\pi_{\otimes}(v_{1}\oplus v_{2})=\pi_{1}(v_{1})\otimes\pi_{2}(v_{2}).

In terms of exponentials, this means

(1.12)

\mathbf{E}(v_{1}\oplus v_{2})=\mathbf{E}(v_{1})\otimes\mathbf{E}(v_{2}),\qquad v_{1}\in V_{1},v_{2}\in V_{2}.

Definition 1.9
.

If
V
is a skew-symmetric space with unitary
\hbar
-representation
\pi
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, its
conjugate
is the unitary
\hbar
-representation

(1.13)

\overline{\pi}\colon\overline{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\overline{\mathcal{H}}),\qquad\overline{\pi}(\overline{v})=\overline{\pi(-v)},

where
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
is the conjugate-linear Hilbert space of
\mathcal{H}
.

With this convention, we can write

(1.14)

\mathbf{E}(\overline{v})=\overline{\mathbf{E}(v)},\qquad v\in V.

1.3.
Gaussians, quantum dilogarithms and quantum exponential maps

Fix
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. Let
V_{1},V_{2}
be two skew-symmetric spaces. With respect to a product
\hbar
-representation, we define unitaries

(1.15)

G(v_{1},v_{2})=G_{\hbar}(v_{1},v_{2}):=e^{\frac{i\mathbf{v}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{v}_{2}}{2\pi\hbar}}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}),\qquad v_{i}\in V_{i},

referred to as
Gaussians
. Then for all
v_{i},w_{i}\in V_{i}
one computes by functional calculus that

(1.16)

G(v_{1},v_{2})\mathbf{E}(w_{1}\oplus w_{2})G(v_{1},v_{2})^{*}=\mathbf{E}((w_{1}+(v_{2},w_{2})v_{1})\oplus(w_{2}+(v_{1},w_{1})v_{2})),

(1.17)

G(v_{1},v_{2})^{*}\mathbf{E}(w_{1}\oplus w_{2})G(v_{1},v_{2})=\mathbf{E}((w_{1}+(w_{2},v_{2})v_{1})\oplus(w_{2}+(w_{1},v_{1})v_{2})).

More generally, if we have
z=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\otimes w_{i}\in V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
such that the
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
(resp.
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
) are mutually
\epsilon
-orthogonal, we put

(1.18)

G(z):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}G(v_{i},w_{i})\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2})

This is indeed independent of the ordering of the factors, and of the presentation of
z
.

Definition 1.10
.

For
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, consider

(1.19)

W=W_{1/\hbar}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\qquad W(z):=-\frac{\pi i}{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{dy}{y}\,\frac{e^{-2iyz}}{\sinh(2\pi y)\sinh(2\pi\hbar y)},\qquad z\in\mathbb{R},

with
\Omega
any curve in
\mathbb{C}
going from
-\infty
to
+\infty
on the real line, with a small bump in the upper half plane around the origin, staying below the poles of the denominator with strictly positive imaginary part.

We define the
(Faddeev) quantum dilogarithm function

\varphi=\varphi_{\hbar}
and the closely associated
(Woronowicz) quantum exponential function
2
2

2

In this terminology, one should think of
F
as an analogue of the exponential map in the form
t\mapsto e^{it}
, but restricted to the semigroup
(\mathbb{R}_{>0},+)
. See Theorem
1.16
below for a motivation of this interpretation.

F=F_{\hbar}
by

(1.20)

\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\qquad\varphi(t):=\exp\left(\frac{i}{2\pi}W(t)\right),\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},

(1.21)

F\colon\mathbb{R}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\qquad F(r)=\overline{\varphi}(\ln(r)),\qquad r>0.

We provide some more information on the quantum dilogarithm and its properties in Appendix
A
. For example, we gather directly from Lemma
A.1
that

•

W
is real-valued, hence
\varphi
and
F
take values on the unit circle,

•

F
has a continuous extension to
0
with

(1.22)

F(0)=1,

or equivalently,

•

\varphi
has a continuous extension to
-\infty
with

(1.23)

\varphi(-\infty)=1.

Given a unitary
\hbar
-representation of a skew-symmetric space
V
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, we use the following shorthand for the associated unitaries obtained through functional calculus:

(1.24)

\varphi(v)=\varphi(\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}),\qquad v\in V.

Theorem 1.11
.

For
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, the following identities hold:

(1.25)

\varphi(v)\varphi(w)=\varphi(w)\varphi(v),\qquad\forall v,w\in V\textrm{ with }(v,w)=0,

(1.26)

\varphi(v)\varphi(w)=\varphi(w)\varphi(v+w)\varphi(v),\qquad\forall v,w\in V\textrm{ with }(v,w)=1.

Here the first identity is basic functional calculus, while the second identity is the celebrated
Kashaev pentagon identity

[
FK94
,
Wor00
,
FKV01
]
. We refer to Appendix
B
for more information.

Definition 1.12
.

Two strictly positive operators
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}

skew-commute
if there exists
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
such that

(1.27)

\mathbf{A}^{it}\mathbf{B}^{is}=e^{2\pi i\hbar st}\mathbf{B}^{is}\mathbf{A}^{it},\qquad\forall s,t\in\mathbb{R}.

We then say more specifically that
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}

\hbar
-commute
.

In other words,
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
arise from a unitary
\hbar
-representation on
\mathcal{H}
of

V=\mathbb{R}a+\mathbb{R}b,\qquad(a,b)=1,

with
\mathbf{A}=e^{\mathbf{a}}
and
\mathbf{B}=e^{\mathbf{b}}
. Note that
\hbar
is uniquely determined by the pair
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
. We also recall the notation (
1.10
), by which
\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}\star\mathbf{B}^{\beta}
is the unique strictly positive operator such that

(1.28)

(\mathbf{A}^{\alpha}\star\mathbf{B}^{\beta})^{it}=e^{-\pi i\hbar\alpha\beta t^{2}}\mathbf{A}^{i\alpha t}\mathbf{B}^{i\beta t},\qquad\alpha,\beta,t\in\mathbb{R}.

Recall now the notion of
form sum

\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B}
of two positive operators
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
with dense intersection of the domains of their square roots: it is the unique positive operator such that

\mathscr{D}((\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B})^{1/2})=\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}),

\|(\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B})^{1/2}\xi\|^{2}=\|\mathbf{A}^{1/2}\xi\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{B}^{1/2}\xi\|^{2},\qquad\forall\xi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{A}^{1/2})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{B}^{1/2}).

See Section
F.3
for more information. We note that
\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B}
is strictly positive if
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
are strictly positive.

Remark 1.13
.

Let
V
be a skew-symmetric space, and choose on
V
a Euclidian structure. Let
V_{\mathbb{C}}
be its complexification, and consider for each
t>0,w\in V_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{C}
the function

g_{t,w,\kappa}(v)=e^{-t\|v\|^{2}+\langle w,v\rangle+\kappa},\qquad v\in V.

If
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
and
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
is a unitary
\hbar
-representation of a skew-symmetric space
V
, denote

\pi(f)\xi=\int_{V}f(v)\pi(v)\xi\mathrm{d}\!\,v,\qquad\xi\in\mathcal{H},f\in L^{1}(V),

and put

\mathcal{H}_{0}=\textrm{linear span}\{\pi(g_{t,w,\kappa})\xi\mid\xi\in\mathcal{H},t>0,w\in V_{\mathbb{C}},\kappa\in\mathbb{C}\}.

Then
\mathcal{H}_{0}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
, and for any
v\in V
we have

\mathcal{H}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{E}(v)),\qquad\mathbf{E}(v)\pi(g_{t,w,\kappa})\xi=\pi(g_{t,w+2tiv,\kappa+i\langle w,v\rangle})\xi,\qquad\xi\in\mathcal{H},t>0,w\in V_{\mathbb{C}},\kappa\in\mathbb{C}.

In particular, we can take the form sum of any finite collection of operators
\mathbf{E}(v_{1}),\ldots,\mathbf{E}(v_{n})
.

Proposition 1.14
.

Let
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. For
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}

\hbar
-commuting strictly positive operators, we have

(1.29)

\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B}=F(\mathbf{A}\star\mathbf{B}^{-1})^{*}\mathbf{B}F(\mathbf{A}\star\mathbf{B}^{-1}).

We present the proof of Proposition
1.14
in Section
F.3
. As far as we know, this result is new in this generality.

Remark 1.15
.

Following
[
Rui05
, Appendix B]
, one can show that when
|\hbar|<1
, the operator
\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B}
has domain
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{A})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{B})
, and coincides there with the algebraic sum of operators
\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}
(which has
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{A})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{B})
as domain by definition). When
|\hbar|>1
on the other hand, one has that the symmetric operator
\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}
is
not essentially self-adjoint
, but has
\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B}
as one of its self-adjoint extensions.

The following is referred to as the
quantum exponential property

[
Wor00
]
. Using Proposition
1.14
, it is a direct consequence of the Kashaev pentagon relation through functional calculus.

Theorem 1.16
.

Let
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. If
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
are
\hbar
-commuting strictly positive operators, then

(1.30)

F(\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B})=F(\mathbf{A})F(\mathbf{B}).

We also record the following property, which is immediate from (
1.29
):

Lemma 1.17
.

Assume
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{C}
are strictly positive operators such that
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
skew-commute, and such that there exists
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
such that
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C})
and
(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})

\hbar
-commute. Then also
\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{C}

\hbar
-commute, and

(1.31)

\mathbf{C}\star(\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B})=\mathbf{C}\star\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{C}\star\mathbf{B}.

2.
Pentagonal Fock–Goncharov flip

In the remainder of this paper, we fix
n\geq 1
a natural number, and put
N=n+1
.

2.1.
Skew-symmetric space of type
A_{n}

Definition 2.1
.

We define

(2.1)

C_{N}=\{(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}\mid a+b+c=N\}.

We write
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{N}
for the
\binom{N+2}{2}
-dimensional skew-symmetric space with basis vectors
e_{a,b,c}
for
(a,b,c)\in C_{N}
, with the only non-trivial positive pairings given by

(2.2)

\left(e_{a,b,c},e_{a+1,b-1,c}\right)=\left(e_{a,b,c},e_{a,b+1,c-1}\right)=\left(e_{a,b,c},e_{a-1,b,c+1}\right)\\
=\begin{cases}1/2&\text{if the common index is }0,\\
1&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We refer to
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{N}
as the
skew-symmetric space of type
A_{n}
(of order
N
).

We denote
C_{N}^{\prime}
for
C_{N}
with its corner vertices cut off, and we put
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}\subseteq\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{N}
for the vector subspace spanned by basis vectors labelled by
C_{N}^{\prime}
. We call
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
the ‘snubbed’ skew-symmetric space of type
A_{n}
, and
C_{N}^{\prime}
the associated snubbed diagram.

We picture the skew form diagrammatically by a graph with
C_{N}
as its nodes, and with an oriented arrow from
(a,b,c)
to
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime},c^{\prime})
if the skew product is
1
, resp. a dashed oriented arrow if the skew product is
1/2
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{400}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 310}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 220}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 130}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{040}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 301}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 211}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 121}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 031}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 202}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 112}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 022}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 103}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 013}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{004}}

Figure 1
.
The diagram
C_{4}
with the diagram
C_{4}^{\prime}
inside.

Definition 2.2
.

For
(a,b,c)\in C_{N}
and
k\geqslant 0
with
b+k\leq N
and
0\leq c-k
, we set

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{a,b,c}{a,b+k,c-k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}e_{a,b+j,c-j}\in\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{N},

and similarly for cyclic shifts of the columns/indices.

For example, we have

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{3,0,2}{3,1,1}\oplus\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{3,2,0}{1,2,2}=(e_{3,0,2}+e_{3,1,1})\oplus(e_{3,2,0}+e_{2,2,1}+e_{1,2,2})\in\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{5}^{\,\oplus 2}.

We also use the shorthand notation

(2.3)

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{N-s,0,s}{N-s,k,s-k},\qquad\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{s,N-s,0}{s-k,N-s,k},\qquad 0\leq k\leq s\leq N,

with the further shorthand.

(2.4)

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s}=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,s},\qquad\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s}=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,s},\qquad 0\leq s\leq N.

Similarly, we put

(2.5)

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{0,N-s,s}{k,N-s,s-k},\qquad\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{1}{N-s,s,0}{N-s,s-k,k},\qquad 0\leq k\leq s\leq N

and
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{s}=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{s,s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s}=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,s}
, so in particular

(2.6)

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s}=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s},\qquad\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{s}=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s},\qquad 0\leq s\leq N

and

(2.7)

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s}-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,s-k-1},\quad\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s}-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,s-k-1},\qquad 0\leq k<s\leq N.

.
The vectors
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{3,2}
,

and

as (undirected) paths in
C_{4}
.

We record here for convenience the following values for pairings between the above vectors.

Lemma 2.3
.

(1)

For all
1\leq s,s^{\prime}\leq n
,
0\leq k<s
and
0\leq k^{\prime}<s^{\prime}
,

(2.8)

(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}})=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,k},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\quad s^{\prime}=s,k^{\prime}>k,\\
1/2&\text{if}\quad s^{\prime}=s+1,k^{\prime}\leq k,\\
1/2&\text{if}\quad s^{\prime}=s-1,k^{\prime}<k,\\
0&\textrm{if}\quad|s-s^{\prime}|\geq 2,\end{cases}

with the remaining cases determined by skew-symmetry.

(2)

For all
0\leq s\leq N
,
1\leq s^{\prime}\leq n
and
0\leq k^{\prime}<s^{\prime}
,

(2.9)

(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}})=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}-1&\text{if}\quad s^{\prime}=s,\\
1/2&\text{if}\quad|s^{\prime}-s|=1,\\
0&\text{if}\quad|s^{\prime}-s|\geq 2.\end{cases}

(3)

For all
0\leq s,t\leq N
,

(2.10)

(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{t})=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{t})=0.

(4)

For all
1\leq s,s^{\prime}\leq n
,
0\leq k<s
and
0\leq k^{\prime}<s^{\prime}
,

(2.11)

(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}})=\delta_{k+k^{\prime},s-1}(\delta_{k+s^{\prime},n}-\delta_{k+s^{\prime},N}).

(5)

For all
0\leq s\leq N
,
1\leq s^{\prime}\leq n
and
0\leq k^{\prime}<s^{\prime}
,

(2.12)

(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\quad s^{\prime}=N-s,\\
-1/2&\text{if}\quad|s^{\prime}-(N-s)|=1,\\
0&\text{if}\quad|s^{\prime}-(N-s)|\geq 2.\end{cases}

(6)

For all
1\leq s\leq n
,
0\leq k<s
and
0\leq s^{\prime}\leq N
,

(2.13)

(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime}})=0.

(7)

For all
1\leq s,t\leq n
,

(2.14)

(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{t})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\quad t=N-s,\\
-1/2&\text{if}\quad|t-(N-s)|=1,\\
0&\text{if}\quad|t-(N-s)|\geq 2.\end{cases}

Proof..

Immediate upon inspection.
∎

Before we continue, we also introduce the following notations:

Definition 2.4
.

We denote

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{N}=\{(a,b,c)\in C_{N}\mid 1\leq a\leq n\}\subseteq C_{N}^{\prime},

so
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{N}
is
C_{N}^{\prime}
with its lower right line removed. We put

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}\,\!^{\prime}_{N}=\{(a,b,c)\in C_{N}\mid 1\leq a\leq n,c>0\}\subseteq C_{N}^{\prime},

so
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}\,\!^{\prime}_{N}
is
C_{N}^{\prime}
with its lower right and upper line removed.

Similarly, we put

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}_{N}=\{(a,b,c)\in C_{N}\mid 1\leq b\leq n\}\subseteq C_{N}^{\prime},

so
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}_{N}
is
C_{N}^{\prime}
with its lower left line removed, and

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}\,\!^{\prime}_{N}=\{(a,b,c)\in C_{N}\mid 1\leq b\leq n,a>0\}\subseteq C_{N}^{\prime},

so
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}\,\!^{\prime}_{N}
is
C_{N}^{\prime}
with its lower left and lower right line removed.

Definition 2.5
.

We put

(2.15)

U_{N}^{-}\subseteq B^{-}_{N}\subseteq\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}

for the vector spaces spanned by respectively the basis vectors labelled by
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}\,\!^{\prime}_{N}
and
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{N}
.

Similarly, we denote

(2.16)

U_{N}^{+}\subseteq B_{N}^{+}\subseteq\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}

for the vector spaces spanned by respectively the basis vectors labelled by
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}\,\!^{\prime}_{N}
and
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}_{N}
.

We further put

(2.17)

T_{N}^{-}=\mathrm{span}\{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s}\mid 1\leq s\leq n\}\subseteq B_{N}^{-},\qquad T_{N}^{+}=\mathrm{span}\{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s}\mid 1\leq s\leq n\}\subseteq B_{N}^{+},

so that

(2.18)

B_{N}^{-}=T_{N}^{-}\oplus U_{N}^{-},\qquad B_{N}^{+}=T_{N}^{+}\oplus U_{N}^{+}.

Lemma 2.6
.

The subspaces
B_{N}^{-}
and
B_{N}^{+}
of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
are non-degenerately paired under the skew pairing.

Proof..

Let

\lambda=\sum_{(a,b,c)\in\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{N}}\lambda_{a,b,c}e_{a,b,c}

be a vector in
B_{N}^{-}
which is orthogonal to
B_{N}^{+}
. It then follows by induction on
a\colon 0\rightarrow n
and taking pairings with vectors of the form
e_{a,b,c}
for
(a,b,c)\in\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}_{N}
(with a second induction on
b
descending) that
\lambda
needs to be constant on northeast-oriented diagonals, i.e.

\lambda=\sum_{(a,b,c)\in\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{N}}\lambda_{a}e_{a,b,c}.

But then taking pairings with vectors of the form
e_{a,b,0}
for
(a,b,0)\in\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}_{N}
, we find that, with
\Lambda
the column vector with entries
\lambda_{a}
, we must have

B\Lambda=0,

with
B
the Cartan matrix for type
A_{n}
(see (
2.20
) below). Hence
\Lambda=0
.
∎

Corollary 2.7
.

Let

(2.19)

D_{N}:=\textrm{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}}_{N,\mathrm{red}}

be the reduction of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
as in (
1.1
). Then through the quotient map from
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
we obtain embeddings

B_{N}^{\pm}\hookrightarrow D_{N}.

We still use the notation
e_{a,b,c}
etc. to denote the images of these elements in
D_{N}
.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
B
be the Cartan matrix of type
A_{n}
,

(2.20)

B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{Z}),\qquad B_{rs}=2\delta_{r,s}-\delta_{|r-s|,1}.

We put

(2.21)

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s}:=\sum_{t=1}^{n}(B^{-1})_{st}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{t}\in T_{N}^{-},\qquad 1\leq s\leq n,

and similarly

(2.22)

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s}:=\sum_{t=1}^{n}(B^{-1})_{st}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{t}\in T_{N}^{+},\qquad 1\leq s\leq n.

By (
2.9
) and (
2.12
) we get for all
1\leq s,s^{\prime}\leq n
and
0\leq k^{\prime}<s^{\prime}
that

(2.23)

(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}})=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}})=-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{s,s^{\prime}},\qquad(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}})=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{s,N-s^{\prime}},

while from (
2.10
) and (
2.14
) it follows that

(2.24)

(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s})=0=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s}),\qquad(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s})=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{t},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s})=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{s,N-t}\qquad 1\leq s,t\leq n.

2.2.
Colored braid graphs

Let
B_{m}
be the braid group on
m
strands with standard generators
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m-1}
.

Definition 2.9
.

By a
positive
m
-strand braid word
we mean an ordered collection of elements of the alphabet
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m-1}
, i.e. we do not allow inverses of the generators as letters.

We call two braid words
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}

mutation-equivalent
if they can be related by some sequence of the following three types of moves, the first two of which are called
mutation moves
:

(1)

braid moves
, consisting of a substring replacement
\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\leftrightarrow\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}
, and

(2)

1
- or
{(m-1)}
-Demazure moves
, consisting of substring replacements
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{1}\leftrightarrow\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{m-1}\sigma_{m-1}\leftrightarrow\sigma_{m-1}
respectively, and

(3)

commutation moves
, consisting of substring replacements
\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\leftrightarrow\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}
when
|i-j|\geq 2
.

Each positive
m
-strand braid word
\mathbf{i}
determines a 1-d manifold with corners
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
via the following inductive construction: to a word consisting of a single generator
\sigma_{i}
corresponds the subset
\Gamma_{\sigma_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by the union of
m
horizontal closed intervals
h_{j}=[0,1]\times\{-j+1\}
for
1\leq j\leq m
, together with a single vertical closed interval
\{1/2\}\times[-i+1,-i]
connecting
h_{i}
to
h_{i+1}
. We then declare that for any concatenation
\mathbf{i}=\mathbf{i}^{\prime}*\mathbf{i}^{\prime\prime}
, the associated manifold
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
is obtained by gluing each point
(1,-j)
on the right boundary of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}
to the corresponding point
(0,-j)
on the left boundary of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime\prime}}
, and rescaling the horizontal intervals back to
[0,1]
. The resulting manifold with corners is naturally embedded into the disk
\mathbb{D}^{2}
, and two such manifolds with corners will be considered the same if they are ambient isotopic. Note that then
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}=\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}
if and only if
\mathbf{i}
and
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
are connected by a sequence of commutation moves.

We may enhance
\Gamma_{\bf i}
into a planar directed graph whose vertices are its corners and boundary points, and whose edges are the intervals connecting pairs of corners or pairs of a corner and a boundary point, oriented so that horizontal edges go to the right and vertical ones go down. We further color in blue the vertices which are sources of the vertical edges, and color in red those that are sinks of vertical edges.

i{-}1
i
i{+}1
*

i{-}1
i
i{+}1

.

\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\mapsto\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}
.

.
Local graph modification for Demazure move
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{1}\mapsto\sigma_{1}
.

.
Local graph modification for Demazure move
\sigma_{m-1}\sigma_{m-1}\mapsto\sigma_{m-1}
.

We call the planar graphs
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
that we obtain in this way
braid graphs
. We will simply refer to them as graphs in what follows. Then saying that two graphs
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}
are related by a mutation move means that we can find representatives
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}
such that
\mathbf{i}
and
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
are related by that mutation move. On the level of the graphs such moves can be represented locally:

(1)

If
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
are related by a braid move, the corresponding directed graphs
\Gamma_{\bf i},\Gamma_{\bf i^{\prime}}
are identical outside the neighborhood illustrated in Figure
5
.

(2)

The case of a Demazure move is similar, and shown in Figures
6
and
7
.

Clearly, mutation can be defined directly on the level of graphs, and we then say that
\Gamma
is mutable at a cell if
\Gamma
can be presented locally in the form
5
,
6
or
7
, with the mutable cell indicated by
*
.

Now let
V
be a skew-symmetric space.

Definition 2.10
.

If
\Gamma
is a braid graph, then a
V
-coloring

\{v_{f}\}
of
\Gamma
is a labelling of the faces
f
of
\Gamma
by linearly independent elements
v_{f}\in V
via a map

(2.25)

\ell\colon\mathrm{Faces}(\Gamma)\rightarrow V,

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

if two faces
f,g
are not separated by any edge connecting red and blue vertices, we have
(v_{f},v_{g})=0
;

(2)

if
f,g
are two faces separated by an edge connecting a red vertex with a blue vertex such that when crossing from
f
into
g
the red vertex lies to the right, we have
(v_{f},v_{g})=1
.

(3)

if
f,g
are two faces separated by an edge connecting a red (resp. blue) vertex to a boundary vertex such that when crossing from
f
into
g
the boundary vertex lies to the left, we have
(v_{f},v_{g})=1/2
(resp.
(v_{f},v_{g})=-1/2
).

Mutation of graphs can also be lifted to the level of
V
-colored braid graphs:

Definition 2.11
.

If
\bf i^{\prime}
is obtained from
\bf i
by a braid move
\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\mapsto\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}
and
\ell
is a
V
-labelling of
\Gamma_{\bf i}
, we define the
mutated
labelling
\mu_{v}(\ell)
of
\Gamma_{\bf i^{\prime}}
as in Figure
8
, with
\mu_{v}(\ell)
coinciding with
\ell
on all faces not appearing in that Figure.

Similarly, if
\bf i^{\prime}
is obtained from
\bf i
by a braid move
\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\mapsto\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}
, the mutated labelling is defined in Figure
9
.

Finally, if
\bf i^{\prime}
is obtained from
\bf i
by a Demazure move, we define the mutated labelling by Figure
10
or Figure
11
.

It is easily checked that
V
-colorings remain
V
-colorings after mutation. As in this case the labelling is by distinct elements of
V
, we may as well refer to a cell by its labelling, and say for example that ‘a face
v
is a mutable face’. Note however that performing two consecutive braid moves
\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\mapsto\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\rightarrow\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}
returns us to the original graph but
not
the original
V
-labelling.

Suppose that
(\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}},\ell)
is a
V
-labelled graph corresponding to positive braid word
\mathbf{i}
, and
\mathbf{i}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{i}
is some (not necessarily consecutive) substring of
\mathbf{i}
. If we write
\mathbf{i}\setminus\mathbf{i}_{0}
for the word obtained from
\mathbf{i}
by deleting the subword
\mathbf{i}_{0}
, the graph
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}\setminus\mathbf{i}_{0}}
is the one built from
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
by deleting the vertical edges in
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
associated to
\mathbf{i}_{0}
. So the faces of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}\setminus\mathbf{i}_{0}}
are unions of faces of
(\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}},\ell)
.

Definition 2.12
.

If
\mathbf{i}_{0}\subseteq\mathbf{i}
and
\ell
is a
V
-labelling on
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
, we define on
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}\setminus\mathbf{i}_{0}}
the labelling

(2.26)

\displaystyle\overline{l}(\overline{f})=\sum_{f\subseteq\overline{f}}l(f)

where the sum is taken over all faces
f
of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
contained in the face
\overline{f}
of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}\setminus\mathbf{i}_{0}}
.

By a direct verification one sees that if
\ell
is a
V
-coloring, also
\overline{\ell}
is a
V
-coloring.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
p
be a directed path in a
V
-labelled graph
(\Gamma_{\bf i},\ell)
starting from the left boundary vertex
a
and ending at the right boundary vertex
b
. We define its
weight

w_{p}
and
adjusted weight

u_{p}
to be the sums

(2.27)

w_{p}=\sum_{f\subseteq p}v_{f}\in V,\qquad u_{p}=w_{p}-w_{p_{b,b}}\in V,

where we write
f\subseteq p
to mean that the face
f
lies under
p
, and where
p_{b,b}
is the unique directed path in
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
connecting the boundary source vertex
b
with the boundary sink
b
.

Inspecting Figures
8
,
9
,
10
and
11
, we see that the weight of this path is invariant under braid and Demazure moves.

The basic colored graphs that we will make use of are the following:

Definition 2.14
.

We define
\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}}=(\Gamma_{\mathbf{w}},\ell)
to be the
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{N}
-colored graph arranged along the decomposition

(2.28)

\mathbf{w}_{0}=(\mathbf{w}_{n},\mathbf{w}_{n-1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{2},\mathbf{w}_{1}),\qquad\mathbf{w}_{k}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{k}),

with the vector
e_{a,b,c}
in the
c
’th cell on the
b
’th row.

Similarly, we define
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}=(\Gamma_{\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{0}},\ell)
to be the
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{N}
-colored graph arranged along the decomposition

(2.29)

\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{0}=\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{n}\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{n-1}\ldots\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{2}\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{1},\qquad\overline{\mathbf{w}}_{k}=(\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n-1},\ldots,\sigma_{N-k}),

’th cell on the

For later use, we include here the following lemma.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
\Gamma_{\mathbf{w}_{0}}^{\prime}
be the
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{N}
-colored subgraph of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{w}_{0}}
forgetting all utmost right vertical line on every row. Consider any inclusion
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{N}\subseteq V
and any extension of
\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}}^{\prime}
to a

-colored graph structure
(\Gamma_{\mathbf{w}_{0}\mathbf{w}_{0}},\widetilde{\ell})
.

(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{w}_{0}\mathbf{w}_{0}})
to a

-labeled graph
(\Gamma_{\mathbf{w}_{0}},\ell^{\prime})
such that
\ell_{\mid\Gamma_{\mathbf{w}_{0}}^{\prime}}=\ell_{\mid\Gamma_{\mathbf{w}_{0}}}
.

Proof..

We claim in fact that the required sequence of mutations can be found using only the mutations

(2.30)

\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\mapsto\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i},\qquad\sigma_{1}\sigma_{1}\mapsto\sigma_{1}.

To see this efficiently, we will encode our diagrams slightly differently, in the spirit of the
snake paths,
see
[
FG06
]
.

Consider the set
\mathscr{P}_{n}^{\prime}
of all possible families
P
of
n
non-intersecting paths in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, with the
i
-th path connecting
(0,i)
with
(i,0)
, and allowing within each unit square only path pieces of the form
\rightarrow,\downarrow,\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}
if the path passes through it. We can encode such a family of paths conveniently as a matrix
P\in M_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
, with the
(k,l)
-th entry equal to
i
if the
i
th path hits the vertex
(k,l)
(and being
0
if no path passes through it). For convenience, we pad this matrix on the left and bottom with the constant vectors
(1,2,\ldots,n)
and
(n,n-1,\ldots,1)
.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qquad,\qquad P=\left[\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}&1&0&0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}&2&1&0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}&2&1&1\\
\hline\cr&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\end{array}\right]\in\mathscr{P}_{3}

(to be consistent with the positioning of the graph, we will count the rows of the matrix from the bottom, starting at
0
, and the columns from the left, starting at
0
).

We then consider within
\mathscr{P}_{n}^{\prime}
the family
\mathscr{P}_{n}
whose matrices have the fixed borders as above, and such that any
2
-by-
2
-square inside (including ones with border entries) is of one of the following types:

(2.31)

\begin{pmatrix}i-1&i-1\\
i&i-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}i&i-1\\
i&i-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}i-1&i-1\\
i&i\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}i&i-1\\
i&i\end{pmatrix},\\
\begin{pmatrix}i-1&i-2\\
i&i-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},

with all entries between
0
and
n
.

To each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{n}
, we can associate a braid graph
\Gamma_{P}
, in the following way: connect a vertex to its right neighbour if they lie on different paths, and else to its northeast neighbour (which is then necessarily on a different path). Necessarily, if a vertex lies on the
i
-th path, it is connected to a vertex on the
i-1
-th path.

We then construct a map

(2.32)

P\mapsto\Gamma_{P},

associating to
P
the braid graph
\Gamma_{P}
whose
i
-th row has its cells labelled in order by the vertices of the
i
-th path of
P
, and where the right hand wall of the
k
th cell on the
i
th row has the
l
’th cell of the
i-1
-th row on top of it if and only if the vertices corresponding to
k
and
l
are connected in the way described in the previous paragraph.

For example, for (
2.31
) we get

(2.33)

\hbox to166.45pt{\vbox to101.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 83.22212pt\lower-50.52783pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

Let us now say that for
P\in\mathscr{P}_{n}
can be
mutated
at position
(k,l)
if the matrix
P^{\prime}
again lies in
\mathscr{P}_{n}
, where
P^{\prime}
equals
P
everywhere except at one entry
(k,l)
for
1\leq k,l\leq n
, where

P_{(k,l)}^{\prime}=P_{(k,l)}-1.

It is easily checked that such mutations are possible exactly at those vertices
(k,l)
where a path passes through, say the
i
th one, where the local configuration looks either as

(2.34)

It is then further immediately checked that these mutations correspond exactly to the braid moves in (
2.30
) under the correspondence (
2.32
).

Finally, let us now consider
V
a vector space, and let
\mathscr{P}_{n}(V)
be the set of couples
(P,\ell)
with
P\in\mathscr{P}_{n}
and
\ell\colon P\rightarrow V
(identifying
P
with its set of vertices where paths pass through - other vertices
(k,l)
can be given the zero vector). So, elements of
\mathscr{P}_{n}(V)
can be represented as matrices with values in
\mathbb{Z}\times V
, e.g.

\left[\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(1,w_{10})}&(1,w_{11})&0&0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(2,w_{20})}&(2,w_{21})&(1,w_{22})&0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(3,w_{30})}&(2,w_{31})&(1,w_{32})&(1,w_{33})\\
\hline\cr&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}3}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}2}&{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}1}\end{array}\right]\in\mathscr{P}_{3}(V)

(it will be unnecessary to weight the bottom row).

Now we also need to specify how such weighted elements in
\mathscr{P}_{n}(V)
mutate: namely, we mutate
P
into
P^{\prime}
as before, and if the local diagram of
P
is in one of the forms of (
2.34
), with weighting

(2.36)

with all other weights remaining the same.

If we now extend (
2.32
) to

(2.38)

\mathscr{P}_{n}(V)\rightarrow\{V\textrm{-colored graphs}\},

by sending
P\in\mathscr{P}_{n}(V)
the colored graph
(\Gamma_{P},\ell)
, where
\ell
assigns to the
k
-th cell on the
i
-th row the difference of the weights at the
k
-th and
k-1
-th position of the
i
-th path of
P
, then one again easily checks that the mutation (
2.37
) corresponds to the transformations in (
8
) and (
10
) (note that there is no need of compatibility with a skew-symmetric space structure on
V
if one simply wants to apply mutations of weighted graphs
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
).

We are now finally ready to deduce that
(\mathbf{w}_{0}\mathbf{w}_{0},\widetilde{\ell})
can be reduced to
(\mathbf{w}_{0},\ell^{\prime})
. Indeed, on the level of
\mathscr{P}_{n}(V)
this means that we want to find a path of mutations from
P_{n}(2)
to
P_{n}
, where

P_{n}(2)_{(k,l)}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}(N-k,\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{kl})&\textrm{if}&l<k,\\
(N-l,\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{f}_{l,n-k})&\textrm{if}&l\geq k.\end{array}\right.,\qquad P_{n,(k,l)}=(\max{0,N-k-l},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{k-l,l}),

where we take
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{i,j}=0
if ill-defined (note also again that we do not care about the bottom border row of the matrix), and where the vectors
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{f}_{s,k}\in V
are chosen so that they map to the labelling
\widetilde{\ell}
under (
2.38
).

Figure 16
.
The cases
P_{3}(2)
and
P_{3}

Now, first of all it is clear that we can find such a sequence of mutations on the level of the unweighted paths: this follows simply from the combinatorics of reduction in the positive braid monoid with the Demazure relation, but let us give a direct proof in terms of the above diagrams: indeed, if at some point we have arrived at a situation where the paths
n,n-1,\ldots,i+1
are all slanted (consisting only of

\textstyle\searrow

-arrows), then the
i
-th path
must
have a corner of the form
{}^{\rightarrow}\!\!\!\downarrow
. If this does not fit into the lower left bottom of the first diagram in (
2.34
), then the existence of a corner of this form
must
propagate to the next path
i-1
. If this keeps happening till we reach the line
1
, then it means that we have a corner of this form on the first line, and we can hence apply the mutation for the second diagram in (
2.34
). This means that the only diagram without mutations is the one of
P_{n}
.

To see that the reduction of
P_{n}(2)
to
P_{n}
is also compatible with the weights, we observe that the mutation from (
2.36
) to (
2.37
) satisfies the following property: if a weight vector
z
appears before mutation in one of the patterns

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-35.6562pt}{-17.03473pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}

{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
