then the same weight vector also appears somewhere after mutation in again one of these shapes. In particular, the original weight vectors
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-i,k}
, for
1<i<N,0\leq k<N-i
, always stay present somewhere on the
i
’th line, respecting their original ordering on that line. By cardinality, we see that they
must
be the weights appearing as in the diagram
P_{n}
.

∎

2.3.
Partition functions and standard generators via form sums

Let now
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, and let
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
be a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
V
on
\mathcal{H}
. Recall the notation (
1.8
).

Definition 2.16
.

Let
(\Gamma,\ell)
be a
V
-colored braid graph.

The
partition function
of
(\Gamma,\ell)
with boundary conditions
(a,b)
is defined as the form sum

(2.39)

\displaystyle Z_{\Gamma,\ell,a,b}:=\boxplus_{p:a\rightarrow b}\mathbf{E}(u_{p}),\quad 1\leq a<b\leq m,

where we sum over all paths
p\colon a\rightarrow b
in
\Gamma
.

Note that the above form sum makes sense (see Remark
1.13
). If
\mathbf{i}
is a positive braid word in
B_{m}
, we then further abbreviate

(2.40)

Z_{\mathbf{i},\ell,a,b}=Z_{\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}},\ell,a,b},

and by

(2.41)

Z_{\mathbf{i},\ell}=Z_{\mathbf{i},\ell,1,m}=\boxplus_{p:1\rightarrow m}\mathbf{E}(w_{p})

the partition function of paths starting at the top left boundary vertex 1 and ending at the bottom right boundary vertex
m
.

We use this construction to define some positive operators analagous to the PBW generators of the subalgebras
U_{q}(\mathfrak{n}^{\pm})\subseteq U_{q}(\mathfrak{sl}(N,\mathbb{C}))
corresponding to the nilpotent subalgebras of strictly upper/lower triangular matrices
\mathfrak{n}^{\pm}\subseteq\mathfrak{sl}(N,\mathbb{C})
.

For
1\leq r<s\leq N
, we define the
\nabla_{N}
-labelled
\mathbb{E}
-graph

\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}_{rs}}
to be the subgraph of the graph
\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}}
illustrated in Figure
12
bounded by horizontal lines
r
and
s
. We write
\mathbf{i}_{\mathbb{E}_{rs}}
for the positive braid word in
B_{r-s+1}
corresponding to the subgraph
\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}_{rs}}
, with the labelling inherited from
\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}}
. Similarly, we form the
\nabla_{N}
-labelled
\mathbb{F}
-graphs
as indicated in Figure
13
.

Definition 2.17
.

We define the
standard generators
to be the positive operators

(2.42)

\mathbf{E}_{rs}=Z_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbb{E}_{rs}},\ell}=Z_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}},\ell,r,s},\quad 1\leq r<s\leq N

and

(2.43)

\mathbf{F}_{rs}=Z_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbb{F}_{rs}},\ell}=Z_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}},\ell,r,s},\quad 1\leq r<s\leq N.

Example 2.18
.

For
N=3
, we have

\mathbf{E}_{1,2}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{2,0})\boxplus\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{2,1}),\quad\mathbf{E}_{2,3}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{1,0}),\quad\mathbf{E}_{1,3}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{2,0}+\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{1,0})

\mathbf{F}_{2,3}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{2,0})\boxplus\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{2,1}),\quad\mathbf{F}_{1,2}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{1,0}),\quad\mathbf{F}_{1,3}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{1,0}+\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{2,1}).

Recall now the quantum dilogarithm
\varphi=\varphi_{\hbar}
introduced in (
1.20
), and the notation for the associated functional calculus introduced in (
1.24
).

Lemma 2.19
.

If
v
is a mutable face of a
V
-colored graph
(\Gamma_{\bf i},\ell)
and
\bf i^{\prime}
the positive braid word obtained by applying the corresponding braid or Demazure move, we have

\varphi(v)Z_{\mathbf{i},\ell,a,b}\varphi(v)^{*}=Z_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mu_{v}(\ell),a,b}.

Proof..

We treat the case of a braid move
\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\mapsto\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}
; the other cases are handled the same way.

Consider the neighborhoods
N_{v},N^{\prime}_{v}
of the face
v
in
\Gamma_{\bf i}
and
\Gamma_{\bf i^{\prime}}
respectively illustrated in Figure
8
.
There is a natural weight-preserving bijection between the set of paths in
\Gamma_{\bf i}
which do not meet
N_{v}
with the set of paths in
\Gamma_{\bf i^{\prime}}
which do not meet
N_{v}^{\prime}
. Then for any path
p
in
\Gamma_{\bf i}
which does not enter
N_{v}
, we have
(u_{p},v)=0
, so that
\varphi(v)\mathbf{E}({u}_{p})\varphi(v)^{*}=\mathbf{E}({u}_{p})
. So the contributions to the partition function
Z_{\bf i,\ell,a,b}
from paths not meeting
N_{v}
is conjugated under the unitary
\varphi(v)
to the contribution to
Z_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mu_{v}(\ell),a,b}
from paths not meeting
N^{\prime}_{v}
.

The same argument applies to show that the contribution to
Z_{\mathbf{i},\ell,a,b}
from paths which enter and exit
N_{v}
along the same horizontal line
r\in{i-1,i,i+1}
is conjugated to the corresponding contribution to
Z_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mu_{v}(\ell),a,b}
, and similarly for the contributions from paths entering
N_{v}
along line
i-1
and exiting along line
i+1
. Indeed, inspecting Figure
8
it is clear that for all such paths we again have
(u_{p},v)=0
, and that the natural bijection between the set of such paths in
\Gamma_{\mathbf{i}}
and the corresponding set
\Gamma_{\bf i^{\prime}}
is weight preserving.

More generally, for
r,s\in\{i-1,i,i+1\}
let
\Pi(\Gamma_{\bf i},v,r,s)
be the set of paths in
\Gamma_{\bf i}
which enter
N_{v}
through horizontal line
r
and exit through horizontal line
s
, and define similarly
\Pi(\Gamma_{\bf i^{\prime}},v,r,s)
. Write

Z_{\Gamma_{\bf i}}(r,s)=\boxplus_{p\in\Pi(\Gamma_{\bf i},v,r,s)}\mathbf{E}(u_{p})

for the corresponding contribution to the partition function
Z_{\mathbf{i},\ell}
. We claim that

(2.44)

\displaystyle\varphi(v)Z_{\Gamma_{\bf i}}(i,i+1)\varphi(v)^{*}=Z_{\Gamma_{\bf i^{\prime}}}(i,i+1).

To see this, we group the paths in
\Pi(\Gamma,v,i,i+1)
into pairs
(p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime})
where the paths
p^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime\prime}
are identical outside of the neighborhood
N_{v}
, and
p^{\prime\prime}
reaches line
i+1
before
p^{\prime}
. Then we have
u_{p^{\prime}}=u_{p^{\prime\prime}}+v
,
(v,u_{p^{\prime}})=-1
.
So by Proposition
1.14
we have

\varphi(v)(\mathbf{E}({u}_{p^{\prime}})\boxplus\mathbf{E}({u}_{p^{\prime\prime}}))\varphi(v)^{*}=\mathbf{E}({u}_{p^{\prime\prime}}).

On the other hand, the pair of paths
p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime}
in
\Gamma_{\bf i}
corresponds to a single path
p
in
\Gamma_{\bf i^{\prime}}
whose adjusted weight is exactly
{u}_{p^{\prime\prime}}
, and so we deduce (
2.44
) holds.

In the same way each path
\pi
in
\Pi(\Gamma_{\bf i},v,i-1,i)
corresponds to a pair of paths
\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime}
in
\Gamma_{\bf i^{\prime}}
identical outside of
N_{v}
, and this time we have
(v,u_{\pi})=1
. So again by Proposition
1.14
we have

\varphi(v)Z_{\Gamma_{\bf i}}(i-1,i)\varphi(v)^{*}=Z_{\Gamma_{\bf i^{\prime}}}(i-1,i),

and this completes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Corollary 2.20

(Serre relations)
.

The pair of positive operators
(\mathbf{E}_{i-1,i+1},\mathbf{E}_{i-1,i})
is
\hbar/2
-commuting in the sense of (
1.27
), as is
(\mathbf{E}_{i,i+1},\mathbf{E}_{i-1,i+1})
.

Proof..

This follows since
\mathbf{E}_{i-1,i}
and
\mathbf{E}_{i-1,i+1}
can both be computed as appropriate partition functions for the graph
\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}_{i-1,i+1}}
, and the latter can be brought by a sequence of braid and Demazure moves to one corresponding to the braid word
\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
. By Lemma
2.19
, conjugating by the corresponding unitary brings
\mathbf{E}_{i-1,i},\mathbf{E}_{i-1,i+1}
to positive operators of the form
\mathbf{E}(u),\mathbf{E}(v)
with
(u,v)=1/2
, so the Corollary follows.
∎

Now suppose
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
correspond to
B_{m}
-braid words with Demazure product admitting prefix
\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{m-1}
, and are equipped with
V_{1}
- and
V_{2}
-colorings
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
for skew-symmetric spaces
V_{1},V_{2}
. Then we can consider a partition function

Z_{(\Gamma_{1},\ell_{1})\times(\Gamma_{2},\ell_{2})}=\boxplus_{(p_{1},p_{2})}\mathbf{E}({w}_{p_{1}}\oplus{w}_{p_{2}})

of pairs of paths
(p_{1},p_{2})
where
p_{1}
is a path in
\Gamma_{1}
entering along the top horizontal line (which we number 1) and exiting along the bottom horizontal line
m
, and
p_{2}
a path in
\Gamma_{2}
entering at 1 and exiting at
m
.
To each such path
p_{1}
we can associate a sequence of integers
(n_{1},\ldots n_{m-1})
where
n_{i}
is the number of faces lying between horizontal lines
i,i+1
and to the right of
p_{1}
. The lexicographic ordering on the set of all integer sequences then defines a total order on the set of all paths
p_{1}
in
\Gamma_{1}
from 1 to
m
. Equivalently,
q_{1}<p_{1}
in this total order if the first time that
q_{1},p_{1}
diverge, it is the path
q_{1}
which goes down. We equip the set of all paths from 1 to
m_{2}
in
\Gamma_{2}
with a total order in the same way, and put the lexical total order on the set of all pairs
(p_{1},p_{2})
where we order in the first factor, then break ties with the second.

Recall now the quantum exponential function
F=F_{\hbar}
from (
1.21
).

Lemma 2.21
.

Suppose
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
are graphs corresponding to
B_{m}
-braid words
\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{i}_{2}
, equipped with
V_{1}
- and
V_{2}
-colorings for skew-symmetric spaces
V_{1},V_{2}
. Then if
\mathbf{i}_{1}
and
\mathbf{i}_{2}
can be brought by some sequence of braid and Demazure moves to words of the form
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{m-1}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
does not contain
\sigma_{m-1}
, we have

(2.45)

\displaystyle\overline{F}(Z_{(\Gamma_{1},\ell_{1})\times(\Gamma_{2},\ell_{2})})=\overset{\longleftarrow}{\prod_{(p_{1},p_{2})}}\varphi(\mathrm{w}_{p_{1}}\oplus\mathrm{w}_{p_{2}}),

where the product is taken over all pairs of paths
(p_{1},p_{2})
, ordered with respect to the lexical total order described above so that the smallest pair is the right-most factor in the product.

Proof..

We argue by induction using the following observation: if
v
is a mutable face, formula (
2.45
) holds for
(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})
if and only if it holds for
(\mu_{v}(\Gamma_{1}),\Gamma_{2})
. Let us show this in the case that
\mu_{v}
corresponds to the braid move
\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\mapsto\sigma_{i+1}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}
from Figure
9
. By Lemma
2.19
, we have

(2.46)

\displaystyle\overline{F}(Z_{\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}})=\varphi(v)^{*}\overline{F}(Z_{\mu_{v}\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}})\varphi(v).

Now observe that the well-ordered list of paths in
\Gamma_{1}
can be obtained from the corresponding list of paths in
\mu_{v}\Gamma_{1}
by the following three-step procedure: first, for each path
\pi
in
\mu_{v}\Gamma_{1}
entering
\mu_{v}(N)\subseteq\mu_{v}\Gamma_{1}
along line
i-1
and exiting along line
i
, replace
\pi
by a consecutive pair of paths
(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})
in
\Gamma_{1}
which are identical to
\pi
outside of
N
, such that the path
\pi^{\prime}
reaches line
i
in
N\subseteq\Gamma_{1}
before
\pi^{\prime\prime}
. Note that
\pi^{\prime\prime}
is indeed the successor to
\pi^{\prime}
in the lexical ordering for
\Gamma_{1}
. The second step consists of identifying in the resulting list each pair
(p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime})
of paths in
\mu_{v}\Gamma_{1}
which enter
\mu_{v}(N)
along line
i
and exit along line
i+1
, and are identical outside of
\mu_{v}(N)
, so that
p^{\prime\prime}
is the successor to
p^{\prime}
in the
\mu_{v}\Gamma_{1}
lexical order. We then replace the consecutive pair
(p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime})
by the single path
p
in
\Gamma_{1}
identical to
p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime}
outside of
N
and which enters
\mu_{v}(N)
through line
i
and exits through line
i+1
. Finally, all remaining items in the list which are still paths in
\mu_{v}\Gamma_{1}
are paths which do not intersect
N
, and we replace each of these by the corresponding identical path in
\Gamma_{1}
. From this description, it follows immediately from formula (
2.46
) and the Kashaev pentagon identity that if (
2.45
) holds for
\mu_{v}\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
then it also holds for
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
. The same argument applies in the case that
\mu_{v}
corresponds to the reverse braid move or a Demazure move.

Now let us perform such a sequence of moves to bring
\mathbf{i}_{1}
to a word of the form
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{m-1}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
where
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
does not contain
\sigma_{m-1}
. Then in the resulting graph
\Gamma_{1}^{\bullet}
there is a unique path starting from the boundary vertex on line
1
ending at the boundary vertex on line
m
. For such a graph
\Gamma_{1}^{\bullet}
, the same argument used above shows that for any mutable face
v
in
\Gamma_{2}
, (
2.45
) holds for
\Gamma_{1}^{\bullet},\Gamma_{2}
if and only if it holds for
\Gamma_{1}^{\bullet},\mu_{v}\Gamma_{2}
.
So if we perform a sequence of braid and Demazure moves bringing
\Gamma_{2}
to a graph
\Gamma_{2}^{\bullet}
of the same form, there is a single pair of paths in
\Gamma_{1}^{\bullet},\Gamma^{\bullet}_{2}
and (
2.45
) becomes a tautology. This completes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Remark 2.22
.

The same proof shows
\overline{F}(Z_{(\Gamma_{1},\ell_{1})\times(\Gamma_{2},\ell_{2})})
can also be expanded as a differently ordered product of the same factors where we first order using
\Gamma_{2}
, then break ties using
\Gamma_{1}
.

Since form sums distribute over tensor product,
\mathbf{E}_{rs}\otimes\mathbf{F}_{rs}
can be computed as a partition function
Z_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}_{rs}}\times\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{rs}}}
of pairs of paths
(p_{E},p_{F})
where
p_{E}
is a path in
\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}_{rs}}
entering along the top horizontal line (which we number 1) and exiting along the bottom horizontal line
s-r+1
, and
p_{F}
a path in
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{rs}}
entering at 1 and exiting at
s-r+1
.

2.4.
\mathcal{R}
-matrix and the braided Fock–Goncharov flip

Consider the unitary

(2.47)

\mathcal{R}=\prod_{a=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\prod_{b=a+1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n+1\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b}).

For
1\leq a<b\leq N
let
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}}}
be the graph obtained from
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}_{a,b}}}
by deleting all vertical edges connecting the top two horizontal lines in
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}_{a,b}}}
except for the left-most such edge, and write
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{>1}_{a,b}}}
for the graph obtained from

\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}_{a,b}}}
by deleting only the left-most such edge. By the construction (
2.26
), the graphs
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}}}
and
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{>1}_{a,b}}}
inherit
V
-labellings from that of
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}_{a,b}}}
, and we set

\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}=Z({\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}}}}),\qquad\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a,b}=Z({\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{>1}_{a,b}}}}),

so that

Z({\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}_{a,b}}}})=Z({\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}}}})\boxplus Z({\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{>1}_{a,b}}}}).

Equivalently, we can think of
\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}
and
\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a,b}
as ‘conditional partition functions’ in the original graph
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}_{a,b}}}
given by restricting the sum (
2.39
) to only paths travsersing (respectively not traversing) the first vertical arrow between horizontal lines
a,a+1
.

Note that when
a=1
there is a single vertical edge in the top row of
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}_{a,b}}
so that for any
b
we have

Z({\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{>1}_{1,b}}}})=0.

Similarly for
1\leq a<b\leq N
and
1\leq j\leq N-a
, let
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}^{[j]}_{a,b}}}
be the graph obtained from
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}_{a,b}}}
by deleting all vertical edges connecting the top two horizontal lines in
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}_{a,b}}}
except for the
j
-th such edge encountered starting from the left. By the construction (
2.26
),
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}^{[j]}_{a,b}}}
inherits a
V
-labelling from that of
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}_{a,b}}}
, and we set

\mathbf{E}^{[j]}_{a,b}=Z({\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}^{[j]}_{a,b}}}}).

Since every path summed over in
Z({\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}_{a,b}}}})
traverses exactly one of the vertical edges mentioned above we have

\mathbf{E}_{a,b}=Z(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}_{a,b}}})=\mathop{\vphantom{\bigoplus}\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{0}{}{$\boxplus$}}}}}\displaylimits_{j=1}^{N-a}Z(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}^{[j]}_{a,b}}}).

In other words, the partition function
Z(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}^{[j]}_{a,b}}})
is the contribution to
Z(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}_{a,b}}})
from paths taking the
j
-th vertical edge between the top pair of vertical lines.

3
2
1
e_{310}+e_{211}
e_{112}+e_{013}
e_{220}
e_{121}
e_{022}

Figure 17
.
The labelled graph
\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}^{[2]}_{1,3}}}
for
N=4
.

We illustrate the setup for
N=4,(a,b)=(1,3),j=2
in Figure
17
. Note that for
j_{1}<j_{2}
, the paths in the sum defining
Z(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}_{a,b}}})
which contribute to
Z(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}^{[j_{1}]}_{a,b}}})
are less than those contributing to
Z(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{E}^{[j_{2}]}_{a,b}}})
in the lexical order from Lemma
2.21
. Similarly, the paths contributing to
Z(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}}})
are less than those contributing to
Z(\Gamma_{{\mathbb{F}^{>1}_{a,b}}})
. It follows from Lemma
2.21
that we have a factorization

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b})

\displaystyle=\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b})~\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b}),

so that

(2.48)

\mathcal{R}=\prod_{a=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\prod_{b=a+1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n+1\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b})~\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b}).

Lemma 2.23
.

We have the following collection of
\hbar/2
-commuting pairs of positive operators:

(2.49)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{F}_{a,b}^{[1]},\mathbf{F}_{a,c}^{>1}),\quad(\mathbf{E}_{a,c},\mathbf{E}_{a,b}),\quad a<b<c,

(2.50)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{F}_{a,b}^{[1]},\mathbf{F}_{a,a+1}^{[1]}),\quad(\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}^{[j]},\mathbf{E}_{a,b}^{[k]}),\quad b>a+1,\quad j<k.

(2.51)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{F}_{a,a+1}^{[1]},\mathbf{F}_{a+1,b}^{>1}),\quad(\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}^{[j+1]},\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b}^{>j}),\quad(\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b}^{[j]},\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}^{[k]}),\quad b>a+1,\quad j<k.

The following pairs of positive operators strongly commute:

(2.52)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{F}_{a,c}^{[1]},\mathbf{F}_{a+1,b}^{>1}),\quad(\mathbf{E}_{a,c},\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b}),\quad a<b<c.

Proof..

We treat case of the pair
(\mathbf{F}_{a,b}^{[1]},\mathbf{F}_{a,c}^{>1})
, with the others being handled using the same technique. The idea is to use Lemma
2.19
to construct a unitary which will conjugate the operators
(\mathbf{F}_{a,b}^{[1]},\mathbf{F}_{a,c}^{>1})
to a standard
\hbar/2
commuting pair. Consider the piece of the graph
\Gamma_{\mathbb{F}}
lying between horizontal lines
a
and
c
. The corresponding braid word takes the form
\beta=\sigma_{c-a}\cdots\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\beta^{\prime}
, and by a sequence of braid and Demazure moves we can transform
\beta^{\prime}
to the word
\beta^{\prime\prime}=(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\cdots\sigma_{c-a})\cdots\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}
. The conjugate of
\mathbf{F}_{a,c}^{>1}
under the associated unitary is computed as a sum over a single path in the labelled graph associated to
\tilde{\beta}=\sigma_{c-a}\cdots\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\beta^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover, observe that deleting all but the first two occurrences of
\sigma_{1}
in
\tilde{\beta}
to produce a word
\widehat{\beta}
has no effect on the conditional partition functions computing the unitary conjugates of
(\mathbf{F}_{a,b}^{[1]},\mathbf{F}_{a,c}^{>1})
. But by a further sequence of braid moves we can bring
\widehat{\beta}
to the word
\sigma_{c-a}\cdots\sigma_{2}\sigma_{1}\sigma_{1}\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}
where
\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}=(\sigma_{c-a}\cdots\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2})\cdots\sigma_{3}\sigma_{2}\sigma_{3}
. In this latter graph the conditional partition functions computing the unitary conjugates of both
(\mathbf{F}_{a,b}^{[1]},\mathbf{F}_{a,c}^{>1})
both consist of contributions from a single path, and the weights of these paths
\hbar/2
-commute as desired.
∎

Corollary 2.24
.

For
b>a+1
, the positive operators
(\mathbf{F}_{a+1,b}^{>1}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b}^{>j},\mathbf{F}_{a,a+1}^{[1]}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}^{[j+1]})
form an
\hbar
-commuting pair, and we have a pentagon identity

\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j+1]}_{a,a+1})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j+1]}_{a,b})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{>j}_{a+1,b})=\\
=\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{>j}_{a+1,b})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j+1]}_{a,a+1}).$$

From (
2.49
) and (
2.52
) we see that the following pairs of positive operators strongly commute:

[\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a,c}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,c},\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b}]=0=[\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b},\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,c}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,c}],\quad a<b<c.

This observation allows us to re-order the factors in (
2.48
) as

(2.53)

\mathcal{R}=\prod_{a=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\left\{\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1})\prod_{b=a+2}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n+1\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b})~\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b})\right\}.

Lemma 2.25
.

For
b>a+1
we have

(2.54)

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b})~\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b})

(2.55)

\displaystyle=\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a;a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}).

Proof..

By Lemma
2.21
, we can split

(2.56)

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a;a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1})=\prod_{j=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}N-a\\[-2.0pt]
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j]}_{a,a+1})

and

(2.57)

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b})=\prod_{j=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}N-b+1\\[-2.0pt]
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j]}_{a,b}).

Using (
2.50
) to move quantum exponential factors from (
2.56
) to the right over those in (
2.57
) with which they commute, we rewrite the left-hand side of (
2.54
) as

(2.58)

\prod_{j=N-b+2}^{\begin{subarray}{c}N-a\\[-2.0pt]
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j]}_{a,a+1})\!\!\!\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}N-b+1\\[-2.0pt]
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\left\{\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j]}_{a,a+1})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j]}_{a,b})\right\}\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b}).

Now we use Lemma
2.24
to make the replacement

\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[1]}_{a,a+1})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[1]}_{a,b})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b})\\
=\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}),

and then use Lemma
2.21
to split

\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b})=\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{>1}_{a+1,b})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[1]}_{a+1,b})

At this point, we can apply Lemma
2.24
again to replace

\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[2]}_{a,a+1})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[2]}_{a,b})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{>1}_{a+1,b})\\
=\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{>1}_{a+1,b})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[2]}_{a,a+1}),

and then split

\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{>1}_{a+1,b})=\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{>2}_{a+1,b})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[2]}_{a+1,b}).

Continuing this way, we rewrite the left-hand-side of (
2.54
) as

\prod_{j=N-b+2}^{\begin{subarray}{c}N-a\\[-2.0pt]
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j]}_{a,a+1})\cdot\prod\limits_{j=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}N-b+1\\[-2.0pt]
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\left\{\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j]}_{a+1,b})\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{[1]}_{a,a+1}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j]}_{a,a+1})\right\}.

By (
2.51
), we can commute the factors
\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a+1,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}^{[j]}_{a+1,b})
all the way to the left, and regrouping the quantum exponentials we arrive at the right-hand-side of (
2.54
).
∎

With this lemma it becomes easy to prove:

Proposition 2.26
.

There is an identity of unitaries

(2.59)

\mathcal{R}=\prod_{a=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\prod_{b=a+1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n+1\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b})=\prod_{k=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\prod_{a=k}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}\left(\mathbf{F}_{a;a+1}^{[k]}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}\right).

Proof..

For
n=1
the two factorizations are just identical, while the statement of the Proposition for rank
n-1
implies the following equality in the rank
n
setting:

\prod_{a=2}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\prod_{b=a+1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n+1\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}\left(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b}\right)=\prod_{k=2}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\prod_{a=k}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}\left(\mathbf{F}_{a;a+1}^{[k]}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}\right).

So the proposition will follow by induction provided we can show that

(2.60)

\mathcal{R}=\left\{\prod_{a=2}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\prod_{b=a+1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n+1\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}\left(\mathbf{F}^{>1}_{a,b}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,b}\right)\right\}\cdot\prod_{a=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}\left(\mathbf{F}_{a;a+1}^{[1]}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}\right).

To this end, by iterative application of Lemma
2.25
to formula (
2.53
) we have

(2.61)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\prod_{a=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\left\{\prod_{b=a+1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}\left(\mathbf{F}_{a+1,b+1}^{>1}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a+1,b+1}\right)\right\}\overline{F}_{\hbar}\left(\mathbf{F}_{a,a+1}^{[1]}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}\right).

Since
\mathbf{F}_{a,a+1}^{[1]}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}
strongly commutes with
\mathbf{F}_{c,d}^{>1}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{c,d}
for any
d>c>a+1
, we
can freely commute the factors
F_{\hbar}\left(\mathbf{F}_{a,a+1}^{[1]}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{a,a+1}\right)
in (
2.61
) rightwards over all such factors
F_{\hbar}\left(\mathbf{F}_{c,d}^{>1}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{c,d}\right)
to arrive at (
2.60
), and this finishes the proof.
∎

The previous expression for the unitary
\mathcal{R}
is reminiscent of the familiar one in the algebraic setup (see e.g.
[
KS97
, Section 8.3.2]
). We will now derive an alternative form of this unitary, identifying it with the quantum mutation operator associated to the Fock–Goncharov flip from cluster theory.

Definition 2.27
.

For any
1\leqslant i\leqslant r\leqslant b\leqslant n
, we define on
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
the unitaries

(2.62)

B^{b,r,i}=\varphi\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b}\right).

Lemma 2.28
.

One has

(2.63)

\left[B^{b,r,i},B^{b^{\prime},r,i^{\prime}}\right]=0,\qquad\forall 1\leqslant i,i^{\prime}\leqslant r\leqslant b,b^{\prime}\leqslant n.

Proof..

Writing

s=b-i+1,\qquad s^{\prime}=b^{\prime}-i^{\prime}+1,

it follows immediately from (
2.8
) that

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b^{\prime}-i^{\prime}+1,b^{\prime}-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b^{\prime}+i^{\prime},n-b^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,b-r},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},b^{\prime}-r})+(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-s+1,n-b},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-s^{\prime}+1,n-b^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,b-r},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},b^{\prime}-r})+(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{n-s+1,n-b},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{n-s^{\prime}+1,n-b^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=0.

The lemma now follows from (
1.25
).
∎

Definition 2.29
.

The
braided Fock–Goncharov flip

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\hbar}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})
is defined as the unitary

(2.64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B^{b,r,i}

(2.65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant s\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\underset{0\leqslant(n-r)-k\leqslant n-s}{\prod_{0\leqslant k\leqslant s-1}^{\longrightarrow}}\varphi\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,(n-r)-k}\right)

(2.66)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant s\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\underset{0\leqslant(n-r)-k\leqslant n-s}{\prod_{0\leqslant k\leqslant s-1}^{\longrightarrow}}\varphi\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,(n-r)-k}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\right).

Note that by Lemma
2.28
, only the first product over
r
in the above expressions must be ordered.

Remark 2.30
.

We note that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is nothing but a unitary implementing the quantum cluster transformation between cluster charts for the moduli space of framed
SL_{N}
local systems corresponding to ideal triangulations related by a single diagonal flip, see
[
FG06
, Section 10.3]
.

Lemma 2.31
.

The braided Fock–Goncharov flip
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
coincides with the unitary
\mathcal{R}
from (
2.47
).

Proof..

We apply Lemma
2.21
to decompose the factorization of
\mathcal{R}
from the right-hand-side in Proposition
2.26
into a product of quantum dilogarithms of the monomials (
2.62
), and by Lemma
2.28
the factors can be reordered to obtain the expression for
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
in (
2.64
).
∎

Remark 2.32
.

We stress that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is defined with respect to a fixed unitary
\hbar
-representation
\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{H},\pi)
of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
. If we want to emphasize this, we will speak of a
braided Fock–Goncharov flip on
\mathcal{H}
or
with respect to
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
.

We now introduce notations

\displaystyle B_{[12]}^{b,r,i}=B_{12}^{b,r,i}=\varphi\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b}\oplus 0\right),

\displaystyle B_{[23]}^{b,r,i}=B_{23}^{b,r,i}=\varphi\left(0\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b}\right),

and

B_{[13]}^{b,r,i}=\varphi\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-i+1}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b}\right),

and we let
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[12]},\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[23]}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[13]}
be their corresponding products in the order as in (
2.64
).

Theorem 2.33
.

The following pentagon relation holds:

(2.67)

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[23]}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[12]}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[12]}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[13]}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[23]}.

This result is well-known, see
[
DGG16
]
, but for reader’s convenience we present a direct algebraic proof in Appendix
G
.

Remark 2.34
.

One can show that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is a
braided multiplicative unitary
as in
[
MRW17
, Definition 3.2]
. As we will not follow this approach further in this paper, we refrain from spelling out the details.

2.5.
Fock–Goncharov flip

We resume the setting of Section
2.4
. We fix
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
and a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.

By (
2.10
) and (
2.24
), the following definition is meaningful.

Definition 2.35
.

We define

(2.68)

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{\hbar}=G_{\hbar}\left(2\sum_{t=1}^{n}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-t}\right)=G_{\hbar}\left(2\sum_{t=1}^{n}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{t}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{N-t}\right).

Theorem 2.36
.

The unitary

(2.69)

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}=\mathcal{K}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}

is a multiplicative unitary, i.e.

(2.70)

\mathcal{F}_{23}\mathcal{F}_{12}=\mathcal{F}_{12}\mathcal{F}_{13}\mathcal{F}_{23}.

We refer to
\mathcal{F}
as the
Fock–Goncharov flip
.

Proof..

By (
1.17
) and (
2.13
), we immediately get that the left hand side of (
2.70
) equals

\mathcal{F}_{23}\mathcal{F}_{12}=\mathcal{K}_{23}\mathcal{K}_{12}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{23}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{12}.

On the other hand, for the factors on the right hand side of (
2.70
) we find via (
2.23
) that

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\varphi(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,(n-r)-k})_{12}\mathcal{K}_{13}\mathcal{K}_{23}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}_{13}\varphi(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,(n-r)-k}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})\mathcal{K}_{23}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}_{13}\mathcal{K}_{[23]}\varphi(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,(n-r)-k})_{12},

so
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{12}
commutes with
\mathcal{K}_{13}\mathcal{K}_{23}
, while from the last identity in (
2.68
) and (
2.23
) it follows that

\varphi(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,(n-r)-k})_{[13]}\mathcal{K}_{23}=\mathcal{K}_{23}\varphi(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,(n-r)-k}).

Using the notation introduced above Theorem
2.33
, this gives that the right hand side of (
2.70
) is

\mathcal{F}_{12}\mathcal{F}_{13}\mathcal{F}_{23}=\mathcal{K}_{12}\mathcal{K}_{13}\mathcal{K}_{23}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[12]}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[13]}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[23]},

so since
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[23]}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{23}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[12]}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{12}
, the theorem follows from Theorem
2.33
once we show that

(2.71)

\mathcal{K}_{23}\mathcal{K}_{12}=\mathcal{K}_{12}\mathcal{K}_{13}\mathcal{K}_{23}.

But using (
2.23
) and (
1.16
) we have

\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s})\otimes 1)\mathcal{K}^{*}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s}),

from which (
2.71
) directly follows.
∎

Remark 2.37
.

Note that if for
1\leq i\leq r\leq b\leq n
we write

(2.72)

C^{b,r,i}=\varphi\left(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,r-i}\oplus-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,i-1}\right),

then by (
1.16
), (
2.7
) and (
2.9
) we get the alternative formula

(2.73)

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{K},

where

(2.74)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}C^{b,r,i}

(2.75)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leq s\leq n}^{\longrightarrow}\underset{0\leqslant r-k-1\leqslant n-s}{\prod_{0\leqslant k\leqslant s-1}^{\longrightarrow}}\varphi\left(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\oplus-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,r-k-1}\right).

3.
Modularity of the Fock–Goncharov flip.

Our goal in this section is to show that for suitable unitary
\hbar
-representations of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
, the Fock–Goncharov flip (
2.69
) is
modular
, in the sense of
[
SW01
, Definition 2.1]
.

3.1.
Modularity

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathbb{W}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})
be a unitary. We call
\mathbb{W}

modular
if there exist strictly positive operators
\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
on
\mathcal{H}
such that

(3.1)

\mathbb{W}^{*}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\otimes\mathbf{Q})\mathbb{W}=(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\otimes\mathbf{Q})

and such that there exists a (necessarily unique) unitary operator
\mathbb{W}_{c}\in\mathcal{B}(\overline{\mathcal{H}}\otimes\mathcal{H})

such that

(3.2)

\langle\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime},\mathbb{W}(\eta\otimes\mathbf{Q}\xi^{\prime})\rangle=\langle\overline{\eta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}\eta^{\prime},\mathbb{W}_{c}(\overline{\xi}\otimes\xi^{\prime})\rangle,\qquad\forall\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{Q}),\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}.

We then say that
\mathbb{W}
is
modular with respect to the couple
(\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}})
, and we call
(\mathbb{W},\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathbb{W}_{c})
a
modular datum
.

Remark 3.2
.

(1)

Under the assumption of (
3.1
), it is easily seen that (
3.2
) holds if and only if

(3.3)

\langle\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime},\mathbb{W}(\eta\otimes\xi^{\prime})\rangle=\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}\otimes\eta^{\prime},\mathbb{W}_{c}(\overline{\xi}\otimes\xi^{\prime})\rangle,\qquad\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}),\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}.

(2)

The above definition is presented in a slightly different form than
[
SW01
, Definition 2.1]
, but the equality of the two definitions is shown in
[
SW01
, Proposition 2.2]
.
3
3

3

We use in fact a slightly stronger version of that property when invoking the equivalence of conditions below, but this is immediately observed to hold from the proof of
[
SW01
, Proposition 2.2]
. We also take the opportunity to switch the inverse of the unbounded operator to the other side, which is as well simply a cosmetic operation. Finally, we formulate the condition for arbitrary unitaries on
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
, instead of just multiplicative unitaries.

Remark 3.3
.

One should think of
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
as an auxiliary object implementing ‘the positive root of the antipode squared’ on a quantum group (and
\mathbf{Q}
as the corresponding object for the dual quantum group). Indeed, assume for the moment that one has a Hopf
*
-algebra
(M,\Delta)
represented faithfully (in a
*
-preserving way) by bounded operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
:

\pi\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}).

Then one wants to make a contragredient representation on the conjugate-linear Hilbert space
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
by means of the antipode
S
:

(3.4)

\pi^{c}\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\overline{\mathcal{H}}),\qquad a\mapsto\overline{\pi(S(a)^{*})}.

The problem is that this representation will not be
*
-preserving in general, as the antipode is not
*
-preserving if
S^{2}\neq\mathrm{id}
:

S(a)^{*}=S^{-1}(a^{*}),\qquad a\in M.

One hence tries to find an operator
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
on
\mathcal{H}
restoring the
*
-compatibility:

(3.5)

\overline{\pi}\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\overline{\mathcal{H}}),\qquad a\mapsto\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\pi(S(a)^{*})\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}}

(ignoring questions of unboundedness/domains of definition).

Now given a ‘good’ multiplicative unitary
\mathbb{W}
(where ‘good’ will turn out to be exactly the modularity condition), one intuitively thinks of its first leg
M
, consisting of (a completion of) elements of the form
(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{W}
for
\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}
, as forming a ‘topological’ Hopf
*
-algebra with coproduct implemented by

(3.6)

a\mapsto\mathbb{W}^{*}(1\otimes a)\mathbb{W}.

(see Section
4
). In this setup, the antipode of
M
should then act on the first leg of
\mathbb{W}
as

(S\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathbb{W}=\mathbb{W}^{*},

which leads to (
3.4
) becoming

\pi^{c}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{W})=\overline{\pi((\mathrm{id}\otimes\overline{\omega})\mathbb{W})},\qquad\overline{\omega}(x)=\overline{\omega(x^{*})}.

Writing out the definition of
\overline{\pi}
in (
3.5
) when
\pi
is the identity representation and
\omega
a vector functional, we arrive at the equation (
3.3
), with

\overline{\pi}((\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{W})=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{W}_{c}^{*}.

Formally, the above manipulations then also lead to the conclusion that one should have

S^{2}(a)=\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{2}a\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-2},\qquad a\in M,

which explains the intuition of
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
implementing ‘the positive root of the antipode squared’.

Note that making all this intuition rigorous requires a lot of hard work. The key point is however that in the analytic setting of multiplicative unitaries (as opposed to the setting of Hopf
*
-algebras), equation (
3.3
) should rather be seen as the starting point, from which a theory resembling the Hopf
*
-algebraic one can then be derived.

The following theorem is known, see Appendix
C
. We will use the notation
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{P}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}d/dx
for the infinitesimal self-adjoint generators of the unitary groups on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
determined by

(3.7)

(e^{it\mathbf{X}}f)(x)=e^{itx}f(x),\qquad(e^{2\pi it\mathbf{P}}f)(x)=f(x+t),\qquad f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Theorem 3.4
.

Consider
\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
together with

\mathbf{F}=e^{\mathbf{X}},\qquad\mathbf{L}=e^{-4\pi^{2}\hbar\mathbf{P}},

so that
\mathbf{F}
and
\mathbf{L}
form an
\hbar
-commuting pair:

\mathbf{F}^{it}\mathbf{L}^{is}=e^{2\pi i\hbar st}\mathbf{L}^{is}\mathbf{F}^{it},\qquad s,t\in\mathbb{R}.

Put

\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{-1},\qquad\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{L}\star\mathbf{F}^{-1}.

Then, using the notation (
1.20
), the unitary

\mathbb{W}=\exp\left(\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}\ln(\mathbf{L})\otimes\ln(\mathbf{K})\right)\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}\otimes\mathbf{E})

is a modular multiplicative unitary with respect to

(3.8)

\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{K}^{-\frac{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}{2}},\qquad\hat{\mathbf{Q}}=\mathbf{L}^{\frac{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}{2}},

and
(\mathbb{W},\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathbb{W}_{c})
is a modular datum for

\mathbb{W}_{c}=\exp\left(\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}\ln(\overline{\mathbf{L}})\otimes\ln(\mathbf{K})\right)F_{\hbar}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}\otimes\mathbf{K}^{-1}\star\mathbf{E}).

We can rephrase this theorem as stating that in the case
n=1
, with associated diagram

C_{2}^{\prime}=\resizebox{}{}{\scalebox{0.8}[0.8]{{\hbox{{\hbox to148.84pt{\vbox to45.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 74.41664pt\lower-22.7639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}}},

the Fock–Goncharov unitary
\mathcal{F}
is modular if we use the specific unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{2}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
given by

\mathbf{E}(e_{101})=\mathbf{F},\qquad\mathbf{E}(e_{110})=\mathbf{E},\qquad\mathbf{E}(e_{011})=\mathbf{L}^{-1}.

Remark 3.5
.

The notation evokes a degenerate representation of the Heisenberg double of the Hopf
*
-algebra

(3.9)

U_{q}(\mathfrak{b}^{-})=\mathbb{C}\langle L,F\mid LF=q^{2}FL\rangle,\qquad F^{*}=F,L^{*}=L,\\
\Delta(L)=L\otimes L,\quad\Delta(F)=F\otimes L+1\otimes F,

with
q=e^{\pi i\hbar}
in this formalism. Indeed, if the dual of
U_{q}(\mathfrak{b}^{-})
is realized as

(3.10)

U_{q}(\mathfrak{b})=\mathbb{C}\langle K,E\mid KE=q^{2}EK\rangle,\qquad E^{*}=E,K^{*}=K,\\
\Delta(K)=K\otimes K,\quad\Delta(E)=E\otimes 1+K\otimes E,

then the (normalized) natural pairing between
U_{q}(\mathfrak{b})
and
U_{q}(\mathfrak{b}^{-})
leads to the Heisenberg commutation relations

KL=q^{2}LK,\quad KF=FK,\quad EL=q^{2}LE,\quad EF-FE=(q-q^{-1})L,

which is compatible with the relations between the operators in Theorem
3.4
.

Further note that the coproduct (
3.6
) is compatible with the coproduct in (
3.9
), through an easy application of the Kashaev pentagon equation. One then sees that the modular structure (
3.8
) amounts to the following modification of the contragredient of the identity representation
\pi
as in (
3.5
):

\overline{\pi}(\mathbf{F})=\overline{\mathbf{F}}\star\overline{\mathbf{L}}^{-1},\qquad\overline{\pi}(\mathbf{L})=\overline{\mathbf{L}}^{-1}.

To extend the modularity in Theorem
3.4
to the general setting, it is convenient to single out the following auxiliary notion, focusing on Condition (
3.3
).

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}
be Hilbert spaces. We call a unitary
U\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{H})

weakly modular
if there exists a strictly positive operator
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
on
\mathcal{G}
and a unitary operator
\widetilde{U}\in\mathcal{B}(\overline{\mathcal{G}}\otimes\mathcal{H})
such that

(3.11)

\langle\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime},U(\eta\otimes\xi^{\prime})\rangle=\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}\otimes\eta^{\prime},\widetilde{U}(\overline{\xi}\otimes\xi^{\prime})\rangle,\qquad\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}),\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}.

We then call
(U,\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\widetilde{U})
a
weakly modular datum
for the Hilbert space couple
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
. We refer to
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
as the
modularity operator
.

The following (easy) observation will be crucial to show modularity of the Fock–Goncharov flip
\mathcal{F}
in general rank.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K},\mathcal{H}
be Hilbert spaces, and let
(V,\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\widetilde{V})
and
(W,\hat{\mathbf{P}},\widetilde{W})
be weakly modular data with respect to the respective pairs of Hilbert spaces
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
and
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{H})
.

Then
(U,\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{P}},\widetilde{U})
is a weakly modular datum with respect to
(\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{K},\mathcal{H})
, where

(3.12)

U=V_{13}W_{23}\in\mathcal{B}((\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{K})\otimes\mathcal{H}),\qquad\widetilde{U}=\widetilde{V}_{13}\widetilde{W}_{23}\in\mathcal{B}((\overline{\mathcal{G}}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{K}})\otimes\mathcal{H}).

Proof..

For
\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}),\theta,\zeta\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{P}})
and
\eta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
, we compute

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi\otimes\hat{\mathbf{P}}\theta\otimes\eta^{\prime},V_{[13]}W_{[23]}(\eta\otimes\zeta\otimes\theta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime},V(\eta\otimes(\omega_{\hat{\mathbf{P}}\theta,\zeta}\otimes\mathrm{id})(W)\theta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}\otimes\eta^{\prime},\widetilde{V}(\overline{\xi}\otimes(\omega_{\hat{\mathbf{P}}\theta,\zeta}\otimes\mathrm{id})(W)\theta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{P}}\theta\otimes\eta^{\prime},\widetilde{V}_{[13]}W_{[23]}(\overline{\xi}\otimes\zeta\otimes\theta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\mathbf{P}}\theta\otimes(\omega_{\overline{\xi},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\widetilde{V}^{*})\eta^{\prime},W(\zeta\otimes\theta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{P}}\zeta}\otimes(\omega_{\overline{\xi},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\widetilde{V}^{*})\eta^{\prime},\widetilde{W}(\overline{\theta}\otimes\theta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{P}}\zeta}\otimes\eta^{\prime},\widetilde{V}_{[13]}\widetilde{W}_{[23]}(\overline{\xi}\otimes\overline{\theta}\otimes\theta^{\prime})\rangle.

In other words, with
U,\widetilde{U}
as in (
3.12
), we find

\langle(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi\otimes\hat{\mathbf{P}}\theta)\otimes\eta^{\prime},U((\eta\otimes\zeta)\otimes\theta^{\prime})\rangle=\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta\otimes\hat{\mathbf{P}}\zeta}\otimes\eta^{\prime},\widetilde{U}(\overline{\xi\otimes\theta}\otimes\theta^{\prime})\rangle.

Since the algebraic tensor product of
\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}})
and
\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{P}})
is a core for the tensor product
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{P}}
, we find that indeed
U
is weakly modular, and that
(U,\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{P}},\widetilde{U})
is a weakly modular datum with respect to
(\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{K},\mathcal{H})
.
∎

We also have the following trivial extension of Theorem
3.4
.

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{F})
and
(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{E})
be
any
two pairs of
\hbar
-commuting strictly positive operators on respective Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{G}
. Then

U=\exp\left(\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}\ln(\mathbf{L})\otimes\ln(\mathbf{K})\right)\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}\otimes\mathbf{E})

fits into a weakly modular datum
(U,\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\widetilde{U})
with

\hat{\mathbf{Q}}=\mathbf{L}^{\frac{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}{2}}

and

\widetilde{U}=\exp\left(\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}\ln(\overline{\mathbf{L}})\otimes\ln(\mathbf{K})\right)F_{\hbar}(\overline{\mathbf{F}}\otimes\mathbf{K}^{-1}\star\mathbf{E}).

Proof..

Let
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L}
be Hilbert spaces. Then clearly
(U,\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\widetilde{U})
is a weakly modular datum for
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
if and only if

(U_{[13]},\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\otimes 1,\widetilde{U}_{[13]})

is a weakly modular datum for
(\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{K},\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{L})
. Similarly, if
u\colon\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
and
v\colon\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}
are unitaries, then
(U,\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\widetilde{U})
is a weakly modular datum for
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
if and only if

((u\otimes v)U(u^{*}\otimes v^{*}),u\hat{\mathbf{Q}}u^{*},(\overline{u}\otimes v)\widetilde{U}(\overline{u}^{*}\otimes v^{*}))

is a weakly modular datum for
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L})
.

Hence the theorem follows immediately from Theorem
3.4
and the Stone-von Neumann theorem.
∎

3.2.
Modularity of Fock–Goncharov flips in general rank

We start by constructing a concrete unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
with respect to which we will show modularity of the Fock–Goncharov flip. We then later show that modularity holds with respect to
any

\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
.

Recall the notations introduced in Definition
2.4
and Definition
2.5
. We identify
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{N}
with the set

I=I_{0}\cup I_{1},\quad I_{0}=\{s=(s,s)\mid 1\leq s\leq n\},\quad I_{1}=\{(s,k)\mid 1\leq s\leq n,0\leq k<s\}

by means of the map

I\cong\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{N},\qquad(s,k)\mapsto(n-s+1,k,s-k).

We totally order
I
(and
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{N}
) by requiring that
I_{0}=[1,n]
has the normal order, that elements in
I_{0}
precede elements in
I_{1}
, and that
I_{1}
is totally ordered by

(3.13)

(s,k)<(s^{\prime},k^{\prime})\qquad\iff\qquad s-k<s^{\prime}-k^{\prime}\textrm{ or }(s-k=s^{\prime}-k^{\prime}\textrm{ and }s>s^{\prime}),

see Figure
18
for an example.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 6}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 5}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 8}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 7}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 9}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}

Figure 18
.
The ordering of
I
identified with
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{4}

Consider the symplectic skew-symmetric space
W=\mathbb{R}f\oplus\mathbb{R}\varpi
with

(3.14)

(f,\varpi)=1/2.

We then consider
I
copies
\{W_{i}\mid i\in I\}
of
W
, whose respective vectors we denote
f_{i},\varpi_{i}
. We further put

(3.15)

V_{N}=\oplus_{i\in I}W_{i},

keeping the same notation for the images of the
f_{i},\varpi_{i}
inside
V_{N}
. We can then by induction (on the reverse order) uniquely make an embedding of skew-symmetric spaces

(3.16)

B^{-}_{N}\subseteq V_{N}

by requiring the following rules with respect to the basis
\{f_{i},\varpi_{i}\mid i\in I\}
of
V_{N}
:

(1)

if
i\in I_{1}
and
j\in I
, then

•

for
j<i
, the
j
-th component of
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{i}
is zero,

•

the
i
-th component of
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{i}
is
f
,

•

for
j>i
, the
j
-th component of
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{i}
lies in
\mathbb{R}\varpi
, and

(2)

if
i\in I_{0}\cong[1,n]
and
j\in I
, then

•

for
j<i
, the
j
-th component of
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i}
is zero,

•

the
i
-th component of
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i}
is
\varpi
, and

•

For
j>i
the
j
-th component of
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i}
lies in
\mathbb{R}\varpi
, and is zero if
j\in I_{0}
.

By (
2.13
) and (
2.14
), this embedding extends to
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
by putting

(3.17)

\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s}=-f_{N-s},\qquad s\in[1,n].

Endow now
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with the unitary
\hbar
-representation of
W
determined by

\mathbf{E}(f)=\mathbf{F},\qquad\mathbf{E}(\varpi)=\mathbf{L}^{1/2},

using the same notation as in Theorem
3.4
. More generally, denote for
i\in I
by
\mathcal{H}_{i}
a copy of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, endowed with the corresponding unitary
\hbar
-representation of
W_{i}
, and put

(3.18)

\mathcal{H}=\otimes_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}_{i},

endowed with the product unitary
\hbar
-representation of
V_{N}=\oplus_{i\in I}W_{i}
. By restriction under the embedding (
3.16
), we can view this also as a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
or of
B^{-}_{N}
.

Denote further, for
1\leq s\leq n
and
0\leq k<s
,

(3.19)

\mathbb{U}^{(s,k)}=G\left(2\varpi_{(s,k)}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}\right)\prod_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\varphi\left(f_{(s,k)}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}\right)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{(s,k)}\otimes\mathcal{H}),

(3.20)

\mathbb{U}^{(s)}=G\left(2\varpi_{s}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}\right)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{s}\otimes\mathcal{H}).

We have the following alternative expression for
\mathbb{U}^{(s,k)}
. Recall that
\boxplus
denotes the form sum.

Lemma 3.9
.

We have

(3.21)

\mathbb{U}^{(s,k)}=G\left(2\varpi_{(s,k)}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}\right)\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{(s,k)})\otimes\left(\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r})\right)\right).

Proof..

This follows immediately from Theorem
1.16
.
∎

The following is the main observation that will allow us to establish modularity of the Fock–Goncharov flip
\mathcal{F}
as in (
2.69
).

Theorem 3.10
.

Inside
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})=\mathcal{B}\left(\left(\otimes_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}_{i}\right)\otimes\mathcal{H}\right)
, we have an equality of unitaries

(3.22)

\mathcal{F}=\overset{\longrightarrow}{\prod}_{i\in I}\mathbb{U}^{(i)}_{i,\bullet}.

Here we use leg numbering notation and keep
\bullet
as the index for the
\mathcal{H}
-factor at the end.

Proof..

For the
\mathbb{U}^{(s,k)}_{(s,k),\bullet}
and
\mathbb{U}^{(s)}_{s,\bullet}
, we can still use the formulas (
3.19
) and (
3.20
), now with the first leg viewed as acting on the
i
-th leg of
\otimes_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}_{i}
(and as the unit on all other legs).

By shifting all the Gaussians through the quantum dilogarithms, using (
1.17
), we find that the right hand side of (
3.22
) becomes

\overset{\rightarrow}{\prod}_{i\in I}\mathbb{U}^{(i)}_{i,\bullet}=\mathcal{L}\overset{\rightarrow}{\prod}_{i\in I_{1}}\mathbb{V}^{(i)}

where

(3.23)

\mathcal{L}=G\left(2\sum_{1\leq s\leq n}\left(\sum_{0\leq k\leq s}\varpi_{(s,k)}\right)\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}\right)

and

(3.24)

\mathbb{V}^{(i)}=\prod_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\varphi\left(f_{(s,k)}+2\sum_{(s^{\prime},k^{\prime})>(s,k)}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s^{\prime}})\varpi_{(s^{\prime},k^{\prime})}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}\right).

We however easily check that

(3.25)

f_{(s,k)}+2\sum_{(s^{\prime},k^{\prime})>(s,k)}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s^{\prime}})\varpi_{(s^{\prime},k^{\prime})}=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}.

(3.26)

\sum_{0\leq k\leq s}\varpi_{(s,k)}=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s}.

Indeed, for (
3.25
), we just need to verify that

-(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-s+1,n-r},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-s^{\prime}+1})=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}}),\qquad(s^{\prime},k^{\prime})>(s,k),

but this is immediate from (
2.8
), (
2.9
) and the definition of the ordering (
3.13
). Similarly, for (
3.26
) we only need to check that for
1\leq s,s^{\prime}\leq n
and
0\leq k^{\prime}<s^{\prime}
, it holds that

\sum_{0\leq k<s}(\varpi_{(s,k)},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}})=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}}).

But the left hand side is

-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}\delta_{k^{\prime}<s}=-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{s,s^{\prime}},

which is exactly the right hand side by (
2.23
).

It follows from the above that
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{K}
as in (
2.68
), and that

\displaystyle\overset{\rightarrow}{\prod}_{i\in I}\mathbb{U}^{(i)}_{i,\bullet}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\prod^{\longrightarrow}_{(s,k)\in I_{1}}\prod_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\varphi\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}\right).

But by (
G.6
), we have

\prod^{\longrightarrow}_{(s,k)\in I_{1}}\prod_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\varphi\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}\right)=\mathcal{F}^{\prime},

the braided Fock–Goncharov flip.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Combined with Theorem
3.4
and Lemma
3.7
, we now obtain:

Theorem 3.11
.

With respect to the unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
on (
3.18
), the Fock–Goncharov flip
\mathcal{F}
is a modular multiplicative unitary with respect to

(3.27)

\hat{\mathbf{Q}}=\mathbf{L}_{\delta}^{1+|\hbar|^{-1}},\qquad\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{K}_{\delta}^{-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})},

where

\mathbf{L}_{\delta}=\mathbf{E}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i}),\qquad\mathbf{K}_{\delta}=\mathbf{E}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i}).

Furthermore,
(\mathcal{F},\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{G})
is a modular datum for

(3.28)

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{L}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{L}

with

(3.29)

\mathcal{L}=G\left(2\sum_{t=1}^{n}\overline{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t}}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-t}\right)

and

(3.30)

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant s\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\underset{0\leqslant(n-r)-k\leqslant n-s}{\prod_{0\leqslant k\leqslant s-1}^{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\varphi}\left(\overline{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}}\oplus-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,(n-r)-k}\right),

(3.31)

\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}=\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant s\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\underset{0\leq r-k-1\leq n-s}{\prod_{0\leqslant k\leqslant s-1}^{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\varphi}\left(-\overline{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,r-k-1}\right).

Proof..

Note that

(f_{(s,k)},2\varpi_{(s,k)})=1,\qquad(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})=1,

so by Lemma
1.17
we have that

(\mathbf{E}(2\varpi_{(s,k)}),\mathbf{E}(f_{(s,k)})),\qquad(\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}),\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}))

form
\hbar
-commuting pairs. From Theorem
3.8
, we conclude that
\mathbb{U}^{(s,k)}
is weakly modular with respect to the operator

\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{(s,k)}=\mathbf{E}(\varpi_{(s,k)})^{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}.

By Lemma
3.7
and using again (
3.26
), it now follows that
\mathcal{F}
is weakly modular for the modularity operator

\hat{\mathbf{Q}}=\otimes_{i\in I}\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{i}=\mathbf{L}_{\delta}^{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}.

The proof of the modularity is concluded by the easy observation that
\mathbf{L}_{\delta}\otimes\mathbf{K}_{\delta}^{-1}
commutes with
\mathcal{F}
.

To see that (
3.28
) completes the modular datum, we note that Lemma
3.7
, Theorem
3.8
and Theorem
3.10
, together with the expression (
3.21
) and the first part of the proof, give that
(\mathcal{F},\mathbf{L}_{\delta}^{1+|\hbar|^{-1}},\mathcal{G})
forms a weakly modular pair if we define

(3.32)

\mathcal{G}=\prod_{i\in I}^{\longrightarrow}\widetilde{\mathbb{U}}^{(i)}_{i,\bullet},

where

\widetilde{\mathbb{U}}^{(s)}=G_{\hbar}(2\overline{\varpi}_{s}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})=G_{-\hbar}(2\overline{\varpi}_{s}\otimes(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}))

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{U}}^{(s,k)}

\displaystyle=
