\displaystyle G_{\hbar}(2\overline{\varpi_{(s,k)}}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})F_{\hbar}\left(\overline{\mathbf{E}(f_{(s,k)})}\otimes\mathbf{E}(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})\star\left(\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{-\hbar}(2\overline{\varpi_{(s,k)}}\otimes(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}))\prod_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longleftarrow}\varphi_{-\hbar}\left(\overline{f_{(s,k)}}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{-\hbar}(2\overline{\varpi_{(s,k)}}\otimes(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}))\prod_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longleftarrow}\varphi_{-\hbar}\left(\overline{f_{(s,k)}}\oplus-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,r-s}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{-\hbar}(2\overline{\varpi_{(s,k)}}\otimes(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}))\prod_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\varphi_{-\hbar}\left(\overline{f_{(s,k)}}\oplus-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{G_{\hbar}(2\varpi_{(s,k)}\otimes\overline{-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}})\prod_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\varphi_{\hbar}\left(f_{(s,k)}\oplus\overline{-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}}\right)}.

As structurally the expression for (
3.32
) is of the same form as (
3.22
), using that

(\overline{-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}},\overline{-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}}})=(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,k},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s^{\prime},k^{\prime}}),\qquad 1\leq s,s^{\prime}\leq n,0\leq k\leq s,0\leq k^{\prime}\leq s^{\prime},

we find that
\mathcal{G}
is indeed of the form in (
3.28
).

The alternative form
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{L}
follows again straightforwardly from (
1.16
) and the formulas in Lemma
2.3
.
∎

For the moment, we can not conclude yet that
\mathcal{F}
will be modular with respect to an
arbitrary
unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
. This turns out to be true, but to prove this we will need some more detailed information on C
∗
-algebras and von Neumann algebras associated to the Fock–Goncharov flip. This is what we will turn to in the next section.

4.
von Neumann algebras associated to the Fock–Goncharov flip

By
[
SW01
, Theorem 2.3]
(see also
[
SW07
, Theorem 5]
) one can associate to a modular multiplicative unitary
\mathbb{W}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})
a von Neumann algebra
M
, obtained as the
\sigma
-weak closure

(4.1)

M=M_{\mathbb{W}}:=[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{W}\mid\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}]^{\sigma\textrm{-weak}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}).

This von Neumann algebra can further be enhanced into a
von Neumann bialgebra
, i.e.
M
comes equipped with a faithful normal unital
*
-homomorphism

(4.2)

\Delta=\Delta_{\mathbb{W}}\colon M\rightarrow M\bar{\otimes}M,\quad x\mapsto\mathbb{W}^{*}(1\otimes x)\mathbb{W},

satisfying the coassociativity condition. We refer to
(M,\Delta)
as the
von Neumann bialgebra
associated to
\mathbb{W}
.

Now if
\mathbb{W}
is modular with respect to
(\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}})
, one has by
[
SW01
, Proposition 2.2]
that

\hat{\mathbb{W}}=\Sigma\mathbb{W}^{*}\Sigma=\mathbb{W}_{21}^{*}

is a modular multiplicative unitary with respect to
(\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathbf{Q})
.
We refer to
(\hat{M},\hat{\Delta})=(M_{\hat{\mathbb{W}}},\Delta_{\hat{\mathbb{W}}})
as the
dual
von Neumann bialgebra. Explicitly, we can write

(4.3)

\hat{M}=M_{\hat{\mathbb{W}}}:=[(\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathbb{W}\mid\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}]^{\sigma\textrm{-weak}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}),

with the dual comultiplication

(4.4)

\hat{\Delta}=\Delta_{\hat{\mathbb{W}}}\colon\hat{M}\rightarrow\hat{M}\bar{\otimes}\hat{M},\quad x\mapsto\hat{\mathbb{W}}^{*}(1\otimes x)\hat{\mathbb{W}}.

The aim of this section is to show that in the case of the Fock–Goncharov flip, these von Neumann algebras are just completions of suitable
quantum tori
. To prove this, we will need to use some of the machinery in
[
Rie93
]
, which we first recall.

4.1.
C
∗
-algebras associated to a skew-symmetric space

The following material is completely standard, but we use some care to introduce the necessary formalism.

To a skew-symmetric space
V
and
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}
one can associate a C
∗
-algebra
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
such that the unitary
\hbar
-representations of
V
correspond to the non-degenerate
*
-representations of
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
. One may either view
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
as a deformation of the group C
∗
-algebra of
C^{*}(V)
or, in Pontryagin dual fashion, as a deformation
C_{0}^{\hbar}(\hat{V})
of the C
∗
-algebra
C_{0}(\hat{V})
, with
\hat{V}
the space of real linear functionals on
V
. Both viewpoints are useful to fuel intuition in the considerations below.

Write the canonical pairing between
V
and its linear dual
\hat{V}
as
4
4

4

In
[
Rie93
]
, one identifies
\hat{V}
and
V
through an auxiliary positive-definite inner product on
V
by which
w\mapsto\hat{w}=\langle w,-\rangle
can actually be viewed as a concrete map instead of abstract notation for elements in the dual space. Though this auxiliary structure will be inessential in what follows, the reader is free to use such a concrete identification to more directly follow the arguments in
[
Rie93
]
.

\hat{w}(v)=\langle\hat{w},v\rangle=\langle v,\hat{w}\rangle=\hat{w}\cdot v=v\cdot\hat{w},\qquad v\in V,\hat{w}\in\hat{V}.

Put

(4.5)

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}_{\hbar}\colon V\rightarrow\hat{V},\qquad\langle v,\mathcal{J}w\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\hbar(v,w),\qquad\forall v,w\in V.

We can then endow the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})
with the
*
-algebra structure such that

f^{*}(\hat{v})=\overline{f(\hat{v})}

and

(4.6)

\displaystyle(f\times_{\hbar}g)(\hat{v})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int\!\!\!\!\int_{V\times\hat{V}}f(\hat{v}-\mathcal{J}u)g(\hat{v}-\hat{w})e^{2\pi i\hat{w}\cdot u}\mathrm{d}\!\,u\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{w}

(4.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{V}f(\hat{v}-\mathcal{J}u)\hat{g}(u)e^{2\pi i\hat{v}\cdot u}\mathrm{d}\!\,u

(4.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{V}\hat{f}(u)g(\hat{v}+\mathcal{J}u)e^{2\pi i\hat{v}\cdot u}\mathrm{d}\!\,u.

Here we use the usual Fourier transform

(4.9)

\mathscr{F}\colon\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(V),\quad f\mapsto\hat{f},\quad\hat{f}(v)=\int_{\hat{V}}f(\hat{w})e^{-2\pi iv\cdot\hat{w}}\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{w},\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}),v\in V,

with the Lebesgue measures of
V
and
\hat{V}
normalized so that one has the reverse Fourier transform as

(4.10)

\mathscr{F}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{S}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}),\quad f\mapsto\check{f},\quad\check{f}(\hat{v})=\int_{V}f(w)e^{2\pi i\hat{v}\cdot w}\mathrm{d}\!\,w,\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(V),\hat{v}\in\hat{V}.

In fact, to see that
(\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}),\times_{\hbar},*)
is an associative
*
-algebra, it is easiest to consider the resulting
*
-algebra structure on
(\mathcal{S}(V),\ast_{\hbar},\sharp)
on
\mathcal{S}(V)
through the Fourier transform
\mathscr{F}
in (
4.9
), which leads to

(f\ast_{\hbar}g)(v)=\int_{V}f(u)g(v-u)e^{2\pi iv\cdot\mathcal{J}u}\mathrm{d}\!\,u,\qquad f^{\sharp}(u)=\overline{f(-u)},\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(V),

and so

(4.11)

\widehat{f\times_{\hbar}g}=\hat{f}\ast_{\hbar}\hat{g},\qquad\widehat{f^{*}}=\hat{f}^{\sharp}.

In fact, one easily checks that
(\mathcal{S}(V),*_{\hbar},\sharp)
is a
normed
associative
*
-algebra for the norm
f\mapsto\|\hat{f}\|_{1}
, which completes into the normed Banach
*
-algebra
(L^{1}(V),\ast_{\hbar},\sharp)
with the twisted convolution
*
-algebra structure. Hence the following definition is meaningful, as any
*
-representation of a Banach
*
-algebra on a Hilbert space is contractive (= norm-decreasing).

Definition 4.1
.

We define
C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)
to be the C
∗
-algebra obtained as the norm-completion of
(\mathcal{S}(V),\ast_{\hbar},\#)
with respect to its universal C
∗
-norm

\|f\|_{\hbar}:=\sup_{\pi}\{\|\pi(f)\|\},

with
\pi
ranging over all non-degenerate
*
-representations
\pi\colon L^{1}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
of
(L^{1}(V),\ast_{\hbar},\sharp)
on Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}
.

Correspondingly, we define
C_{0}^{\hbar}(\hat{V})
to be the C
∗
-algebra completion of
(\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}),\times_{\hbar},*)
through the C
∗
-norm

\|f\|_{\hbar}:=\|\hat{f}\|_{\hbar},\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}).

By construction, we then have that
C_{0}^{\hbar}(\hat{V})
and
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
are isomorphic through

C_{0}^{\hbar}(\hat{V})\cong C^{*}_{\hbar}(V),\quad f\mapsto\hat{f}\textrm{ for }f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}),

and in what follows we can simply identify these two C
∗
-algebras. For
f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})
, we denote by
f_{\hbar}
its copy inside
C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)
for emphasis, and we then denote the inclusion map of
\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})
into
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
by

(4.12)

\mathcal{Q}\colon\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})\rightarrow C^{*}_{\hbar}(V),\qquad f\mapsto f_{\hbar}.

On the other hand, there is less need to make such a differentiation on the Fourier dual side, so if
f\in\mathcal{S}(V)
we simply write also
f\in C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
.

Lemma 4.2
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between unitary
\hbar
-representations
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
of
V
and non-degenerate
*
-representations
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
of
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
, determined by

(4.13)

\pi(f_{\hbar})=\pi(\hat{f}):=\int_{V}\hat{f}(v)\pi(v)\mathrm{d}\!\,v,\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}).

Proof..

Well-known. The proof goes as follows: writing

(u\cdot f)(v)=e^{\pi i\hbar(u,v)}f(v-u),\qquad f\in L^{1}(V),u,v\in V,

one checks that, for any
f\in L^{1}(V)
, the map

V\ni u\mapsto u\cdot f\in L^{1}(V),

is continuous with respect to the
L^{1}
-norm. One further verifies that

L^{1}(V)\ni f\mapsto u\cdot f\in L^{1}(V)

is an isometric linear map for any
u\in V
, with inverse

f\mapsto u^{-1}\cdot f.

As a last ingredient, one uses the easily checked
multiplier property

g^{\sharp}\ast_{\hbar}(u\cdot f)=(u^{-1}\cdot g)^{\sharp}\ast_{\hbar}f,\qquad f,g\in L^{1}(V),u\in V.

∎

For example, with respect to the standard unitary
\hbar
-representation of
V
given through (
1.6
), the associated
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
-representation is determined by

(4.14)

\pi(f)g=f\ast_{\hbar}g,\qquad f,g\in\mathcal{S}(V).

If we conjugate by the unitary Fourier transform
\mathscr{F}
, we find the unitarily equivalent unitary
\hbar
-representation
\pi=\pi_{L^{2}(\hat{V})}
of
V
on
L^{2}(\hat{V})
(which we will denote by the same symbol), which is now determined by

(4.15)

(\pi(v)g)(\hat{w})=e^{2\pi iv\cdot\hat{w}}g(\hat{w}+\mathcal{J}v),\qquad v\in V,\hat{w}\in\hat{V},g\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}),

and where the associated
*
-representation of
C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)
on
L^{2}(\hat{V})
is determined,
using (
4.8
), by

(4.16)

\pi(f_{\hbar})g=f\times_{\hbar}g,\qquad f,g\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}).

Definition 4.3
.

We refer to either
(L^{2}(V),\pi)
or
(L^{2}(\hat{V}),\pi)
, with
\pi
as in (
4.14
) or (
4.16
), as the
standard
*
-representation
of
C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)
, or as the
standard unitary
\hbar
-representation
of
V
. We then put

(4.17)

L_{\hbar}(V):=[\pi(f_{\hbar})\mid f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})]^{\sigma-\textrm{weak}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\hat{V})),

which defines a von Neumann algebra called the
twisted group von Neumann algebra
of
V
.

We call a non-degenerate
*
-representation of
C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)

(faithfully) normal
if it extends to a (faithful) normal
*
-representation of
L_{\hbar}(V)
. We then also use this terminology for the associated unitary
\hbar
-representation of
V
.

If
V
is symplectic with Lagrangian subspace
W\subseteq V
, we get through the construction in Example
1.6
identifications

(4.18)

C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)\cong\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(W)),\qquad L_{\hbar}(V)\cong\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(W)),

and
any
unitary
\hbar
-representation give rise to a faithfully normal
*
-representation of
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
.

In particular,
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
is a nuclear C
∗
-algebra if
V
is either symplectic or trivial. Since unitary
\hbar
-representations of a direct sum of skew-symmetric spaces are just pairs of pointwise commuting
\hbar
-representations of the components on a same Hilbert space, it follows from the decomposition (
1.2
) that

(4.19)

C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)\cong C^{*}(\mathrm{Rad}(V))\otimes C^{*}_{\hbar}(V^{\prime})

is nuclear for any skew-symmetric space, and that

(4.20)

C^{*}_{\hbar}(V\oplus W)\cong C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)\otimes C^{*}_{\hbar}(W)

for any direct sum of skew-symmetric spaces. Moreover, the isomorphism (
4.19
) also gives that the standard
*
-representation
\pi
of
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
is faithful for any skew-symmetric space
V
and that, more generally, the decomposition (
4.19
) is implemented concretely through an identification of (tensor products of) standard representations in the form (
4.14
) via the canonical unitary

L^{2}(V\oplus W)\cong L^{2}(V)\otimes L^{2}(W).

This then also establishes

L_{\hbar}(V\oplus W)\cong L_{\hbar}(V)\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(W).

If we now, for a general skew-symmetric space
V
, consider a direct sum decomposition as in (
1.3
), and identify
\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}e_{i}
as a Lagrangian subspace of
Z_{i}
, one gets in particular
isomorphisms of C
∗
-algebras and von Neumann algebras

(4.21)

C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)\cong C_{0}(\widehat{\mathrm{Rad}(V)})\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{K}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})),\qquad L_{\hbar}(V)\cong L^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathrm{Rad}(V)})\bar{\otimes}\overline{\bigotimes}_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R})).

The following is a direct consequence of the above decompositions.

Lemma 4.4
.

If
V
is a skew-symmetric space, then
L_{\hbar}(V)
is a type
I
-von Neumann algebra. It is a factor if and only if
V
is symplectic.

The following lemma is a consequence of the fact that if
Z\subseteq V
is a subspace of a skew-symmetric space, we can always choose the decomposition (
1.3
) of
V
such that

Z=(Z\cap\mathrm{Rad}(V))\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}(Z\cap Z_{i}),

with either
\emptyset,\{e_{i}\}
or
\{e_{i},\hat{e}_{i}\}
a basis of
Z\cap Z_{i}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

If
Z\subseteq V
is an inclusion of skew-symmetric spaces, then the standard unitary
\hbar
-representation of
V
restricts to a faithfully normal unitary
\hbar
-representation of
Z
.

Concretely, the resulting embedding
L_{\hbar}(Z)\subseteq L_{\hbar}(V)
is determined by

f_{\hbar}=\int_{Z}\hat{f}(z)\pi(z)\mathrm{d}\!\,z,\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{Z}).

4.2.
Rieffel deformation of vector spaces with boundary

For what follows, it will be important to know that one can extend the domain of the quantization map (
4.12
).

Fix again
V
a skew-symmetric space, and consider
C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V})
, the space of uniformly smooth bounded functions on
\hat{V}
, i.e. those functions such that the Banach space-valued function
\hat{v}\mapsto f_{\hat{v}}\in C_{b}(\hat{V})
is smooth, with
f_{\hat{v}}(\hat{w})=f(\hat{w}-\hat{v})
. The following is then a special case of Proposition
E.1
.

Lemma 4.6
.

The quantization map (
4.12
) extends uniquely to a map

(4.22)

\mathcal{Q}\colon C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V})\rightarrow M(C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)),\qquad f\mapsto f_{\hbar},

with
M(C^{*}_{\hbar}(V))
the multiplier C
∗
-algebra of
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
, in such a way that, for
g\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})
, one has

f_{\hbar}g_{\hbar}=(f\times_{\hbar}g)_{\hbar},\qquad g_{\hbar}f_{\hbar}=(g\times_{\hbar}f)_{\hbar},\qquad f\in C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V}),g\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}),

with
f\times_{\hbar}g
and
g\times_{\hbar}f
defined by respectively (
4.7
) and (
4.8
).

For example, we have in
C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V})
the function

(4.23)

e^{2\pi iv}\colon\hat{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\qquad\hat{w}\mapsto e^{2\pi iv\cdot\hat{w}}.

Then it is easily checked, e.g. from (
4.7
) and (
4.13
), that for any unitary
\hbar
-representation
\pi
we have, inversely to (
4.13
),

(4.24)

\pi(e_{\hbar}^{2\pi iv})=\pi(v).

One further has

(4.25)

e_{\hbar}^{v}\,\eta\,C_{\hbar}^{*}(V),

where
\eta
denotes the affiliation relation for C
∗
-algebras, which we recall in Appendix
D
. The affiliation (
4.25
) is determined uniquely by

(4.26)

\pi(e_{\hbar}^{2\pi v})=\mathbf{E}(v),\qquad v\in V,

for any unitary
\hbar
-representation of
V
(see the discussion following Theorem
E.2
).

We apply this as follows. Assume we have dual bases
\{f_{i}\}
and
\{\hat{f}_{i}\}
of
V
and
\hat{V}
, indexed by a set

(4.27)

I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}.

We identify
V\cong\mathbb{R}^{I}\cong\hat{V}
through these bases via

v=\sum_{i}v_{i}f_{i},\qquad\hat{v}=\sum_{i}\hat{v}_{i}\hat{f}_{i},\qquad v\in V,\hat{v}\in\hat{V}.

Consider the convex closed cone

(4.28)

V\supseteq V_{+}=V_{+}(I_{0},I_{1}):=\{v\in V\mid v_{i}=\langle v,\hat{f}_{i}\rangle\geq 0,\forall i\in I_{1}\}

as well as the dual extended convex closed cone

(4.29)

\hat{V}\subseteq\hat{V}_{+}=\hat{V}_{+}(I_{0},I_{1}):=\mathbb{R}^{I_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}_{-\infty}^{I_{1}},\qquad\mathbb{R}_{-\infty}=[-\infty,\infty),

endowed with the product topology and with
\mathbb{R}_{-\infty}
having its topology generated by the open intervals in
\mathbb{R}
as well as the intervals
[-\infty,a)
for
a\in\mathbb{R}
.

Consider the inclusions

(4.30)

C_{0}(\hat{V})\subseteq C_{0}(\hat{V}_{+})\subseteq C_{b}(\hat{V}_{+})\subseteq C_{b}(\hat{V}),

where the final inclusion is through restriction to
\hat{V}
. Since the translation action of
\hat{V}
extends continuously to
\hat{V}_{+}
, the above inclusions are
\hat{V}
-equivariant, and we can consider also

(4.31)

C_{0}^{\infty}(\hat{V})\subseteq C_{0}^{\infty}(\hat{V}_{+})\subseteq C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V}_{+})\subseteq C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V}).

Put

(4.32)

C_{\hbar}^{*}(V_{+}):=[f_{\hbar}\mid f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\hat{V}_{+})]\subseteq M(C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)).

Theorem 4.7
.

The space
C_{\hbar}^{*}(V_{+})
is a C
∗
-algebra. Moreover,

(4.33)

e_{\hbar}^{w}\,\eta\,C_{\hbar}^{*}(V_{+}),\qquad w\in V_{+}.

The proof of the above theorem can be found in Appendix
E
.

This theorem will be important to have access to particular quotient maps. Namely, assume that

I^{\prime}\subseteq I,\qquad\textrm{ with }I_{i}^{\prime}=I_{i}\cap I^{\prime},

and assume that we have

(f_{i},f_{j})=0,\qquad\forall i\in I_{0}\setminus I_{0}^{\prime},j\in I^{\prime}.

Put

V^{\prime}=V(I_{0}^{\prime},I_{1}^{\prime})\subseteq V

as a skew-symmetric subspace. Then although this map does not allow a splitting as skew-symmetric spaces, such a splitting exists when passing to the associated C
∗
-algebras:

Theorem 4.8
.

There exists a unique non-degenerate C
∗
-algebra homomorphism

(4.34)

\pi_{I^{\prime}}\colon C_{\hbar}^{*}(V_{+})\rightarrow C_{\hbar}^{*}(V_{+}^{\prime}),\qquad f_{\hbar}\mapsto f_{I^{\prime},\hbar},\qquad f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\hat{V}_{+}),

where
f_{I^{\prime}}=f\circ\iota_{I^{\prime}}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\hat{V}_{+}^{\prime})
for

(4.35)

\iota_{I^{\prime}}\colon\hat{V}_{+}^{\prime}\rightarrow\hat{V}_{+},\qquad\iota_{I^{\prime}}(\hat{v}^{\prime})_{i}=\begin{cases}\hat{v}^{\prime}_{i}&\text{if}\quad i\in I^{\prime},\\
-\infty&\text{if}\quad i\in I_{1}\setminus I_{1}^{\prime},\\
0&\text{if}\quad i\in I_{0}\setminus I_{0}^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Moreover, under this quotient map we have

(4.36)

\pi_{I^{\prime}}(e_{\hbar}^{f_{i}})=\begin{cases}e_{\hbar}^{f_{i}}&\text{if}\quad i\in I^{\prime},\\
0&\text{if}\quad i\in I_{1}\setminus I_{1}^{\prime},\\
1&\text{if}\quad i\in I_{0}\setminus I_{0}^{\prime}.\end{cases}

The proof is presented at the end of Appendix
E
.

Remark 4.9
.

To compare this with the algebraic setup, one should view
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)
as an analytic analogue of
\mathbb{C}_{q}[X_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,X_{n}^{\pm 1}]
, a skew Laurent polynomial algebra with (say)

X_{i}X_{j}=q^{\varepsilon_{ij}}X_{j}X_{i}

for
q
a (formal) constant and
\varepsilon_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then although no
X_{i}
can be put to zero in this algebra, one can easily obtain such quotient maps for the associated skew polynomial algebra
\mathbb{C}_{q}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, which is the algebraic counterpart of
C_{\hbar}^{*}(V_{+})
when
I=I_{1}
.

4.3.
von Neumann algebras associated to Fock–Goncharov flips

In this section, we determine explicitly the von Neumann algebras
M_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\hat{M}_{\mathcal{F}}=M_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}}
associated to a Fock–Goncharov flip
\mathcal{F}
(as in (
4.1
) and (
4.3
)), where in particular

\hat{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{F}_{21}^{*}.

We first show that the modularity of
\mathcal{F}
, as well as the associated von Neumann algebras
M_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\hat{M}_{\mathcal{F}}
, are independent of the precise choice of unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an arbitrary Fock–Goncharov flip. Then with
\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
and
\mathcal{G}
as in Theorem
3.11
, we have that
(\mathcal{F},\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{G})
is a modular datum.

Proof..

Let
V_{N}
be the symplectic space as in (
3.15
), with its associated
\hbar
-Heisenberg representation on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then this representation is faithfully normal, and hence also its restriction to
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
is faithfully normal by Lemma
4.5
. We may hence in particular identify
C^{*}_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
.

Let now
\mathcal{F}
be the associated Fock–Goncharov flip acting on
\mathcal{H}
. By (
4.25
), we have

(4.37)

\mathcal{F}\in M(C^{*}_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})\otimes C^{*}_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}))

Consider its associated modular datum
(\mathcal{F},\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{G})
. If then
\pi
is a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
, seen as a non-degenerate
*
-representation of
C^{*}_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})
, we are to show that

(\mathcal{F}_{\pi},\mathbf{Q}_{\pi},\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi},\mathcal{G}_{\pi}):=((\pi\otimes\pi)\mathcal{F},\pi(\mathbf{Q}),\pi(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}),(\overline{\pi}\otimes\pi)\mathcal{G})

is a modular datum.

Now (
3.2
) can be rewritten as

\omega((\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega_{\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\xi,\mathbf{Q}\eta})\mathcal{F})=\omega(\overline{(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega_{\xi,\eta})\mathcal{G}}^{*}),\qquad\forall\xi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{Q}^{-1}),\forall\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{Q}),\forall\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*},

which implies

(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega_{\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\xi,\mathbf{Q}\eta})\mathcal{F}=\overline{(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega_{\xi,\eta})\mathcal{G}}^{*}.

As these are elements of
M(C^{*}_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}))
, we can apply
\pi
, and then find

\langle\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime},(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{F})(\eta\otimes\mathbf{Q}\xi^{\prime})\rangle=\langle\overline{\eta}\otimes\mathbf{Q}\eta^{\prime},(\overline{\pi}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{G})(\overline{\xi}\otimes\xi^{\prime})\rangle,\qquad\forall\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{Q}),\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H}.

Since
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}\otimes\mathbf{Q}
clearly commutes with
(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathcal{F}
, this implies as well that

\langle\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime},(\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{F})(\eta\otimes\xi^{\prime})\rangle=\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}\eta}\otimes\eta^{\prime},(\overline{\pi}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{G})(\overline{\xi}\otimes\xi^{\prime})\rangle,\\
\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}),\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}).

So,

(\omega_{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}\xi,\eta}\pi\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathcal{F}=(\omega_{\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}\eta},\overline{\xi}}\overline{\pi}\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathcal{G},\qquad\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}).

As these are elements in
M(C^{*}_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}))
, we can again apply
\pi
, leading to

\langle\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{\pi}(\eta\otimes\xi^{\prime})\rangle=\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}\eta}\otimes\eta^{\prime},\mathcal{G}_{\pi}(\overline{\xi}\otimes\xi^{\prime})\rangle,\qquad\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}),\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}).

As
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pi}\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\pi}
commutes with
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
, we are done.
∎

Note that there is some substance to this result: for any
N\geq 2
we have that
\mathrm{Rad}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})\neq\{0\}
, so
C^{*}_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})
allows non-faithful non-degenerate
*
-representations. We show however that, nevertheless, all Fock–Goncharov flips lead to the same von Neumann bialgebras.

Recall
B^{-}_{N},B_{N}^{+}
as defined in (
2.15
).

Lemma 4.11
.

If
\pi
is a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
, its restrictions to
B_{N}^{\pm}
are faithfully normal.

Proof..

By Lemma
2.6
, we can find symplectic subspaces
Z^{\pm}
such that

B_{N}^{\pm}\subseteq Z^{\pm}\subseteq\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}.

The result now follows from Lemma
4.5
and the fact that any unitary
\hbar
-representation of a symplectic space is faithfully normal.
∎

If we consider the standard unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
as in (
4.17
), we will refer to its associated Fock–Goncharov flip (
2.69
) as the
standard Fock–Goncharov flip
, and in this section we fix for this the notation

(4.38)

\mathcal{F}\in L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}).

We then view

(4.39)

M=M_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}),\qquad\hat{M}=M_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}}\subseteq L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}).

Note that it immediately follows from the formula for
\mathcal{F}
that in fact

(4.40)

\mathcal{F}\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}).

From Lemma
4.11
, we then obtain:

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the standard Fock–Goncharov flip, and
\pi
a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
. Then with
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}=(\pi\otimes\pi)\mathcal{F}
, we get

(M_{\mathcal{F}},\Delta_{\mathcal{F}})\cong(M_{\mathcal{F}_{\pi}},\Delta_{\mathcal{F}_{\pi}}),\qquad(M_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}},\Delta_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}})\cong(M_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}},\Delta_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{\pi}}).

Because of this, we from now on simply refer to

(M_{\mathcal{F}},\Delta_{\mathcal{F}}),\qquad(M_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}},\Delta_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}})

as the
Fock–Goncharov von Neumann bialgebra
and
dual von Neumann bialgebra
.

Specializing Corollary
4.12
further, we have:

Corollary 4.13
.

Consider the symplectic quotient

\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}\twoheadrightarrow D_{N}

as in (
2.19
). Let
\pi
be an arbitary unitary
\hbar
-representation of
D_{N}
.

Then viewing
\pi
as a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
, the associated Fock–Goncharov flip
\mathcal{F}
implements an isomorphic copy of the Fock–Goncharov von Neumann bialgebra and its dual.

Note that from (
4.40
), we can view

M_{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\qquad M_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}}\subseteq L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}).

Our next aim is to show that these are in fact equalities.

Endow
B_{N}^{\pm}
with the respective bases

(4.41)

\{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{f}_{i}\mid i\in I\}=\{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{(s,k)}\mid 1\leq t,s\leq n,0\leq k<s\},

(4.42)

\{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{f}_{i}\mid i\in I\}=\{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{(s,k)}\mid 1\leq t,s\leq n,0\leq k<s\}.

We use for
B_{N}^{\pm}
, endowed with these bases, the notation introduced in (
4.28
) and (
4.29
).

We fix a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
with associated Fock–Goncharov flip
\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma 4.14
.

For
1\leq r\leq n
,
r\leq b\leq n
and
1\leq i\leq r
, let
B^{b,r,i}
be as in (
2.62
). Then

B^{b,r,i}\in M(C_{\hbar}^{*}(B^{-}_{N,+})\otimes C_{\hbar}^{*}(B^{+}_{N,+})).

In particular,

(4.43)

\mathcal{F}\in M(C_{\hbar}^{*}(B^{-}_{N,+})\otimes C^{*}_{\hbar}(B^{+}_{N,+})).

Note that (
4.43
) is much stronger than (
4.37
), as when passing to multiplier algebras the inclusion
C_{\hbar}^{*}(B^{-}_{N})\subseteq C_{\hbar}^{*}(B^{-}_{N,+})
switches the order of inclusion, cf. (
4.30
) for the classical intuition for this.

Proof..

By (
4.33
), we have that

\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r})=e_{\hbar}^{2\pi\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}}\,\eta\,C_{\hbar}^{*}(B_{N,+}^{-}),

and similarly

\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b})=e_{\hbar}^{2\pi\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b}}\,\eta\,C_{\hbar}^{*}(B_{N,+}^{+}).

Then by the observation (
D.3
), we have

\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b})=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r})\otimes\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b})\,\eta\,C_{\hbar}^{*}(B^{-}_{N,+})\otimes C^{*}_{\hbar}(B^{+}_{N,+}).

Now as
\overline{F}
is a continuous function on
[0,\infty)
, we get by functional calculus (Lemma
D.6
and the remark following it) that

B^{b,r,i}=\varphi(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b})=\overline{F}(\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r})\otimes\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b}))\\
\in M(C_{\hbar}^{*}(B^{-}_{N,+})\otimes C^{*}_{\hbar}(B^{+}_{N,+})).

The statement for
\mathcal{F}
follows immediately.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
be two self-adjoint operators on Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{G}
, and assume
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{A})=\mathbb{R}
. Assume that
u
is a unitary in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})
such that

(1\otimes u)G_{\hbar}(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})=G_{\hbar}(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})(1\otimes u).

Then
u
commutes with all
e^{it\mathbf{B}}
, and hence commutes with
\mathbf{B}
.

Proof..

Immediate by specializing
\mathbf{A}
to
t\in\mathbb{R}
using functional calculus.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
be two strictly positive operators on Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{G}
, and assume
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{A})=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Assume that
u
is a unitary in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G})
such that

(1\otimes u)F_{\hbar}(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})=F_{\hbar}(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})(1\otimes u).

Then
u
commutes with all
\mathbf{B}^{it}
, and hence commutes with
\mathbf{B}
.

Proof..

Again by functional calculus, the assumption implies that

uF_{\hbar}(t\mathbf{B})=F_{\hbar}(t\mathbf{B})u,\qquad\forall t>0.

In other words, writing
\mathbf{H}=\ln(\mathbf{B})
, we have

(4.44)

u\overline{\varphi}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{H}+t)u^{*}=\overline{\varphi}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{H}+t),\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}.

Assume now that
\hbar>0
. Then as
\overline{\varphi}_{\hbar}
extends to a holomorphic function on the upper half plane
\mathbb{H}
, the equality (
4.44
) continues to hold (as an equality of unbounded normal operators) for any
t\in\mathbb{H}
.
As
\overline{\varphi}_{\hbar}
satisfies, rewriting (
A.12
), the functional equation

(4.45)

\overline{\varphi}_{\hbar}(z+2\pi i)=(1+e^{i\pi\hbar^{-1}}e^{\hbar^{-1}z})\overline{\varphi}_{\hbar}(z),

we can conclude from (
4.44
) that

ue^{\hbar^{-1}\mathbf{H}}u^{*}=e^{\hbar^{-1}\mathbf{H}}.

So
u
commutes with
\mathbf{H}
, and the lemma follows.

The proof in the case
\hbar<0
follows by taking adjoints.
∎

Note now that by Lemma
2.3
, we can apply Theorem
4.8
to obtain non-degenerate
*
-representations

(4.46)

\pi_{t}=\pi_{\{t\}},\qquad\pi_{(s,k)}=\pi_{\{s,(s,0)\}},\qquad t\in I_{0},(s,k)\in I_{1}

of
C_{\hbar}^{*}(B^{-}_{N,+})
. Writing

I_{0}^{\prime}(t)=\{t\},\quad I_{1}^{\prime}(t)=\emptyset,\quad I_{0}^{\prime}(s,k)=\{s\},\qquad I_{1}^{\prime}(s,k)=\{(s,0)\},

we may identify (for respectively
i=t
or
i=(s,k)
)

B^{-}(I_{0}^{\prime}(i),I_{1}^{\prime}(i))\subseteq W_{i},\qquad\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t}\mapsto\varpi_{t},\quad\varpi_{s}\mapsto\varpi_{(s,k)},\quad\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,0}\mapsto f_{(s,k)},

with
W_{i}
as in (
3.15
), and hence view the above
\pi_{i}
as non-degenerate
*
-representations of
C_{\hbar}^{*}(B^{-}_{N,+})
on
\mathcal{H}_{i}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. By Lemma
4.14
, we can apply these projection maps to the first leg of
\mathcal{F}
. Then one sees in fact immediately that

(4.47)

(\pi_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{U}^{(i)},\qquad i\in I,

with
\mathbb{U}^{(i)}
as in (
3.19
) and (
3.20
). This is the main ingredient to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a Fock–Goncharov flip with associated dual von Neumann algebra
\hat{M}=M_{\hat{\mathcal{F}}}
. Then

\hat{M}=L_{\hbar}(B^{+}_{N}).

Proof..

Assume that
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})
. As we already know that
\hat{M}\subseteq L_{\hbar}(B^{+}_{N})
, we are to show

\hat{M}^{\prime}\subseteq L_{\hbar}(B^{+}_{N})^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}).

But assume that
x\in\hat{M}^{\prime}
, i.e.

(4.48)

\mathcal{F}(1\otimes x)=(1\otimes x)\mathcal{F}.

Then (
4.48
) gives us

(4.49)

\mathbb{U}^{(i)}(1\otimes x)=(1\otimes x)\mathbb{U}^{(i)},\qquad i\in I.

By the expressions (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) for the
\mathbb{U}^{(i)}
, we find that

[(1\otimes x),G(2\varpi\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})]=0=\left[(1\otimes x),\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f)\otimes\left(\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r})\right)\right)\right]

for all
1\leq s\leq n
and
s\leq r\leq n
. Applying Lemma
4.15
and Lemma
4.16
, we find, using the notation
[-,-]=0
to denote strong commutation between two operators, that

[x,\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})]=0=[x,\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r})],\qquad\forall 1\leq s\leq n.

The theorem now follows from the first part of the proposition below.
∎

Recall the standard generators from Definition
2.17
, where we abbreviate

(4.50)

\mathbf{E}_{s}:=\mathbf{E}_{s,s+1}=\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}),\qquad 1\leq s\leq n.

We also use the notation

(4.51)

\mathbf{K}_{s}:=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}),\qquad 1\leq s\leq n.

Proposition 4.18
.

(1)

The operators
\{\mathbf{E}_{s}\mid 1\leq s\leq n\}
generate
L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{+})
.

(2)

The operators
\{\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathbf{E}_{s}\mid 1\leq s\leq n\}
generate
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})
.

Proof..

(1) Assume that
x
is an operator which strongly commutes with all
\mathbf{E}_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq n
. We show by induction on
N-s
that
x
then also commutes with all
\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,k})
for
0\leq k<s
, and hence with all
\mathbf{E}(e_{a,b,c})^{it}
for
(a,b,c)\in\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}\,\!^{\prime}_{N}
, which is enough to prove the lemma.

The base case is automatic as
\mathbf{E}_{n}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{1,0})
.

Assume then that, for fixed
s
, we know that
x
commutes with all
\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,k})
for
0\leq k<s
, as well as with
\mathbf{E}_{N-s-1}
. Putting

\mathbf{A}=\boxplus_{N-s-1\leq r<n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s+1,n-r}),\qquad\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s+1,0}),

we have that
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}

-\hbar
-commute, so by (
1.29
) we can write

\mathbf{E}_{N-s-1}=\mathbf{A}\boxplus\mathbf{B}=F_{\hbar}(\mathbf{A}\star\mathbf{B}^{-1})\mathbf{B}F_{\hbar}(\mathbf{A}\star\mathbf{B}^{-1})^{*}.

Hence from (
2.8
) and Lemma
1.17
we deduce

(4.52)

e^{-\pi\hbar s}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,0})^{it}\mathbf{E}_{N-s-1}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,0})^{-it}=F_{\hbar}(e^{-2\pi\hbar s}\mathbf{A}\star\mathbf{B}^{-1})\mathbf{B}F_{\hbar}(e^{-2\pi\hbar s}\mathbf{A}\star\mathbf{B}^{-1})^{*}.

As
x
commutes with the left hand side by assumption, and as
F_{\hbar}(e^{-s}\mathbf{C})
converges strongly to
1
for any positive operator
\mathbf{C}
(by the dominated convergence theorem and (
1.22
)), we see by letting
\hbar s
tend to
-\infty
on the right hand side of (
4.52
) that
x
must also commute with
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s+1,0})
.

Similarly, writing now rather

\mathbf{E}_{N-s-1}=\mathbf{B}\boxplus\mathbf{A}=F_{\hbar}(\mathbf{B}\star\mathbf{A}^{-1})^{*}\mathbf{A}F_{\hbar}(\mathbf{B}\star\mathbf{A}^{-1}),

a same argument gives that
x
must also commute with
\mathbf{A}=\boxplus_{N-s-1\leq r<n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s+1,n-r})
.

We can now conclude by a second induction on the variable
k
: if we assume that
x
commutes with
\boxplus_{N-s-1\leq r\leq n-k}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s+1,n-r})
, the same argument as above, conjugating now however with
\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,k})^{it}
, gives that
x
also commutes with
\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s+1,n-k})
and
\boxplus_{N-s-1\leq r\leq n-k-1}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s+1,n-r})
. This finishes the proof part (1) of the lemma.

(2) This follows immediately from part (1).
∎

We can similarly determine the von Neumann algebra
M=M_{\mathcal{F}}
associated to
\mathcal{F}
, by interpreting
M
as the dual von Neumann algebra associated to
\hat{\mathcal{F}}
. First, we have the following analogue of Proposition
4.18
. We introduce for this the notation

(4.53)

\mathbf{F}_{s}:=\mathbf{F}_{s,s+1}=\boxplus_{0\leqslant r\leqslant s-1}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,r}),\qquad 1\leq s\leq n,

(4.54)

\mathbf{L}_{s}:=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s}),\qquad 1\leq s\leq n.

Proposition 4.19
.

(1)

The operators
\{\mathbf{F}_{s}\mid 1\leq s\leq n\}
generate
L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{-})
.

(2)

The operators
\{\mathbf{L}_{s},\mathbf{F}_{s}\mid 1\leq s\leq n\}
generate
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
.

Proof..

The proof is completely similar to the one for Proposition
4.18
.
∎

To get an analogue of Theorem
4.17
, we first obtain a direct formula for
\hat{\mathcal{F}}
.

Lemma 4.20
.

We have

(4.55)

\hat{\mathcal{F}}=\hat{\mathcal{K}}\hat{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}

where

\hat{\mathcal{K}}=\overline{G}(2\sum_{t=1}^{n}(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t})\otimes(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{N-t}))

and

\hat{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}}=\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant s\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\underset{0\leqslant(n-r)-k\leqslant n-s}{\prod_{0\leqslant k\leqslant s-1}^{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\varphi}(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,(n-r)-k}\oplus-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}).

Proof..

This follows immediately from (
2.73
).
∎

Now note that we have an isomorphism of skew-symmetric spaces

(4.56)

\theta\colon\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{N}\rightarrow\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown$\!}_{N}},\qquad e_{a,b,c}\mapsto\overline{-e_{b,a,c}}

and that under this isomorphism

-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\mapsto\overline{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}},\qquad-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}\mapsto\overline{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}},\qquad 1\leq s\leq n,0\leq k\leq s.

This leads to a
*
-isomorphism of von Neumann algebras

(4.57)

\Theta\colon L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})\rightarrow\overline{L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})},\qquad\mathbf{E}(v)\mapsto\mathbf{E}(\theta(v))

such that

(4.58)

\Theta(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}))=\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})},\qquad\Theta(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}))=\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})}.

On the other hand, from (
4.55
) and a comparison with (
2.66
) we get for the standard Fock–Goncharov flip that

(4.59)

(\Theta\otimes\Theta)(\hat{\mathcal{F}})=\overline{\mathcal{F}},\qquad(\Theta\otimes\Theta)(\mathcal{F})=\overline{\hat{\mathcal{F}}}.

Then through Corollary
4.12
, we get the following analogue of Theorem
4.17
:

Theorem 4.21
.

Let
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a Fock–Goncharov flip. Then

M_{\mathcal{F}}=L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}).

To end this section, we determine how the coproducts (
4.2
) and (
4.4
) act on the standard generators. Note that by Proposition
4.18

and Proposition
4.19
, this then completely determines the coproduct.

Proposition 4.22
.

On the generators
\mathbf{L}_{s},\mathbf{F}_{s}
of
M
, resp.
\mathbf{K}_{s},\mathbf{E}_{s}
of
\hat{M}
, we have

\Delta(\mathbf{L}_{s})=\mathbf{L}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{L}_{s},\qquad\hat{\Delta}(\mathbf{K}_{s})=\mathbf{K}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{K}_{s},

\Delta(\mathbf{F}_{s})=(\mathbf{F}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{L}_{s})\boxplus(1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s}),\qquad\hat{\Delta}(\mathbf{E}_{s})=(\mathbf{E}_{s}\otimes 1)\boxplus(\mathbf{K}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{s}).

Proof..

Recall the identity (
4.47
). Then applying
\pi_{i}
to the first leg of the pentagon identity

\mathcal{F}_{23}\mathcal{F}_{12}\mathcal{F}_{23}^{*}=\mathcal{F}_{12}\mathcal{F}_{13},

we find

(4.60)

(\mathrm{id}\otimes\hat{\Delta})\mathbb{U}^{(i)}=\mathbb{U}^{(i)}_{13}\mathbb{U}^{(i)}_{12}.

If we take
i=s\in I_{0}
, this gives

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes\hat{\Delta})G(2\varpi_{s}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(2\varpi_{s}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})_{13}G(2\varpi_{s}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})_{12}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(2\varpi_{s}\otimes(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}\otimes 1\dotplus 1\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})),

so by functional calculus we get

\hat{\Delta}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})=\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}\otimes 1\dotplus 1\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s},

and hence
\hat{\Delta}(\mathbf{K}_{s})=\mathbf{K}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{K}_{s}
. Alternatively, we could also verify directly from e.g. (
2.69
) that

(\mathbf{K}^{-it}\otimes\mathbf{K}^{-it})\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{K}^{it}\otimes 1)=\mathcal{F},\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.

Now taking on the other hand
i=(s,0)
, we find from (
4.60
) and (
3.21
) that

(\mathrm{id}\otimes\hat{\Delta})\left(G\left(2\varpi_{(s,k)}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}\right)\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{(s,k)})\otimes\mathbf{E}_{s}\right)\right)\\
=G\left(2\varpi_{(s,k)}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}\right)_{12}\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{(s,k)})\otimes\mathbf{E}_{s}\right)_{13}G\left(2\varpi_{(s,k)}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}\right)_{12}\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{(s,k)})\otimes\mathbf{E}_{s}\right)_{12}.

Then from (
1.17
), (
3.14
) and the first part of the proof, this gives

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\otimes\hat{\Delta})\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{(s,k)})\otimes\mathbf{E}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{(s,k)})\otimes\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})\otimes\mathbf{E}_{s}\right)\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{(s,k)})\otimes\mathbf{E}_{s}\right)_{12}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{(s,k)})\otimes((\mathbf{K}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{s})\boxplus(\mathbf{E}_{s}\otimes 1))\right),

where in the last step we used (
1.30
).
Again by functional calculus (cf. the proof of Lemma
4.16
), this let’s us conclude that

\hat{\Delta}(\mathbf{E}_{s})=(\mathbf{E}_{s}\otimes 1)\boxplus(\mathbf{K}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{s}).

Now note that, with
\Theta
as in (
4.57
), we have

\overline{\Theta}(\overline{\mathbf{K}_{N-s}})=\mathbf{L}_{s}^{-1},\qquad\overline{\Theta}(\overline{\mathbf{E}_{N-s}})=\mathbf{L}_{s}^{-1}\star\mathbf{F}_{s}.

Then from (
4.59
), it follows that

\Delta(\mathbf{L}_{s})=\mathbf{L}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{L}_{s},\qquad\Delta(\mathbf{L}_{s}^{-1}\star\mathbf{F}_{s})=(\mathbf{L}_{s}^{-1}\star\mathbf{F}_{s}\otimes 1)\boxplus(\mathbf{L}_{s}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{L}_{s}^{-1}\star\mathbf{F}_{s}),

and hence

\Delta(\mathbf{F}_{s})=(\mathbf{L}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{L}_{s})\star(\Delta(\mathbf{L}^{-1}\star\mathbf{F}_{s}))=(\mathbf{F}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{L}_{s})\boxplus(1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s}).

∎

4.4.
Modular duality

In this subsection, we briefly explain how modular duality arises in this setting.

Let
(V,\epsilon)
be an
n
-dimensional skew-symmetric space. Let
\pi_{\hbar}
be the standard
\hbar
-representation on
L^{2}(V)
as in Example
1.5
, which we now explicitly index by
\hbar
for differentation. We also label the associated infinitesimal generator by
\hbar
, so
\mathbf{E}_{\hbar}(v)^{it}=\pi_{\hbar}(tv)
for
t\in\mathbb{R},v\in V
.

Let
u_{\hbar}
be the unitary

u_{\hbar}\colon L^{2}(V)\rightarrow L^{2}(V),\qquad(ug)(w)=|\hbar|^{n/2}g(\hbar w),\qquad g\in L^{2}(V),w\in V.

Then we have a normal
*
-isomorphism

(4.61)

\Theta_{\hbar}=\operatorname{Ad}(u_{\hbar})\colon L_{\hbar^{-1}}(V)\cong L_{\hbar}(V),\qquad\mathbf{E}_{1/\hbar}(v)\mapsto u_{\hbar}\mathbf{E}_{1/\hbar}(v)u_{\hbar}^{*}=\mathbf{E}_{\hbar}(v)^{1/\hbar}=\mathbf{E}_{\hbar}(\hbar^{-1}v).

Theorem 4.23
.

Let
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta_{\hbar})
be the Fock–Goncharov von Neumann bialgebra at parameter
\hbar
. Then
\Theta_{\hbar}
as in (
4.61
) provides an isomorphism

(L_{1/\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta_{1/\hbar})\cong(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta_{\hbar}).

Proof..

Note that when writing
\mathbf{v}_{\hbar}=\ln(\mathbf{E}_{\hbar}(v))
, the isomorphism
\Theta_{\hbar}
is simply acting via

\Theta_{\hbar}(\mathbf{v}_{1/\hbar})=\hbar^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{\hbar}.

Consider now the decomposition (
2.69
) for the Fock–Goncharov flip. Then on the one hand, we see that

(\Theta_{\hbar}\otimes\Theta_{\hbar})(\mathcal{K}_{\hbar^{-1}})=G_{1/\hbar}\left(2\hbar^{-2}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-t}\right)=G_{\hbar}\left(2\sum_{t=1}^{n}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-t}\right)=\mathcal{K}_{\hbar}.

On the other hand, from (
A.15
) it follows that

\varphi_{1/\hbar}(z/\hbar)=\varphi_{\hbar}(z),\qquad z\in\mathbb{R},

so for any of the factors
B^{b,r,i}=B_{\hbar}^{b,r,i}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, defined in (
2.62
), we get

(\Theta_{\hbar}\otimes\Theta_{\hbar})(B_{1/{\hbar}}^{b,r,i})=(\Theta_{\hbar}\otimes\Theta_{\hbar})(\varphi_{1/\hbar}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b}))\\
=\varphi_{1/\hbar}(\hbar^{-1}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b}))=\varphi_{\hbar}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{b-i+1,b-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{n-b+i,n-b})=B_{\hbar}^{b,r,i},

hence
(\Theta_{\hbar}\otimes\Theta_{\hbar})(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1/\hbar})=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\hbar}
.

It follows from the above that
(\Theta_{\hbar}\otimes\Theta_{\hbar})(\mathcal{F}_{1/\hbar})=\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}
, and so in particular we get for any
x\in L_{1/\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
that

(\Theta_{\hbar}\otimes\Theta_{\hbar})(\Delta_{1/\hbar}(x))=(\Theta_{\hbar}\otimes\Theta_{\hbar})(\mathcal{F}_{1/\hbar}^{*}(1\otimes x)\mathcal{F}_{1/\hbar})=\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{*}(1\otimes\Theta_{\hbar}(x))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}=\Delta_{\hbar}(\Theta_{\hbar}(x)),

finishing the proof.
∎

5.
Quantized Borel groups as locally compact quantum groups

In this section, we show that the Fock–Goncharov von Neumann bialgebra
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
is a locally compact quantum group, in the sense of
[
KV03
]
.

5.1.
Invariant weights associated to modular multiplicative unitaries

For a brief general overview of weight theory for von Neumann algebras, we refer to Appendix
F
.

Fix
\mathcal{H}
a Hilbert space with associated trace
\mathrm{Tr}
on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
as in (
F.1
). For
\mathbf{T}
a positive operator on
\mathcal{H}
, consider the deformation
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{T}}
as in (
F.4
), so with
\chi_{[0,r]}
the characteristic function of
[0,r]
and

\mathbf{T}_{r}=\mathbf{T}\chi_{[0,r]}(\mathbf{T}),

we have

\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{T}}(x)=\sup_{r>0}\sum_{i}\omega_{\mathbf{T}_{r}^{1/2}e_{i},\mathbf{T}_{r}^{1/2}e_{i}}(x)=\sup_{r>0}\sum_{i}\|x^{1/2}\mathbf{T}_{r}^{1/2}e_{i}\|^{2},\qquad x\geq 0,

with
\{e_{i}\}
any orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
be a multiplicative unitary on
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
, and
(\mathbb{W},\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathbb{W}_{c})
a modular datum. Rewriting (
3.2
), we see that

(5.1)

\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,\mathbb{W}(\eta^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{Q}\xi)\rangle=\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\xi},\overline{\mathbb{W}_{c}}^{*}(\eta^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}\eta})\rangle,\qquad\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{Q}),\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}.

The following is then a variation on
[
Wor03
, Theorem 1.1]
, see also
[
VVD03
]
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{W}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})
be a modular multiplicative unitary with respect to
(\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}})
, with associated von Neumann algebras
M,\hat{M}
as in (
4.1
) and (
4.3
). Let
a\in\hat{M}^{\prime}
. Then the normal weight

\varphi=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{Q}^{2},a}\colon\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{+}\rightarrow[0,\infty],\qquad x\mapsto\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{Q}^{2}}(axa^{*})

is left invariant with respect to

\Delta=\Delta_{\mathbb{W}}\colon\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\bar{\otimes}\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}),\qquad x\mapsto\Delta(x)=\mathbb{W}^{*}(1\otimes x)\mathbb{W},\qquad x\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}),

in the sense that

(5.2)

\varphi((\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta(x))=\varphi(x),\qquad\forall x\in\mathfrak{m}_{\varphi}^{+},\textrm{ all states }\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}^{+}.

Proof..

Assume that
x\in\mathfrak{m}_{\varphi}^{+}
. If then
\xi
is a unit vector in
\mathcal{H}
, it is sufficient to show (
5.2
) with respect to
\omega=\omega_{\xi,\xi}
. Since
x
is the supremum of the directed set of sums of positive rank
1
operators below
x
, it is moreover sufficient to prove (
5.2
) for
x=\theta_{\eta,\eta}
(as in (
0.2
)) with
\|\eta\|=1
and
axa^{*}=\theta_{a\eta,a\eta}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{Q}^{2}}}^{+}
. Note that the latter implies
a\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{Q})
.

Since
\mathbb{W}\in M\bar{\otimes}\hat{M}
, we now compute that indeed, for
\{e_{n}\}
an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
,

\displaystyle\varphi((\omega_{\xi,\xi}\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta_{\mathbb{W}}(x))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{r>0}\sum_{n}(\omega_{\xi,\xi}\otimes\omega_{a^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{r}e_{n},a^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{r}e_{n}})(\mathbb{W}^{*}(1\otimes\theta_{\eta,\eta})\mathbb{W})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{r>0}\sum_{m,n}(\omega_{\xi,\xi}\otimes\omega_{a^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{r}e_{n},a^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{r}e_{n}})(\mathbb{W}^{*}(\theta_{e_{m},e_{m}}\otimes\theta_{\eta,\eta})\mathbb{W})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{r>0}\sum_{m,n}|\langle e_{m}\otimes\eta,\mathbb{W}(\xi\otimes a^{*}\mathbf{Q}_{r}e_{n})\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{r>0}\sum_{m,n}|\langle e_{m}\otimes a\eta,\mathbb{W}(\xi\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{r}e_{n})\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqRightInvOpAlt}}{=}

\displaystyle\sup_{r>0}\sum_{m,n}|\langle e_{m}\otimes\overline{\chi_{[0,r]}(\mathbf{Q})e_{n}},\overline{\mathbb{W}_{c}}^{*}(\xi\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}a\eta})\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{r>0}\|(1\otimes\overline{\chi_{[0,r]}(\mathbf{Q})})\overline{\mathbb{W}_{c}}^{*}(\xi\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}a\eta})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\overline{\mathbb{W}_{c}}^{*}(\xi\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}a\eta})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\xi\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}a\eta}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Q}a\eta\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi(x).

∎

5.2.
Invariant weights on Rieffel deformations

To apply Proposition
5.1
in the setting of the Fock–Goncharov flip, we need some information on weights on twisted group von Neumann algebras of skew-symmetric spaces.

We start with the following well-known observations. Fix
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, and fix
V
a skew-symmetric space with its standard unitary
\hbar
-representation
(L^{2}(\hat{V}),\pi)
and associated von Neumann algebra

L_{\hbar}(V)\subseteq\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\hat{V}))

as in (
4.17
). We consider also the (ordinary) unitary representations of
V
and
\hat{V}
on
L^{2}(\hat{V})
given by

(5.3)

(e_{v}f)(\hat{w})=e^{2\pi iv\cdot\hat{w}}f(\hat{w}),\qquad f\in L^{2}(\hat{V}),v\in V,\hat{w}\in\hat{V},

(5.4)

(\theta_{\hat{v}}f)(\hat{w})=f(\hat{w}-\hat{v}),\qquad f\in L^{2}(\hat{V}),\hat{v},\hat{w}\in\hat{V}.

Note that from (
4.15
) we get

(5.5)

\pi(v)=e_{v}\theta_{-\mathcal{J}v},

with
\mathcal{J}
as in (
4.5
).

Lemma 5.2
.

There exists a unique
\sigma
-weakly continuous action
\alpha
of
\hat{V}
on
L_{\hbar}(V)
such that

(5.6)

\alpha_{\hat{v}}(\pi(v))=e^{-2\pi i\hat{v}\cdot v}\pi(v),\qquad\forall v\in V,\hat{v}\in\hat{V}.

Moreover, this action is ergodic, and for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})
one has

(5.7)

\alpha_{\hat{v}}(f_{\hbar})=(f_{\hat{v}})_{\hbar},\qquad f_{\hat{v}}(\hat{w})=f(\hat{w}-\hat{v}),\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}),\hat{v},\hat{w}\in\hat{V}.

Proof..

With the
\theta_{\hat{v}}
as in (
5.4
), one sees that

x\mapsto\alpha_{\hat{v}}(x)=\theta_{\hat{v}}x\theta_{\hat{v}}^{*}

satisfies (
5.6
), and hence indeed implements a
\hat{V}
-action on
L_{\hbar}(V)
. Using e.g. (
4.13
) and (
4.15
), one finds (
5.7
).

To see that the action is ergodic, consider also the unitaries

\pi^{\prime}(v)=e_{v}\theta_{\mathcal{J}v},\qquad v\in V.

From (
5.5
) one sees that

[\pi(v),\pi^{\prime}(w)]=0,\qquad v,w\in V,

and hence the
\pi^{\prime}(w)
also commute with all
x\in L_{\hbar}(V)
. So, if
x\in L_{\hbar}(V)
and
\alpha_{\hat{v}}(x)=x
for all
\hat{v}\in\hat{V}
, then
x
commutes with all
\theta_{\hat{v}}
and all
e_{v}\theta_{\mathcal{J}v}
, hence with all
\theta_{\hat{v}}
and
e_{v}
. But the latter generate
\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\hat{V}))
as a von Neumann algebra, so
x
must be a scalar.
∎

Recall now the construction and notation from (
F.31
).

Lemma 5.3
.

The weight

\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}:=\varphi_{\alpha}\colon L_{\hbar}(V)_{+}\rightarrow[0,\infty]

associated to
\alpha
is a tracial nsf weight.

Proof..

Faithfulness of
\varphi_{\alpha}
follows from the general theory and construction of
\varphi_{\alpha}
. One can also prove semi-finiteness with general machinery, but let us give a direct approach: first note that if
f,g\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})\subseteq L^{2}(\hat{V})
, then

k_{f,g}\colon v\mapsto\langle f,\pi(v)g\rangle=\int_{\hat{V}}e^{2\pi iv\cdot\hat{w}}g(\hat{w}+\mathcal{J}v)\overline{f(\hat{w})}\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{w}=\int_{V}e^{-2\pi iv\cdot\mathcal{J}w}\hat{g}(w-v)\overline{\hat{f}(w)}\mathrm{d}\!\,w

lies in
\mathcal{S}(V)
. Take then
f,g\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})
with
\|g\|_{2}=1
, and put

h=f^{*}\times_{\hbar}f,\qquad k=k_{g,g}.

Then with
\omega=\omega_{g,g}
, and noting that
k\hat{h}\in\mathcal{S}(V)
, we find

\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}h_{\hbar}=\int_{\hat{V}}\omega(\alpha_{\hat{v}}(h_{\hbar}))\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{v}=\int_{\hat{V}}\int_{V}k(v)\hat{h}(v)e^{-2\pi iv\cdot\hat{v}}\mathrm{d}\!\,v\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{v}=k(0)\hat{h}(0)=\hat{h}(0)=\int_{\hat{V}}h(\hat{v})\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{v}.

In particular,
h_{\hbar}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\varphi_{\alpha}}^{+}
, and since such
h_{\hbar}
generate
L_{\hbar}(V)
we find that
\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}
is semi-finite.

Finally, note that the definition of
\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}
and (
5.6
) immediately give that

\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}\pi(v)x\pi(v)^{*}=\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}x,\qquad x\in L_{\hbar}(V)_{+},v\in V.

This implies that the modular automorphism group of
\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}
vanishes on the
\pi(v)
. But as the latter generate
L_{\hbar}(V)
, it follows that the modular automorphism group of
\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}
is trivial, i.e.
\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}
is a trace.
∎

In particular, we record from the above proof that
f,g\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})
satisfy
f_{\hbar}g_{\hbar}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\varphi_{\alpha}}
with

(5.8)

\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}f_{\hbar}g_{\hbar}=\int_{\hat{V}}f(\hat{v})g(\hat{v})\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{v}.

Lemma 5.4
.

The GNS-space of
\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}
can be uniquely identified with
L^{2}(\hat{V})
in such a way that the associated GNS-map satisfies

(5.9)

\Lambda(f_{\hbar})=f\in L^{2}(\hat{V}),\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}).

Proof..

We use below the notion of a (unimodular) Hilbert algebra with respect to a trace, see
[
Tak03
, Chapter VI]
.

The
*
-algebra
(\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}),\times_{\hbar},*)
forms a Hilbert algebra with respect to the
2
-norm

\|f\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}f_{\hbar}^{*}f_{\hbar}=\int_{\hat{V}}|f(\hat{v})|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{v},

where the last equality is (
5.8
). From e.g.
[
Tak03
, Proposition VIII.3.16]
, one sees that the associated weight on
L_{\hbar}(V)
must equal
\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}
, which is enough to conclude the lemma.
∎

We suspect the following lemma is known, but we do not know a direct reference.

Lemma 5.5
.

Assume
Z\subseteq V
is a subspace, and put

\hat{W}=\mathrm{Ker}(Z)=\{\hat{v}\in\hat{V}\mid\hat{v}_{\mid Z}=0\}\subseteq\hat{V}.

Then for
x\in L_{\hbar}(V)
, one has

(5.10)

L_{\hbar}(Z)=\{x\in L_{\hbar}(V)\mid\alpha_{\hat{w}}(x)=x\textrm{ for all }\hat{w}\in\hat{W}\}.

The proof of this lemma will unfortunately be a bit technical, but it is simply a quantized version of the following well-known fact: we can identify

L^{\infty}(\hat{V}/\hat{W})=\{f\in L^{\infty}(\hat{V})\mid\forall\hat{w}\in\hat{W},\textrm{for almost all }\hat{v}\in\hat{V}:f(\hat{v}+\hat{w})=f(\hat{v})\}.

Indeed, if we now identify
\hat{Z}=\hat{V}/\hat{W}
and consider the Pontryagin dual picture, we get

L(Z)=L^{\infty}(\hat{Z}),\quad L(V)=L^{\infty}(\hat{V}),

which is the classical counterpart to (
5.10
).

Proof..

Write the right hand side of (
5.10
) as
N
. Then clearly
N\subseteq L_{\hbar}(V)
is a von Neumann subalgebra with
L_{\hbar}(Z)\subseteq N
. It is also clear that the
\hat{V}
-action
\alpha
restricts to
N
, where it factorizes over
\hat{Z}=\hat{V}/\hat{W}
. Let us specifically write this action of
\hat{Z}
as
\beta
, which then restricts to the usual
\alpha
-action of
\hat{Z}
on
L_{\hbar}(Z)
.

Obviously the
\hat{Z}
-action
\beta
on
N
is still ergodic. It is also still integrable, as the associated weight

\varphi_{\beta}(x)=\int_{\hat{Z}}\beta_{\hat{z}}(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{z},\qquad x\in U_{+}

restricts to the tracial semi-finite weight
\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar}
on
L_{\hbar}(Z)
, so that all elements of the form

xyz,\qquad y\in N,x,z\in\mathfrak{m}_{\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar}}=(\mathfrak{n}_{\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar}})^{2}=(\mathfrak{n}_{\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar}})^{*}\mathfrak{n}_{\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar}}

are integrable.

The same argument as in the proof of traciality in Lemma
5.5
moreover gives that the modular automorphism
\sigma_{t}^{\varphi_{\beta}}
is trivial on
L_{\hbar}(Z)
. By Takesaki’s Theorem
[
Tak03
, Theorem X.4.2]
it follows that there exists a conditional expectation
E\colon N\rightarrow L_{\hbar}(Z)
such that

\varphi_{\beta}=\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar}\circ E.

This conditional expectation is uniquely determined via

E(x)p=pxp,\qquad x\in N,

for
p
the projection of
L^{2}(N)
onto
L^{2}(\hat{Z})\subseteq L^{2}(N)
, with this isometric inclusion given via

L^{2}(\hat{Z})\subseteq L^{2}(N),\quad f\mapsto\Lambda_{\varphi_{\beta}}(f_{\hbar}),\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{Z}).

To show that
L_{\hbar}(Z)=N
, it suffices now to show that
p=1
. But note that by ergodicity and integrability, one can uniquely define an isometric map

(5.11)

\mathcal{G}\colon L^{2}(N)\otimes L^{2}(N)\rightarrow L^{2}(\hat{Z},L^{2}(N)),\quad\Lambda_{\varphi_{\beta}}(x)\otimes\Lambda_{\varphi_{\beta}}(y)\mapsto\left(\hat{z}\mapsto\Lambda_{\varphi_{\beta}}(\beta_{-\hat{z}}(y)x)\right),\\
\qquad x,y\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi_{\beta}}.

However, using the above identification (
5.9
) for the GNS-map of
(L_{\hbar}(Z),\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar})
, one sees by using

\beta_{\hat{z}}(f_{\hbar})=\alpha_{\hat{z}}(f_{\hbar})=(f_{\hat{z}})_{\hbar},\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{Z}),\hat{z}\in\hat{Z},

that the restriction of
\mathcal{G}
to
L^{2}(N)\otimes L^{2}(\hat{Z})
satisfies

\mathcal{G}(\Lambda_{\varphi_{\beta}}(x)\otimes\Lambda(f_{\hbar}))=\left(\hat{z}\mapsto\Lambda_{\varphi_{\beta}}(((f_{-\hat{z}})_{\hbar})x)\right),\qquad x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi_{\beta}},f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{Z}).

So, conjugating with the Fourier transform
\mathscr{F}
and identifying
L^{2}(N)\otimes L^{2}(Z)\cong L^{2}(Z,L^{2}(N))
, we see that
\hat{\mathcal{G}}=(1\otimes\mathscr{F})\mathcal{G}(1\otimes\mathscr{F}^{*})
gives the unitary transformation

\hat{\mathcal{G}}\colon L^{2}(Z,L^{2}(N))\rightarrow L^{2}(Z,L^{2}(N)),\qquad F\mapsto\left(z\mapsto\pi(z)F(z)\right).

Since
\mathcal{G}
is isometric on
L^{2}(N)\otimes L^{2}(N)
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mid L^{2}(N)\otimes L^{2}(\hat{Z})}
is unitary onto the range of
\mathcal{G}
, this implies that indeed
L^{2}(N)=L^{2}(\hat{Z})
, finishing the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
Z\subseteq V
be a vector subspace, and put
\hat{W}=\mathrm{Ker}(Z)\subseteq\hat{V}
. There exists a unique nsf operator-valued weight

(5.12)

\mathscr{E}^{\hbar}_{\hat{W}}\colon L_{\hbar}(V)_{+}\rightarrow L_{\hbar}(Z)_{+}^{\mathrm{ext}}

such that, for any normal state
\omega
on
L_{\hbar}(V)
, it holds that

\omega_{\mid L_{\hbar}(Z)}\left(\mathscr{E}^{\hbar}_{\hat{W}}(x)\right)=\int_{\hat{W}}\omega(\alpha_{\hat{w}}(x))\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{w},\qquad x\in L_{\hbar}(V)_{+}.

Moreover, with the Lebesgue measure on
\hat{Z}
suitably normalized, it holds that

\int^{\hbar}_{\hat{V}}=\int^{\hbar}_{\hat{Z}}\circ\,\mathscr{E}^{\hbar}_{\hat{W}}.

Proof..

Considering
L_{\hbar}(V)
with the restricted
\hat{W}
-action
\alpha_{\mid\hat{W}}
, we simply define

\mathscr{E}_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}=T_{\alpha_{\hat{W}}}

as in (
F.30
), which by Lemma
5.5
is indeed an operator valued weight onto

L_{\hbar}(V)^{\alpha_{\mid\hat{W}}}=L_{\hbar}(Z).

Normalize now the Lebesgue measure on
\hat{Z}
such that for positive
f\in C_{c}(\hat{V})
we have

\int_{\hat{Z}}E_{\hat{W}}(f)(\hat{z})\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{z}=\int_{\hat{V}}f(\hat{v})\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{v},\qquad\textrm{where }E_{\hat{W}}(f)(\hat{v}+\hat{W})=\int_{\hat{W}}f(\hat{v}+\hat{w})\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{w}.

Choose any linear splitting
s\colon\hat{Z}\rightarrow\hat{V}
of the quotient map
\hat{V}\twoheadrightarrow\hat{Z}
. Noting that
\alpha_{s(\hat{z})}
restricts to
\alpha_{z}
on
L_{\hbar}(Z)\subseteq L_{\hbar}(V)
, we then find for
x\in L_{\hbar}(V)_{+}
and
\omega\in L_{\hbar}(V)_{*}^{+}
a state:

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar}\left(\mathscr{E}_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{Z}}(\omega\circ\alpha_{\hat{z}})\left(\mathscr{E}_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{Z}}\int_{\hat{W}}(\omega\circ\alpha_{s(\hat{z})}\circ\alpha_{\hat{w}})(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{w}\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{z}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{V}}(\omega\circ\alpha_{\hat{v}})(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}x.

∎

Remark 5.7
.

If
W\subseteq V
is any vector space complementary to
Z
, so
V=Z\oplus W
, we easily see that

\mathscr{E}_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}(x)=\left(\int_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}x\right)1,\qquad x\in L_{\hbar}(W)_{+}\subseteq L_{\hbar}(V)_{+},

where we consider
\hat{W}
as the dual space of
W
upon restriction.

In the following, we will want to further perturb the weight
\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}
. Namely, if
v\in V
, we can form the nsf weight

\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(v)}=\varphi_{\alpha,\mathbf{E}(v)}

as defined through (
F.4
). This is meaningful as indeed
\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}
is an nsf tracial weight and
\mathbf{E}(v)
is affiliated to
L_{\hbar}(V)
.

Recall now from Lemma
4.4
that
L_{\hbar}(V)
is a type
I
-factor if
V
is a symplectic. Let
\mathrm{Tr}
be the trace on
L_{\hbar}(V)
, normalized so that minimal projections have trace
1
. By uniqueness of the trace, we must have (up to suitable normalisation)

\mathrm{Tr}=\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}.

The following is the key result that we need.

Lemma 5.8
.

Assume
V
is symplectic. Let
Z\subseteq V
, put
\hat{W}=\mathrm{Ker}(Z)\subseteq\hat{V}
, and choose a subspace
W\subseteq V
such that

V=Z\oplus W

as vector spaces. Assume
z\in Z,w\in W
. Then there exists
a\in L_{\hbar}(W)\subseteq L_{\hbar}(V)
such that

(5.13)

\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(z)}=\left(\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{E}(z+w)}(a-a^{*})\right)_{\mid L_{\hbar}(Z)_{+}}.

Proof..

In the following, we again identify
\hat{W}
with the dual of
W
by the restriction map.

Consider the
\sigma
-weakly continuous one-parameter group of automorphisms

\gamma_{t}\colon L_{\hbar}(W)\rightarrow L_{\hbar}(W),\qquad x\mapsto\mathbf{E}(z)^{it}x\mathbf{E}(z)^{-it}.

This is clearly well-defined, and scales the weight
\int_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(w)}
:

\int_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(w)}\circ\gamma_{t}=e^{2\pi\hbar t(w,z)}\int_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(w)}.

Taking a non-zero positive
c\in\mathfrak{n}_{\int_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}}
and putting

b=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-t^{2}/2}\gamma_{t}(\widetilde{c})\mathrm{d}\!\,t,\qquad\widetilde{c}=\chi_{[0,1]}(\mathbf{E}(w))c\chi_{[0,1]}(\mathbf{E}(w)),

we see that we can find a non-zero
b\in L_{\hbar}(W)
which is square
\int_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(w)}
-integrable, with
\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}b
bounded, and with
b
and
\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}b
analytic for
\gamma_{t}
. Normalize
b
such that

(5.14)

\int_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(w)}bb^{*}=1,

and put then
