a=\gamma_{i/2}(b)\in L_{\hbar}(W).

Then also
\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}a
is bounded, and
\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}a
is analytic for
\gamma_{t}
.

Now for
y\in L_{\hbar}(V)
and
\mathbf{T}\in L_{\hbar}(V)_{+}^{\mathrm{ext}}
, let us write
y^{*}\cdot\mathbf{T}\cdot y\in L_{\hbar}(V)_{+}^{\mathrm{ext}}
for the element

\omega(y^{*}\cdot\mathbf{T}\cdot y)=\omega(y^{*}-y)(\mathbf{T}),\qquad\omega\in M_{*}^{+}.

We then claim that

(5.15)

\mathscr{E}_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}(a^{*}\cdot\mathbf{E}(z+w)\cdot a)=\mathbf{E}(z).

Indeed, take
\xi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{E}(z)^{1/2})\subseteq L^{2}(\hat{V})
. Then we compute, using the notation (
5.4
), that

(5.16)

\displaystyle\omega_{\xi,\xi}(\mathbf{E}(z))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{E}(z)^{1/2}\xi\|^{2}

(5.17)

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqNormb}}{=}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(w)}bb^{*}\right)\|\mathbf{E}(z)^{1/2}\xi\|^{2}

(5.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}(\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}b)^{*}(\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}b)\right)\|\mathbf{E}(z)^{1/2}\xi\|^{2}

(5.19)

\displaystyle\underset{\textrm{Remark }\ref{RemWeight}}{=}

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathbf{E}(z)^{1/2}\xi,\mathbf{E}(z)^{1/2}\xi}\left(\mathscr{E}_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}\left((\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}b)^{*}(\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}b)\right)\right)

(5.20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{W}}\|\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}b\theta_{\hat{w}}^{*}\mathbf{E}(z)^{1/2}\xi\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{w}

(5.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{W}}\|\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}b\mathbf{E}(z)^{1/2}\theta_{\hat{w}}^{*}\xi\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{w}

(5.22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{W}}\|\mathbf{E}(w)^{1/2}\mathbf{E}(z)^{1/2}a\theta_{\hat{w}}^{*}\xi\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{w}

(5.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{W}}\|\mathbf{E}(z+w)^{1/2}a\theta_{\hat{w}}^{*}\xi\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{w}

(5.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\xi,\xi}\left(\int_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}a^{*}\cdot\mathbf{E}(z+w)\cdot a\right).

Writing the left hand side of (
5.15
) as
\mathbf{T}
, this shows by (
F.5
) that
\mathbf{E}(z)^{1/2}\subseteq\mathbf{T}^{1/2}
, which by self-adjointness of both sides is sufficient to conclude that they are equal. Hence equality holds in (
5.15
).

Choose now
x\in L_{\hbar}(Z)
. Then we compute that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{E}(z+w)}(axx^{*}a^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{V}}^{\hbar}x^{*}a^{*}\cdot\mathbf{E}(z+w)\cdot ax

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar}\mathscr{E}_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}\left(x^{*}\cdot(a^{*}\cdot\mathbf{E}(z+w)\cdot a)\cdot x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar}x^{*}\cdot\left(\mathscr{E}_{\hat{W}}^{\hbar}\left(a^{*}\cdot\mathbf{E}(z+w)\cdot a\right)\right)\cdot x

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqEqualPosOp}}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar}x^{*}\cdot\mathbf{E}(z)\cdot x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{Z}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(z)}xx^{*}.

∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Assume
V
is symplectic, and assume

V=Z\oplus W

as vector spaces. Let
v\in V
. Then there exists
a\in L_{\hbar}(W)\subseteq L_{\hbar}(V)
such that the restriction of

\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{E}(v),a}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{E}(v)}(a-a^{*})

to
L_{\hbar}(Z)
is an nsf weight.

Remark 5.10
.

If we denote

\varphi=(\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{E}(v),a})_{\mid L_{\hbar}(Z)},

then clearly the restriction of the GNS-map
\Lambda
of
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{E}(v),a}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
is a GNS-map for
\varphi
, giving an embedding

L^{2}(L_{\hbar}(Z),\varphi)\subseteq L^{2}(L_{\hbar}(V),\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{E}(v),a}),\qquad\Lambda(x)\mapsto\Lambda(x),\qquad x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},

which intertwines the standard
L_{\hbar}(Z)
-bimodule structures (cf.
[
Tak02
, Theorem IX.4.2]
).

5.3.
Unitary corepresentations

In this section, we revisit the notion of modularity in the following setting.

Definition 5.11
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
be a multiplicative unitary, represented on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. A
unitary corepresentation
for
\mathbb{W}
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{G}
is a unitary
\mathbb{U}
on
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{G}
such that

(5.25)

\mathbb{U}_{23}\mathbb{W}_{12}=\mathbb{W}_{12}\mathbb{U}_{13}\mathbb{U}_{23}.

Note that if
\mathbb{W}
is modular, with associated von Neumann bialgebra
(M,\Delta)
, then slicing (
5.25
) on the second and third leg reveals that

\mathbb{U}\in M\bar{\otimes}\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{G}),

and (
5.25
) can then be rewritten as

(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathbb{U}=\mathbb{U}_{13}\mathbb{U}_{23}.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
\mathbb{U}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{G})
be a unitary corepresentation for a multiplicative unitary
\mathbb{W}
. Assume that there exists a unitary operator
\mathbb{U}^{c}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{G}})
and a strictly positive operator
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
on
\mathcal{G}
such that

(5.26)

(\mathbb{U}_{21}^{*},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathbb{U}_{21}^{c})

is a weakly modular datum. Then also
\mathbb{U}^{c}
is a unitary corepresentation.

Proof..

The modularity condition can be rewritten as

(5.27)

\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,\mathbb{U}(\eta^{\prime}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi)\rangle=\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\xi},\mathbb{U}^{c,*}(\eta^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})\rangle,\qquad\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}),\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}.

Let then
\{e_{i}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
. On the one hand, we have for
\xi_{i}^{\prime},\eta_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
and
\xi,\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}})\subseteq\mathcal{G}
that

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\langle\xi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\xi_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\eta,\mathbb{W}_{12}^{*}\mathbb{U}_{23}\mathbb{W}_{12}(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(\mathbb{W}(\xi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\xi_{2}^{\prime}))\otimes\eta,\mathbb{U}_{23}(\mathbb{W}(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}^{\prime}))\otimes\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\langle(\omega_{e_{i},\xi_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathbb{W})\xi_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\eta,\mathbb{U}((\omega_{e_{i},\eta_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathbb{W})\eta_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi)\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\langle(\omega_{e_{i},\xi_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathbb{W})\xi_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\xi},\mathbb{U}^{c,*}((\omega_{e_{i},\eta_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathbb{W})\eta_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\xi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\xi_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\xi},\mathbb{W}_{12}^{*}\mathbb{U}_{23}^{c,*}\mathbb{W}_{12}(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})\rangle.

On the other hand, choosing now an orthonormal basis
\{e_{k}\}
of
\mathcal{G}
and putting
p_{r}=\chi_{(r,r^{-1})}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}})
for
r>0
, we have that

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\langle\xi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\xi_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\eta,\mathbb{U}_{13}(1\otimes 1\otimes p_{r})\mathbb{U}_{23}(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi)\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k}\langle\xi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\xi_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\eta,\mathbb{U}_{13}e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes p_{r}e_{k}\rangle\langle e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes p_{r}e_{k},\mathbb{U}_{23}(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi)\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k}\langle\xi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\xi_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}p_{r}e_{k}},\mathbb{U}_{13}^{c,*}e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}\rangle\langle e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes\overline{\xi},\mathbb{U}_{23}^{c,*}(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}p_{r}e_{k}})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\langle\xi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\xi_{2}^{\prime},(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega_{\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}p_{r}e_{k}},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}})(\mathbb{U}^{c,*})\eta_{1}^{\prime}\otimes(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega_{\overline{\xi},\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}p_{r}e_{k}}})(\mathbb{U}^{c,*})\eta_{2}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\langle\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}p_{r}e_{k}},(\omega_{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathbb{U}^{c,*})\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}\rangle\langle\overline{\xi},(\omega_{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathbb{U}^{c,*})\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}p_{r}e_{k}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\overline{\xi},(\omega_{\xi_{2}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathbb{U}^{c,*})p_{r}(\omega_{\xi_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{1}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathbb{U}^{c,*})\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\xi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\xi_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\xi},\mathbb{U}_{23}^{c,*}(1\otimes 1\otimes p_{r})\mathbb{U}_{13}^{c,*}(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})\rangle.

Taking the limit as
r\rightarrow 0
, this gives

\langle\xi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\xi_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\eta,\mathbb{U}_{13}\mathbb{U}_{23}(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\xi)\rangle=\langle\xi_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\xi_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\xi},\mathbb{U}_{23}^{c,*}\mathbb{U}_{13}^{c,*}(\eta_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\eta_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})\rangle.

Comparing the above two expressions and using the corepresentation property of
\mathbb{U}
, we find

\mathbb{W}_{12}^{*}\mathbb{U}_{23}^{c,*}\mathbb{W}_{12}=\mathbb{U}_{23}^{c,*}\mathbb{U}_{13}^{c,*},

i.e.
\mathbb{U}^{c}
is a unitary corepresentation.
∎

We will apply this as follows. Consider
\mathcal{F}
the Fock–Goncharov flip in the faithfully normal representation of
L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})
as constructed in (
3.2
). Let
\hat{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{F}_{21}^{*}
be its dual. Then
\hat{\mathcal{F}}
can be viewed as a unitary corepresentation with respect to itself.

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Fock–Goncharov flip, and let
\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{G}
be as in Theorem
4.10
. Then
\mathcal{G}_{21}
is a unitary corepresentation for
\hat{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof..

This follows immediately from Proposition
5.12
, the fact that
\hat{\mathcal{F}}
is a unitary corepresentation for
\hat{\mathcal{F}}
and the fact that
(\mathcal{F},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{G})
forms a weakly modular datum.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

Alternatively, one can show that if
\mathbb{W}
is modular, the unitary
\mathbb{U}^{c}
in (
5.12
) coincides with the contragredient of
\mathbb{U}
as defined in
[
SW07
, Definition 11]
, from which Corollary
5.13
then also immediately follows.

5.4.
Locally compact quantum group associated to the Fock–Goncharov flip

We are now ready for the main result of this paper.

We first make the following observation: we have an isomorphism of skew-symmetric spaces

(5.28)

\vartheta\colon\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}\cong\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}},\quad e_{a,b,c}\mapsto\overline{-e_{c,b,a}}.

This gives an associated
*
-isomorphism

(5.29)

\varTheta\colon L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})\rightarrow\overline{L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})},\qquad\mathbf{E}(v)\mapsto\mathbf{E}(\vartheta(v)).

Lemma 5.15
.

The map
\varTheta
restricts to an isomorphism

(5.30)

(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}),\hat{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})\cong(\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})},\overline{\hat{\Delta}}).

Proof..

With
\mathcal{F}
the standard Fock–Goncharov flip and
\mathcal{G}
as in Theorem
3.11
, we easily verify that

(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varTheta)\mathcal{G}=\overline{\mathcal{F}}.

Hence

\hat{\mathbb{V}}:=(\varTheta^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathcal{G}=(\varTheta^{-1}\otimes\varTheta^{-1})(\overline{\mathcal{F}})

is also a multiplicative unitary.

On the other hand, it follows from Corollary
5.13
that
\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{21}
is a unitary
\hat{\mathcal{F}}
-corepresentation, so

\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{12}^{*}\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{32}\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{12}=\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{31}\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{32},\qquad\textrm{i.e.}\qquad\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{23}^{*}\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{13}\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{23}=\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{12}\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{13}.

It now follows that

\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{23}\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{12}\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{23}^{*}=\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{12}\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{13}=\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{23}^{*}\hat{\mathbb{V}}_{13}\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{23}.

Since the second leg of
\hat{\mathbb{V}}
equals
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})
, we find

\hat{\Delta}(x)=\hat{\mathbb{V}}(x\otimes 1)\hat{\mathbb{V}}^{*}=\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{*}(1\otimes x)\hat{\mathcal{F}},\qquad x\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}).

Now
\varTheta\colon L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})\cong\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})}
, and we compute

\hat{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}(x)=\Sigma\hat{\mathbb{V}}(x\otimes 1)\hat{\mathbb{V}}^{*}\Sigma=(\varTheta^{-1}\otimes\varTheta^{-1})(\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{21}(1\otimes\varTheta(x))\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{21}^{*})\\
=(\varTheta^{-1}\otimes\varTheta^{-1}))(\overline{\hat{\mathcal{F}}}^{*}(1\otimes\varTheta(x))\overline{\hat{\mathcal{F}}}),\qquad x\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}).

This proves the lemma.
∎

We recall now the notion of a locally compact quantum group
[
KV00
,
KV03
]
.

Definition 5.16
.

A
locally compact quantum group
consists of a von Neumann bialgebra
(M,\Delta)
for which there exists a left invariant nsf weight
\varphi
and a right invariant nsf weight
\psi
:

(5.31)

\varphi((\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta(x))=\varphi(x),\qquad\forall x\in\mathfrak{m}_{\varphi}^{+},\textrm{ all states }\omega\in M_{*}^{+},

(5.32)

\psi((\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\Delta(x))=\psi(x),\qquad\forall x\in\mathfrak{m}_{\psi}^{+},\textrm{ all states }\omega\in M_{*}^{+}.

These left and right invariant nsf weights are then unique up to multiplication with a positive scalar.

Theorem 5.17
.

The Fock–Goncharov von Neumann bialgebra
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
is a locally compact quantum group.

Proof..

Put

V=D_{N},\qquad Z=B_{N}^{-}

as in Corollary
2.7
. Then by Lemma
2.6
, we have that we can pick a complementary subspace
W\subseteq D_{N}
to
Z
such that

(5.33)

V=Z\oplus W,\qquad(w,b)=0,\qquad\forall b\in B_{N}^{+}.

In particular,
L_{\hbar}(W)
commutes pointwise with
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})
.

Implement
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
via a Fock–Goncharov flip with respect to an irreducible normal
*
-representation of
L_{\hbar}(D_{N})
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
as in Corollary
4.13
. Then since we can identify
L_{\hbar}(D_{N})=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, we are in the setting of Proposition
5.1
, and combined with Corollary
5.9
applied with respect to (
5.33
), we find that
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
admits a left invariant nsf weight.

By the self-duality (
4.59
), also
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}),\hat{\Delta})
then has a left invariant nsf weight. But by Lemma
5.15
, this means that
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}),\hat{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})
has a left invariant nsf weight, i.e.
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}),\hat{\Delta})
has a right invariant nsf weight. Again by self-duality, it follows that
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
has a right invariant nsf weight, and we are done.
∎

6.
Modular structure of

(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)

We resume the setting and notations of the previous sections.

Theorem
5.17
shows that the Fock–Goncharov von Neumann bialgebra
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
is a locally compact quantum group. Following common practice in quantum group theory, we designate this locally compact quantum group with a symbol such that the above von Neumann algebra is its
function algebra
, and the
group von Neumann algebra
of its dual. Given the duality between
B_{N}^{-}
and
B_{N}^{+}
, this motivates the notation

(6.1)

(L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{+}),\Delta):=(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta),

and likewise for the Pontryagin dual:

(6.2)

(L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-}),\hat{\Delta}):=(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}),\hat{\Delta}).

Now by the general theory of
[
KV00
,
KV03
]
, the locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{+}
comes with a variety of associated
modular data
, that we will determine in this section.

6.1.
Left Haar measure for
\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{+}
.

We start off by determining more explicitly the left invariant nsf weight on
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
.

Write
\hat{B}_{N}^{-}=\widehat{B_{N}^{-}}
for the dual vector space. By Lemma
5.4
, we can uniquely identify

L^{2}(M)\cong L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})

in such a way that, with
\Lambda
the GNS-map associated to
\int_{\hat{B}_{N}^{-}}^{\hbar}
, we have

(6.3)

\Lambda(f_{\hbar})=f\in L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-}),\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-}).

The associated standard representation of
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
is then uniquely determined by

f_{\hbar}g=f\times_{\hbar}g,\qquad f,g\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-}),

which has associated unitary
\hbar
-representation of
B_{N}^{-}
given by (
4.15
) (or equivalently (
5.5
)). As
\int_{\hat{B}_{N}^{-}}^{\hbar}
is tracial, we also know that the modular conjugation of
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
with respect to this standard representation is given by

(6.4)

Jg=\overline{g},\qquad g\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-}).

Now by Corollary
4.13
, Proposition
5.1
and Lemma
5.8
, we have that
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
has a left invariant nsf weight

\varphi=\int_{\hat{B}_{N}^{-}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(2d_{l})},

for a particular
d_{l}\in B_{N}^{-}
. More precisely, by these results, together with the fact that

\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{K}_{\delta}^{-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})}=\mathbf{E}(-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\sum_{i}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i}),

we can take any
d_{l}\in B_{N}^{-}
such that there exists
w\in\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
, vanishing on
B_{N}^{+}
, with

2d_{l}+w=-2(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\sum_{i}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i}.

By Lemma
2.6
,
d_{l}
must then be the unique element in
B_{N}^{-}
such that

(6.5)

(d_{l},z)=-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i},z),\qquad\forall z\in B_{N}^{+}.

Lemma 6.1
.

We have

2d_{l}=(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\sum_{(a,b,c)\in\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{N}}ac\,e_{a,b,c}=(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\underset{0\leq k<s}{\sum_{1\leq s\leq n}}(N-s)\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}.

Proof..

Using (
6.5
), it follows by a direct computation from Lemma
2.3
.
∎

So, summarizing, we have:

Theorem 6.2
.

The left invariant nsf weight for
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
is

(6.6)

\varphi=\int_{\hat{B}_{N}^{-}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(2d_{l})},\qquad\mathbf{E}(2d_{l})=\mathbf{E}(\underset{0\leq k<s}{\sum_{1\leq s\leq n}}(N-s)\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k})^{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}.

Now by general theory
[
PT73
,
Vae01
]
, we can identify also

(6.7)

L^{2}(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\varphi)\cong L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})

by means of the unique GNS-map

\Lambda_{\varphi}\colon\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}\rightarrow L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})

such that

\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)=\Lambda(x\mathbf{E}(d_{l})),\qquad x\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})\textrm{ and }x\mathbf{E}(d_{l})\textrm{ bounded with closure in }\mathfrak{n}_{\int_{\hat{B}_{N}^{-}}^{\hbar}}.

Corollary 6.3
.

In the above GNS-representation, the modular operator of the weight
\varphi
in (
6.6
) is given by

\nabla_{\varphi}=\mathbf{E}(2d_{l})J\mathbf{E}(2d_{l})^{-1}J,\qquad\nabla_{\varphi}^{it}=\mathbf{E}(2d_{l})^{it}J\mathbf{E}(2d_{l})^{it}J.

6.2.
Right Haar measure for
\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{+}
.

We now determine explicitly the right invariant nsf weight for
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
.

First note that the maps

\theta
and
\Theta
in (
4.58
) and (
4.59
) lead to isomorphisms

(6.8)

\Theta\colon(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}),\hat{\Delta})\cong(\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})},\overline{\Delta}),\qquad\Theta\colon(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)\cong(\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})},\overline{\hat{\Delta}}).

On the other hand, by Lemma
5.15
we have that the maps
\vartheta
and
\varTheta
in (
5.28
) and (
5.29
) lead to an isomorphism

(6.9)

\varTheta\colon(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}),\hat{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}})\cong(\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})},\overline{\hat{\Delta}}).

By composition, (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) lead to

\upsilon=\theta\vartheta^{-1}\theta\colon\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}\cong\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}},\quad e_{a,b,c}\mapsto\overline{-e_{a,c,b}},\qquad\Upsilon\colon L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})\cong\overline{L_{\hbar}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})},\qquad\mathbf{E}(v)\mapsto\mathbf{E}(\upsilon(v))

inducing an isomorphism

\Upsilon\colon(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})\cong(\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})},\overline{\Delta}).

Now

\overline{\int_{\hat{B}_{N}^{-}}^{\hbar}}\circ\Upsilon=\int_{\hat{B}_{N}^{-}}^{\hbar},

so since

\upsilon^{-1}(\overline{d_{l}})=d_{r},\qquad 2d_{r}=-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\sum_{(a,b,c)\in\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{C}_{N}}ab\,e_{a,b,c}=-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\underset{0\leq k<s}{\sum_{1\leq s\leq n}}(N-s)\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k},

we find:

Theorem 6.4
.

The right invariant nsf weight for
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
is

\psi=\int_{\hat{B}_{N}^{-}}^{\hbar,\mathbf{E}(2d_{r})},\qquad\mathbf{E}(2d_{r})=\mathbf{E}(\underset{0\leq k<s}{\sum_{1\leq s\leq n}}(N-s)\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k})^{-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})}.

6.3.
Modular element for
\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{+}

If
(M,\Delta)
is a locally compact quantum group with left and right nsf weights
\varphi,\psi
, there exists a unique strictly positive operator
\delta\,\eta\,M
(i.e.
\delta^{it}\in M
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
) which is
group-like
, i.e.

\Delta(\delta)=\delta\otimes\delta,\qquad\Delta(\delta^{it})=\delta^{it}\otimes\delta^{it},

and such that, possibly rescaling
\psi
, we have

\psi=\varphi_{\delta},

where
\varphi_{\delta}
is the unique nsf weight such that

(6.10)

\varphi_{\delta}(x^{*}x)=\varphi(\delta^{1/2}x^{*}x\delta^{1/2}),\qquad\forall x\in M\textrm{ with }x\circ\delta^{1/2}\textrm{ bounded and with closure }x\delta^{1/2}\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}.

One calls
\delta
the
modular element
of
(M,\Delta)
.

Lemma 6.5
.

The elements
\mathbf{E}(v)
for
v\in T_{N}^{-}
are group-like.

Proof..

Fix
v\in T_{N}^{-}
. We have to compute

\Delta(\mathbf{E}(v)^{it})=\mathcal{F}^{*}(1\otimes\mathbf{E}(v)^{it})\mathcal{F}.

But using the expression (
2.73
) and noting that

(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k},v)=0,\qquad 0\leq k<s\leq n

by (
2.12
) and (
2.14
), we find by (
1.17
) that indeed

\Delta(\mathbf{E}(v)^{it})=\mathcal{K}^{*}\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime*}(1\otimes\mathbf{E}(v)^{it})\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{*}(1\otimes\mathbf{E}(v)^{it})\mathcal{K}=\mathbf{E}(v)^{it}\otimes\mathbf{E}(v)^{it}

as claimed.
∎

Theorem 6.6
.

The modular element of
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
equals

(6.11)

\delta=\mathbf{L}_{\delta}^{-2(1+|\hbar|^{-1})},\qquad\mathbf{L}_{\delta}=\mathbf{E}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i}).

Proof..

This follows directly from Theorem
6.2
and Theorem
6.4
, together with the fact that

2d_{r}-2d_{l}=-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\underset{0\leq k<s}{\sum_{1\leq s\leq n}}(N-s)(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}+\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k})=-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\sum_{1\leq s\leq n}(N-s)s\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s}\\
=-2(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i},

and the fact that
\mathbf{L}_{\delta}
is indeed group-like by Lemma
6.5
.
∎

Note that with
\varphi
and
\psi
scaled as in Theorem
6.2
and Theorem
6.4
, we then indeed get

\psi=\varphi_{\delta}.

6.4.
Scaling constant of
\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{+}

If
(M,\Delta)
is a locally compact quantum group with left invariant nsf weight
\varphi
, then associated to
\varphi
one has the modular automorphism group
(\sigma_{t}^{\varphi})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}
of
M
, determined by

\sigma_{t}^{\varphi}(x)=\nabla_{\varphi}^{it}x\nabla_{\varphi}^{-it},\qquad x\in M.

There then exists a unique number
\nu>0
, called the
scaling constant
of
(M,\Delta)
, such that if
\delta
is the modular element of
(M,\Delta)
, we have

\sigma_{t}^{\varphi}(\delta)=\nu^{t}\delta

(see e.g.
[
KV03
, Proposition 2.13.(18)]
).

Now in the case of
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
, we immediately see that

\sigma_{t}^{\varphi}(x)=\nabla_{\varphi}^{it}x\nabla_{\varphi}^{-it}=\mathbf{E}(2d_{l})^{it}x\mathbf{E}(2d_{l})^{-it},\qquad x\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),t\in\mathbb{R}.

Together with the concrete expression (
6.11
) and the simple computation

2\underset{0\leq k<s}{\sum_{1\leq s,t\leq n}}(N-s)(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k},\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t})=\underset{0\leq k<s}{\sum_{1\leq s\leq n}}(N-s)=\sum_{1\leq s\leq n}(N-s)s=\tau_{n},

with
\tau_{n}
the
n
-th tetrahedral number

\tau_{n}=\binom{n+2}{3},

this then leads to:

Theorem 6.7
.

The scaling constant of
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
equals

\nu=e^{-2\pi\beta_{\hbar}\tau_{n}},\qquad\beta_{\hbar}=\operatorname{sgn}(\hbar)(|\hbar|^{1/2}+|\hbar|^{-1/2})^{2}.

Remark 6.8
.

This shows in particular that
\mathbb{B}_{N}^{+}
cannot be a
*
-algebraic quantum group as in
[
VD98
]
, as the latter have scaling constant equal to
1

[
DCVD10
, Theorem 3.4]
.

6.5.
Antipode

Let
\mathbb{W}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})
be a modular multiplicative unitary with associated modular datum
(\mathbb{W},\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\hat{\mathbb{W}})
. Let
(M,\Delta)
be its associated von Neumann bialgebra. Then by
[
SW01
, Theorem 2.3]
(applied to the dual of
\mathbb{W}
), the Banach space

(6.12)

A=A_{\mathbb{W}}=[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{W}\mid\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}]

is a C
∗
-algebra, to which
\Delta_{\mathbb{W}}
restricts as a non-degenerate
*
-homomorphism

\Delta_{\mathbb{W}}\colon A\rightarrow M(A\otimes A).

There then exists a unique closed unbounded operator

S\colon\mathscr{D}(S)\subseteq A\rightarrow A

such that
\{(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{W}\mid\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}\}
is a core for
S
and satisfies there

(6.13)

S((\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{W})=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)(\mathbb{W}^{*}),\qquad\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}).

Moreover, by this same theorem there then exists a unique involutive, anti-multiplicative automorphism

R\colon M\rightarrow M

the
unitary antipode
, and a
\sigma
-weakly continuous one-parameter group of automorphisms

(6.14)

\tau_{t}\colon M\rightarrow M,\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},

called the
scaling group
, such that
\tau_{t}
is norm-continuous on
A
, and such that on
A
we have the equality

S=R\circ\tau_{-i/2},

where
\tau_{-i/2}
has as domain all
a\in A
for which
t\mapsto\tau_{t}(a)
has a norm-continuous extension to the halfstrip
\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid-i/2\leq\mathrm{Im}(z)\leq 0\}
, and is holomorphic on the interior of that strip. The scaling group is then implemented concretely through

\tau_{t}(x)=\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{2it}x\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-2it},\qquad x\in M,t\in\mathbb{R}.

The following theorem tells us that the above data only depends on
(M,\Delta)
. It is a small variation of
[
SW07
, Theorem 5]
.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
\mathbb{W},\mathbb{V}
be modular multiplicative unitaries giving rise to isomorphic von Neumann bialgebras

(6.15)

(M_{\mathbb{W}},\Delta_{\mathbb{W}})\underset{\theta}{\cong}(M_{\mathbb{V}},\Delta_{\mathbb{V}}).

Then
\theta
intertwines the respective unitary antipode and scaling group.

Proof..

For the first part of the theorem, it is enough to show that
\theta
intertwines the C
∗
-algebra
A_{\mathbb{W}}
in (
6.12
) with the C
∗
-algebra
A_{\mathbb{V}}
, for one can then simply apply
[
SW07
, Theorem 5]
.

Now since any isomorphism of von Neumann algebras can always be implemented through unitary conjugation, upon tensoring their defining normal representation with a multiplicity Hilbert space, we may assume that we are simply given a von Neumann bialgebra
(M,\Delta)
with
M\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, and two modular multiplicative unitaries

\mathbb{W},\mathbb{V}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H})

such that

M=[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{W}\mid\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}]^{\sigma\textrm{-weak}}=[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{V}\mid\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}]^{\sigma\textrm{-weak}},

and such that

\Delta(x)=\mathbb{W}^{*}(1\otimes x)\mathbb{W}=\mathbb{V}^{*}(1\otimes x)\mathbb{V},\qquad x\in M.

Now
\mathbb{V}\in M\bar{\otimes}\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a unitary corepresentation for
(M,\Delta)
, in the sense that

(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathbb{V}=\mathbb{V}_{13}\mathbb{V}_{23}.

But since
\Delta=\Delta_{\mathbb{W}}
, this entails

\mathbb{V}_{13}=\mathbb{W}_{12}^{*}\mathbb{V}_{23}\mathbb{W}_{12}\mathbb{V}_{23},

and so from
\mathbb{W}\in M(A_{\mathbb{W}}\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}))
(
[
SW01
, Theorem 2.3]
applied to
\hat{\mathbb{W}}=\mathbb{W}_{21}^{*}
) we see that
\mathbb{V}\in M(A_{\mathbb{W}}\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}))
. So
\mathbb{V}
is also a representation of
(A_{\mathbb{W}},\Delta_{\mathbb{W}})
in the sense of
[
SW07
]
, and moreover it is right absorbing, in the sense that for any other unitary corepresentation
U
of
(M_{\mathbb{W}},\Delta_{\mathbb{W}})
we have a unitary conjugation

\mathbb{V}_{13}U_{23}\cong\mathbb{V}_{13},

using the identity

\mathbb{V}_{13}U_{23}=U_{32}\mathbb{V}_{13}U_{32}^{*},

which follows immediately from the fact that
U
is a unitary corepresentation and
\Delta=\Delta_{\mathbb{V}}
.

It then follows from the arguments at the beginning of
[
SW07
, Section 4]
that there exists a normal isomorphism

\hat{\theta}\colon\hat{M}_{\mathbb{W}}\rightarrow\hat{M}_{\mathbb{V}},\qquad(\mathrm{id}\otimes\hat{\theta})\mathbb{V}=\mathbb{W}.

But this implies immediately

A_{\mathbb{W}}=[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{W}\mid\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}]=[(\mathrm{id}\otimes\omega)\mathbb{V}\mid\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}]=A_{\mathbb{V}}.

∎

Now if
(M,\Delta)
is a locally compact quantum group with left invariant nsf weight
\varphi
, there exist a unique multiplicative unitary
\mathbb{W}
on
L^{2}(M)\otimes L^{2}(M)
, called the
left regular representation
, such that

(6.16)

(\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathbb{W}^{*})\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)=\Lambda_{\varphi}((\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta(x)),\qquad\forall\omega\in M_{*},\forall x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}.

Corollary 6.10
.

If
\mathbb{W}
is a modular multiplicative unitary such that
(M_{\mathbb{W}},\Delta_{\mathbb{W}})
defines a locally compact quantum group, then its unitary antipode and scaling group, as defined by (
6.13
), agree with the ones defined in
[
KV03
]
.

Proof..

By
[
KV00
, Proposition 6.10]
, the left regular multiplicative unitary attached to a locally compact quantum group is modular. The corollary then follows directly from Theorem
6.9
, since the antipode for a locally compact quantum group is determined by (
6.13
) with respect to the left regular representation.
∎

Let us return now again to the locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{B}_{N}^{+}=(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
. Invoking (
6.14
), Theorem
3.11
and Theorem
4.10
, we find:

Theorem 6.11
.

The scaling group of
\mathbb{B}_{N}^{+}
is given by

(6.17)

\tau_{t}(x)=\mathbf{L}_{\delta}^{2i(1+|\hbar|^{-1})t}x\mathbf{L}_{\delta}^{-2i(1+|\hbar|^{-1})t},\qquad x\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}).

In particular, the unitary one-parameter group implementing the scaling group (
[
KV00
, Definition 6.9]
) is

(6.18)

P^{it}=\mathbf{L}_{\delta}^{2i(1+|\hbar|^{-1})t}J\mathbf{L}_{\delta}^{2i(1+|\hbar|^{-1})t}J.

From
[
SW01
, Theorem 2.3]
it can also be gathered that, writing
x^{T}=\overline{x^{*}}
, the unitary antipode
R
is uniquely determined by

(6.19)

(R\otimes T)(\mathcal{F})=\overline{\mathcal{G}},

with
\mathcal{F}
a Fock–Goncharov flip and
\mathcal{G}
as in Theorem
3.11
. From the concrete formulas for
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
, we then easily obtain:

Theorem 6.12
.

The unitary antipode of
\mathbb{B}_{N}^{+}
is determined by

(6.20)

R(\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}))=\mathbf{E}(-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k}),\qquad 0\leq k\leq s\leq n.

Recalling the notation (
6.10
), general theory
[
KV00
, Section 7]
gives us that

\varphi R=\varphi_{\delta}.

6.6.
Dual modular structure

Given a locally compact quantum group
(M,\Delta)
, we have already mentioned that its associated left regular multiplicative unitary
\mathbb{W}
, defined by (
6.16
), is modular. One further shows that
(\hat{M},\hat{\Delta})
(as defined through (
4.3
) and (
4.4
)) is again a locally compact quantum group. There is then a canonical way to identify

(6.21)

L^{2}(\hat{M})\cong L^{2}(M).

Namely, write

\lambda(\omega)=(\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathbb{W},\qquad\omega\in M_{*}.

Consider

\mathcal{I}=\{\omega\in M_{*}\mid\exists C\geq 0:|\omega(x^{*})|\leq C\|\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)\|\textrm{ for all }x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}\},

and let then
\hat{\Lambda}(\lambda(\omega))
, for
\omega\in\mathcal{I}
, be the unique vector in
L^{2}(M)
such that

\langle\Lambda_{\varphi}(x),\hat{\Lambda}(\lambda(\omega))\rangle=\omega(x^{*}),\qquad x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},\omega\in\mathcal{I}.

Then one shows that one can pick the left invariant nsf weight for
(\hat{M},\hat{\Delta})
such that
\lambda(\mathcal{I})\subseteq\mathfrak{n}_{\hat{\varphi}}
, and such that the map

\Lambda_{\hat{\varphi}}(\lambda(\omega))\mapsto\hat{\Lambda}(\lambda(\omega)),\qquad\omega\in\mathcal{I}

extends to a unitary, giving the identification (
6.21
).

Let us now return to the concrete locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{B}_{N}^{+}
determined by (
6.1
), with dual
\mathbb{B}_{N}^{-}
. Write
\rho
for the linear involution

(6.22)

\rho\colon B_{N}^{-}\rightarrow B_{N}^{-},\qquad\rho(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k})=-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\swarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\swarrow$}}}{e}_{s,k},\qquad 0\leq k\leq s\leq N,

and write

\hat{\rho}\colon\hat{B}_{N}^{-}\rightarrow\hat{B}_{N}^{-},\qquad\hat{v}\mapsto\hat{v}\circ\rho.

Proposition 6.13
.

Let
\hat{J}
be the modular conjugation for
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}_{N}^{-})
, implemented on
L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})
through (
6.21
) and (
6.7
). Then

(6.23)

\hat{J}g=\overline{g\circ\hat{\rho}},\qquad g\in L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-}).

Proof..

For a general locally compact quantum group
(M,\Delta)
with left invariant nsf weight
\varphi
and unitary antipode
R
, a GNS-map
\Gamma
for the right invariant nsf weight
\psi=\varphi R
can be realized on
L^{2}(M)
in a unique way such that

\Gamma(x)=\Lambda_{\varphi}(x\delta^{1/2}),\qquad x\in M\textrm{ with }x\circ\delta^{1/2}\textrm{ bounded and }x\delta^{1/2}\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},

and
\hat{J}
is then determined, for such
x
as above, by

(6.24)

\hat{J}\Gamma(x)=\Lambda_{\varphi}(R(x)^{*}).

Consider now the elements

\pi(f)=\int_{V}f(v)\pi(v)\mathrm{d}\!\,v\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\qquad f\in L^{1}(B_{N}^{-})

as in (
4.14
), so that with respect to (
6.3
) we have

\Lambda(\pi(f))=\check{f},\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(V).

Then it is easily seen that with

\mathcal{E}(B_{N}^{-})=\{f\colon v\mapsto P(v)e^{-|v|^{2}/2}\mid P\textrm{ polynomial}\}

(choosing an arbitrary Euclidian norm on
B_{N}^{-}
), we have
\pi(f)\circ\mathbf{E}(w)
bounded for any
w\in B_{N}^{-}
, with

\pi(f)\mathbf{E}(w)=\pi(\kappa_{w}f),\qquad(\kappa_{w}f)(v)=e^{\pi\hbar(v,w)}f(v+iw).

It then follows that for
f\in\mathcal{E}(B_{N}^{-})
we have

\pi(f)\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},\quad\pi(f)\circ\delta^{1/2}\textrm{ bounded and }\pi(f)\delta^{1/2}\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},

and that, using
-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i}=d_{r}-d_{l}
,

\Lambda_{\varphi}(\pi(f))=(\kappa_{d_{l}}f)^{\vee},\qquad\Gamma(\pi(f))=(\kappa_{d_{r}}f)^{\vee},\qquad f\in\mathcal{E}(B_{N}^{-}).

It then follows from (
6.24
) and (
6.20
) that

(6.25)

\hat{J}((\kappa_{d_{r}}f)^{\vee})=(\kappa_{d_{l}}\overline{f\circ-\rho})^{\vee}.

Using that

\rho(d_{l})=d_{r},\qquad(\rho v,w)=-(v,\rho w),\qquad v,w\in B_{N}^{-},

(
6.25
) simplifies to

\hat{J}(f^{\vee})=(\overline{f\circ-\rho})^{\vee},\qquad f\in\mathcal{E}(B_{N}^{-}).

A further simplification, together with the fact that
\mathcal{E}(B_{N}^{-})
is dense in
L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})
, leads to (
6.23
).
∎

7.
Left regular representation of
\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{+}
and duality

7.1.
Left regular representation

In this section, we determine more explicitly the left regular representation
\mathbb{W}
of
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
(see (
6.16
)). We start with the following lemma.

Lemma 7.1
.

The standard right representation
\rho
of
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
on
L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})
, defined by

\rho(x)\xi=Jx^{*}J\xi,\qquad\xi\in L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-}),

is concretely determined by

(7.1)

\rho(\mathbf{E}(v))=\mathbf{E}(\overline{v}),

where we consider the unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\overline{B_{N}^{-}}
on
L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})
given by

(7.2)

(\mathbf{E}(\overline{v})^{it}g)(\hat{w})=e^{2\pi itv\cdot\hat{w}}g(\hat{w}-t\mathcal{J}v),\qquad g\in L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-}),\hat{w}\in\hat{B}_{N}^{-},v\in B_{N}^{-},t\in\mathbb{R}.

Proof..

This is immediate from (
4.15
) and (
6.4
).
∎

In general, if
M
is a von Neumann algebra, we will view the standard right representation of
M
on
L^{2}(M)
as inducing an ordinary normal
*
-representation of
\overline{M}
on
L^{2}(M)
via

\overline{x}\xi=\rho(x^{*})\xi=JxJ\xi,\qquad x\in M,\xi\in L^{2}(M).

Then compatibly with (
7.1
), we get that

\overline{\mathbf{E}(v)}=\mathbf{E}(\overline{v}),\qquad v\in B_{N}^{-}.

Let us now further equip
L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})
with the unitary
T_{N}^{+}
-representation such that

(7.3)

(\mathbf{E}(v)^{it}g)(\hat{v})=g(\hat{v}+2t\mathcal{J}v),\qquad g\in L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-}).

Recall the unitaries introduced in (
2.64
), (
2.68
) and (
3.31
).

Theorem 7.2
.

The left regular representation
\mathbb{W}
of
(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\Delta)
is given by the unitary

(7.4)

\mathbb{W}=\overline{\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}}\mathcal{K}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})\otimes L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})).

Note that since

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\quad\overline{\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}}\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})\bar{\otimes}\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})},\quad\mathcal{K}\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(T_{N}^{+}),

we can indeed make sense of this formula through the standard action of
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
and the actions (
7.2
) and (
7.3
) of respectively
\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})}
and
L_{\hbar}(T_{N}^{+})
.

Proof..

Let
\mathcal{H}
be an irreducible unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
, and
\mathcal{F}
the associated Fock–Goncharov flip, with associated modular datum
(\mathcal{F},\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{G})
. Pick

a\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}).

By Proposition
5.1
, the normal weight

\varphi=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{Q}^{2},a}

is left invariant with respect to

\Delta\colon\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\bar{\otimes}\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}),\quad x\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{*}(1\otimes x)\mathcal{F}.

By (
5.9
), we can choose
a
such that
\varphi
is still semi-finite on
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
, and by Remark
5.10
we may view the GNS-map
\Lambda_{\varphi}
for
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
as the restriction of the one for
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, compatible with the standard left and right representations. Note that since
a\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})^{\prime}
, we have that
a
and
\mathbf{Q}
(strongly) commute.

Now the GNS-map for
\varphi
on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
can be concretely realized on
\mathcal{H}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{H}}
via

(7.5)

L^{2}(\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}))\cong\mathcal{H}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{H}},\qquad\Lambda_{\varphi}(\theta_{\xi,\eta})\cong\xi\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}a\eta}\in\mathcal{H}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{H}},\qquad\xi\in\mathcal{H},\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{Q}).

Further observe that if
x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
, then also
(\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta(x)\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
for any
\omega\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}
, by left invariance of
\varphi
. So if
\xi,\xi^{\prime},\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
and
\{e_{k}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
, we compute for
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{Q}^{2})
that:

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda_{\varphi}(\theta_{\xi,\eta}),\Lambda_{\varphi}((\omega_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta(\theta_{\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}))\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\xi,(\omega_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\Delta(\theta_{\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}))a^{*}\mathbf{Q}^{2}a\eta\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\zeta\otimes\xi,\Delta(\theta_{\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}})(\zeta^{\prime}\otimes a^{*}\mathbf{Q}^{2}a\eta)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\zeta\otimes\xi,\mathcal{F}^{*}(1\otimes\theta_{\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}})\mathcal{F}(\zeta^{\prime}\otimes a^{*}\mathbf{Q}^{2}a\eta)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{F}(\zeta\otimes\xi),e_{k}\otimes\xi^{\prime}\rangle\langle e_{k}\otimes\eta^{\prime},\mathcal{F}(\zeta^{\prime}\otimes a^{*}\mathbf{Q}^{2}a\eta)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{F}(\zeta\otimes\xi),e_{k}\otimes\xi^{\prime}\rangle\langle e_{k}\otimes a\eta^{\prime},\mathcal{F}(\zeta^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{Q}^{2}a\eta)\rangle

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqRightInvOp}}{=}

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{F}(\zeta\otimes\xi),e_{k}\otimes\xi^{\prime}\rangle\langle e_{k}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}a\eta},\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{*}(\zeta^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}a\eta^{\prime}})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\xi\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}a\eta},(\omega_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{F}^{*}_{12}\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{*}_{13})(\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{Q}a\eta^{\prime}})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda_{\varphi}(\theta_{\xi,\eta}),(\omega_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{F}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\Lambda_{\varphi}(\theta_{\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}})\rangle.

So, we find that

(7.6)

\Lambda_{\varphi}((\omega_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})\Delta(y))\rangle=(\omega_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{F}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\Lambda_{\varphi}(y),\qquad y\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi},\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}.

Now using (
2.69
) and (
3.28
), we can write

\mathcal{F}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{*}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime*}\mathcal{K}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}}^{*}.

As also
(\mathcal{K},\mathbf{Q},\hat{\mathbf{Q}},\mathcal{L})
is a modular datum, we can see from the same computation as above that
\mathcal{K}^{*}\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{*}
restricts to
L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})\otimes L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})
as the unitary operator
\mathbb{P}^{*}
such that

(\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathbb{P}^{*})\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)=\Lambda_{\varphi}((\omega\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathcal{K}^{*}(1\otimes x)\mathcal{K})),\qquad\forall\omega\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})_{*},\forall x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}.

But extending the proof of Lemma
6.5
, we easily see that

(7.7)

\mathcal{K}^{*}(1\otimes\mathbf{E}(v))\mathcal{K}=\mathbf{E}(\sum_{t=1}^{n}2(v,\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-t})\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{t}\oplus v),\qquad v\in B_{N}^{-}.

By a small calculation, we then see that indeed
\mathbb{P}=\mathcal{K}
, with its second leg acting through (
7.3
).

As

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),\qquad\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}\in\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})}\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}),

compatibility of the standard representations of
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
on
L^{2}(L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-}))\subseteq L^{2}(\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}))
then lets us conclude from (
7.6
) that (
7.4
) holds.
∎

7.2.
Dual representation

We next aim to find a formula for
\mathbb{W}
which is more in line with the expression in (
2.69
), with
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
in the form
\mathcal{R}
as in (
2.47
).

Recall first from
(
6.9
) that if we view

\mathcal{F}\in L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+}),

and
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
is represented in its standard form on
L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})
, then there exists a unique normal
*
-representation
\hat{\pi}
of
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})
on
L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})
such that

(\mathrm{id}\otimes\hat{\pi})\mathcal{F}=\mathbb{W}.

If we denote the image of the standard generators of (
2.42
) as

\mathbf{E}(\hat{\varpi}_{r}):=\hat{\pi}(\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{r})),\qquad\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{rs}:=\hat{\pi}(\mathbf{E}_{rs}),\qquad 1\leq r<s\leq N,

then we trivially have

\mathbb{W}=G_{\hbar}\left(2\sum_{t=1}^{n}\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{t}\otimes\hat{\varpi}_{N-t}\right)\prod_{r=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\prod_{s=r+1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n+1\\[-2.0pt]
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\mathbf{F}_{rs}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{rs}).

Our goal will be to determine the operators
\mathbf{E}(\hat{\varpi}_{r})
and
\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{rs}
more explicitly.

We start with the following trivial observation, which is immediate from the definitions of the structures involved (see (
7.2
) for the second part of the statement):

Lemma 7.3
.

The standard unitary
\hbar
-representation of
B_{N}^{-}
and the unitary
\hbar
-representation of
T_{N}^{+}
in (
7.3
) extend to a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
:

\mathbf{E}(v)^{it}\mathbf{E}(w)^{is}=e^{2\pi i\hbar st(v,w)}\mathbf{E}(w)^{is}\mathbf{E}(v)^{it},\qquad w\in B_{N}^{-},v\in T_{N}^{+}.

The similar rule holds for the
\overline{L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})}
-representation:

\mathbf{E}(v)^{it}\mathbf{E}(\overline{w})^{is}=e^{2\pi i\hbar st(v,w)}\mathbf{E}(\overline{w})^{is}\mathbf{E}(v)^{it},\qquad w\in B_{N}^{-},v\in T_{N}^{+}.

If we hence write

\mathbf{E}(\overline{v})=\mathbf{E}(-v),\qquad v\in T_{N}^{+},

then we also obtain a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}}
. So, if we put

\mathrm{H}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})=\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}\widetilde{\oplus}\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}},

where
\widetilde{\oplus}
indicates that we take the unique skew-symmetric bilinear form restricting to the usual one on
\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}
and
\overline{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}}
, and with cross form

(u,\overline{v})=(w,\overline{z})=0,\quad(u,\overline{z})=-(u,z),\quad(w,\overline{v})=(w,v),\qquad u,v\in B_{N}^{-},w,z\in T_{N}^{+},

the above formulas combine into a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
H(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})
with

(7.8)

\mathbf{E}(v\oplus\overline{w})=\mathbf{E}(v)\star\mathbf{E}(\overline{w}),\qquad v,w\in\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}.

Now from the above, we can by (
3.28
) rewrite (
7.4
) as

(7.9)

\mathbb{W}=\mathcal{K}\overline{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})\otimes L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})),

with
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as in (
3.30
). Then using the notation of Section
3.2
and of (
4.46
), we get with
i=s
that

(\pi_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathbb{W}=G(2\varpi_{s}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}),

which is enough to conclude the following lemma:

Lemma 7.4
.

One has

(7.10)

\mathbf{E}(\hat{\varpi}_{s})=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s}),\qquad 1\leq s\leq n,

with
\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{s})
as determined by (
7.3
).

Now on the other hand, if
i=(s,k)
, we get that

\displaystyle(\pi_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathbb{W}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(2\varpi_{i}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})\prod_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\varphi\left(f_{i}\oplus\overline{-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}}\right)\prod_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\varphi\left(f_{i}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(2\varpi_{i}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{i})\otimes\left(\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overline{-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{i})\otimes\left(\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r})\right)\right).

Since we can write

\mathbf{E}(\overline{-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}})=\mathbf{E}(\overline{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,r-s}})*\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s}),

and all
\mathbf{E}(\overline{\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,r-s}})
and
\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r^{\prime}})
strongly commute, it follows that
\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overline{-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}})
and
\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r})

-\hbar
-commute, and we can further meaningfully simplify the above expression for
\mathbb{W}
(using commutativity of the form sum) to

\displaystyle(\pi_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathbb{W}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(2\varpi_{i}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})

\displaystyle\times\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{i})\otimes\left(\left(\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overline{-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}})\right)\boxplus\left(\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r})\right)\right)\right).

On the other hand, this needs to equal also

(\pi_{i}\otimes\mathrm{id})\mathbb{W}=G(2\varpi_{i}\otimes\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})\overline{F}\left(\mathbf{E}(f_{i})\otimes\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{s}\right).

We hence deduce:

Theorem 7.5
.

For all and
1\leq s\leq n
, it holds that

(7.11)

\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{s}=\left(\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overline{-\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nwarrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nwarrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r}})\right)\boxplus\left(\boxplus_{s\leqslant r\leqslant n}\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s,n-r})\right).

We now show how this formula can be extended to all standard generators, in the form of partition functions.

Note first that we have two embeddings of
U_{N}^{+}
into
\mathrm{H}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})
, namely

(7.12)

U_{N}^{+}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}),\quad e_{abc}\mapsto e_{abc}=e_{abc}\oplus 0,\qquad e_{abc}\mapsto f_{abc}=0\oplus\vartheta(e_{abc}),

with
\vartheta
as in (
5.28
). We obtain in this way a twisted direct sum

(7.13)

U_{N}^{+}\widetilde{\oplus}U_{N}^{+}\subseteq\mathrm{H}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N}).

Lemma 7.6
.

The resulting cross relations on
U_{N}^{+}\widetilde{\oplus}U_{N}^{+}
are determined by

(7.14)

(e_{abc},f_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}})=0,\qquad c\neq 0\textrm{ or }c^{\prime}\neq 0,\qquad(e_{ab0},f_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}0})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}1&\textrm{if}&a=a^{\prime},\\
-1/2&\textrm{if}&|a-a^{\prime}|=1\\
0&\textrm{if}&|a-a^{\prime}|\geq 2.\end{array}\right.

Proof..

It follows from a straightforward verification.
∎

The diagram
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}\,\!^{\prime}_{N}(2)
associated to
U_{N}^{+}\widetilde{\oplus}U_{N}^{+}
(with respect to its given basis) is obtained by stacking a second (horizontally reflected) copy of
\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{C}\,\!^{\prime}_{N}

*

Figure 21
.
The
U_{4}^{+}(2)
-labelled graph
\mathbb{E}_{4}(2)=(\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}_{4}(2)},\ell(2))
.

It is elementary to check that this labelling is indeed consistent with the graph structure (again, the utmost right labelling is just for padding, and the precise choice of these vectors are not relevant for the further computations).

Note now that the unitary
\hbar
-representation (
7.13
) of
\mathrm{H}(\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bigtriangledown^{\prime}$\!\!}_{N})
on
L^{2}(\hat{B}_{N}^{-})
restricts via (
7.8
) to a unitary
\hbar
-representation of
U_{N}^{+}\widetilde{\oplus}U_{N}^{+}
. Let
Z_{\bullet}
be the partition operator associated to a labelled graph
\bullet
.

Theorem 7.7
.

We have

\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{rs}=Z_{\mathbb{E}_{[r,s]}(2)}.

Proof..

Consider the embedding

(7.15)

\iota\colon U_{N}^{+}\rightarrow U_{N}^{+}\widetilde{\oplus}U_{N}^{+},\qquad v\mapsto v\oplus 0.

We will show that there exists a unitary
u
such that

(7.16)

Z_{\mathbb{E}_{[r,s]}(2)}=uZ_{\Gamma_{\mathbb{E}_{[r,s]}},\iota\circ\ell}u^{*}.

This is enough to establish the theorem: indeed, then both

\mathbf{E}_{rs}\mapsto Z_{\mathbb{E}_{[r,s]}}\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad\mathbf{E}_{rs}\mapsto\hat{\mathbf{E}}_{rs}

arise from normal
*
-representations of
L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{+})
. As these coincide on the
\mathbf{E}_{r}
, they must be equal by Proposition
4.18
.

By Lemma
2.19
, we will have shown (
7.16
) if we can reduce
(\mathbf{w}_{0}\mathbf{w}_{0},\ell(2))
to
(\mathbf{w}_{0},\ell)
by a particular sequence of mutations (ignoring the rightmost columns of the latter). But this is exactly the content of Lemma
2.15
.
∎

8.
Quantum
SL^{+}(N,\mathbb{R})
as a locally compact quantum group

From any locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{B}=(L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B},\Delta)
one can construct its Drinfeld double
D\mathbb{B}
, which is again a locally compact quantum group
[
BV05
]
.

More precisely, writing the dual of
\mathbb{B}
as
\hat{\mathbb{B}}=(L(\mathbb{B}),\hat{\Delta})
, one considers

L^{\infty}(D\mathbb{B})=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B})\bar{\otimes}L(\mathbb{B})\subset\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{B})\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{B}))

with the comultiplication

\Delta_{D\mathbb{B}}=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\sigma\otimes\mathrm{id})(\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathsf{ad}(\mathbb{W})\otimes\mathrm{id})(\Delta\otimes\hat{\Delta}),

where
\mathsf{ad}(\mathbb{W})(x)=\mathbb{W}x\mathbb{W}^{*}
denotes conjugation by the left regular multiplicative unitary
\mathbb{W}
and
\sigma
is the tensor flip. The unitary antipode and scaling group of
L^{\infty}(D\mathbb{B})
are given by

R_{D\mathbb{B}}=(R\otimes\hat{R})\mathsf{ad}(\mathbb{W})=\mathsf{ad}(\mathbb{W}^{*})(R\otimes\hat{R}),\quad\tau^{D\mathbb{B}}_{t}=\tau_{-t}\otimes\hat{\tau}_{t},

respectively.

We are interested in the case
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-}
, and we will identify
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B})=L(\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{+})=L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})
and
L(\mathbb{B})=L(\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-})=L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
, using the notation introduced at the start of Section
6
, so that
L^{\infty}(D\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-})=L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
.

Let us write
L_{\hbar}(T_{N})
for the diagonal copy of
L_{\hbar}(T_{N}^{\pm})
inside
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{+})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{-})
generated by the operators

\mathbf{D}_{s}=\mathbf{K}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{L}_{s},\qquad 1\leq s\leq n,

compare (
4.51
), (
4.54
), and consider the von Neumann subalgebra
M\subseteq L^{\infty}(D\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-})
generated by
L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{+})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{-})
and
L_{\hbar}(T_{N})
.

Proposition 8.1
.

The von Neumann algebra
M
is a Baaj–Vaes subalgebra of
L^{\infty}(D\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-})
. That is, we have
\Delta_{D\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-}}(M)\subseteq M\bar{\otimes}M
, and
M
is invariant under the unitary antipode and the scaling group of
L^{\infty}(D\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-})
.

Proof..

Consider the Fock–Goncharov flip
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{K}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{K}
.
Using (
2.65
) one easily checks that

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}\in L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{-})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{+})
, or equivalently,
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{21}\in L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{+})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{-})\subseteq M
.
Moreover, by inspecting (
2.75
) one obtains
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{21}\in M
, but note that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime\prime}_{21}
is not contained in
L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{+})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{-})
.
It follows from the proof of Theorem
6.9
that we can similarly write
\mathbb{W}=\mathcal{K}\mathbb{W}^{\prime}=\mathbb{W}^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{K}
with
\mathbb{W}^{\prime}_{21}\in L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{+})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{-})\subseteq M
and
\mathbb{W}^{\prime\prime}_{21}\in M
, see also the discussion at the start of section
7.2
.

Let us now abbreviate
D=D\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-}
and observe that the left regular representation of
\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-}
is given by
\widehat{\mathbb{W}}=\mathbb{W}^{*}_{21}=(\mathbb{W}^{\prime})^{*}_{21}\mathcal{K}_{21}^{*}=\mathcal{K}_{21}^{*}(\mathbb{W}^{\prime\prime})^{*}_{21}
.
According to Proposition
4.22
we have
\Delta(\mathbf{L}_{s})=\mathbf{L}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{L}_{s}
and
\hat{\Delta}(\mathbf{K}_{s})=\mathbf{K}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{K}_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq n
.
Using (
2.10
) one checks that
(\hat{\Delta}\otimes\Delta)(\mathbf{D}_{s})
strongly commutes with
\mathcal{K}_{32}
for
1\leq s\leq n
, and as a consequence we obtain

\Delta_{D}(L_{\hbar}(T_{N}))\subseteq(\mathbb{W}^{\prime})^{*}_{23}(L_{\hbar}(T_{N})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(T_{N}))(\mathbb{W}^{\prime})_{23}\subseteq M\bar{\otimes}M.

We next claim
\Delta_{D}(1\otimes L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{-}))\subseteq M\bar{\otimes}M
. By Proposition
4.19
it is enough to check
\Delta_{D}(1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s}^{it})\in M\bar{\otimes}M
for the standard generators
\mathbf{F}_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq n
defined in (
4.53
). Due to Proposition
4.22
we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{D}(1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s})

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{W}^{\prime})^{*}_{23}\mathcal{K}^{*}_{23}(1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s}\otimes 1\otimes\mathbf{L}_{s}\boxplus 1\otimes 1\otimes 1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s})\mathcal{K}_{23}\mathbb{W}^{\prime}_{23}

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{W}^{\prime})^{*}_{23}(1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{K}_{s}\otimes\mathbf{L}_{s}\boxplus 1\otimes 1\otimes 1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s})\mathbb{W}^{\prime}_{23},

using that
\mathcal{K}^{*}(\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k})\otimes 1)\mathcal{K}=\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{s,k})\otimes\mathbf{E}(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{N-s})
for all
0\leq k\leq s-1
by (
2.23
). Combining this again with
\mathbb{W}^{\prime}\in L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{-})\bar{\otimes}L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{+})
we conclude that
\Delta_{D}(1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s}^{it})\in M\bar{\otimes}M
for
1\leq s\leq n
as required. Using Proposition
4.18
one verifies
\Delta_{D}(L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{+})\otimes 1)\subseteq M\bar{\otimes}M
in a similar way, which allows us to conclude that
\Delta_{D}(M)\subseteq M\bar{\otimes}M
.

Next we use (
6.20
) to show that
M
is invariant under the unitary antipode of the Drinfeld double. Firstly, we observe
(\hat{R}\otimes R)(\mathbf{D}_{s})=\mathbf{D}_{s}^{-1}
for
1\leq s\leq n
, and using that
\mathbf{D}_{s}^{-1}
strongly commutes with
\mathcal{K}_{21}
combined with
\mathbb{W}_{21}^{\prime\prime}\in M
we get
R_{D}(L_{\hbar}(T_{N}))\subseteq M
.
For the standard generators
\mathbf{F}_{s}
for
1\leq s\leq n
we obtain

\displaystyle R_{D}(1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{W}_{21}^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{K}_{21}(\hat{R}\otimes R)(1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s})\mathcal{K}_{21}^{*}(\mathbb{W}_{21}^{\prime\prime})^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{W}_{21}^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{K}_{21}(1\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s}*\mathbf{L}_{s}^{-1})\mathcal{K}^{*}_{21}(\mathbb{W}_{21}^{\prime\prime})^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{W}_{21}^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{K}_{s}^{-1}\otimes\mathbf{F}_{s}*\mathbf{L}_{s}^{-1})(\mathbb{W}_{21}^{\prime\prime})^{*},

and using again
\mathbb{W}_{21}^{\prime\prime}\in M
and Proposition
4.19
we deduce
R_{D}(1\otimes L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{-}))\subseteq M
.
Analogously one verifies
R_{D}(L_{\hbar}(U_{N}^{+})\otimes 1)\subseteq M
.

Finally, recall from Theorem
6.11
and Theorem
3.11

that the scaling groups of
L_{\hbar}(B_{N}^{\pm})
are implemented by conjugation with
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{it}
and
\mathbf{Q}^{it}
, respectively. Here
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}=\mathbf{L}_{\delta}^{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}
where
\mathbf{L}_{\delta}=\mathbf{E}(\sum\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i})
, and
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{K}_{\delta}^{-(1+|\hbar|^{-1})}
where
\mathbf{K}_{\delta}=\mathbf{E}(\sum\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{\varpi}_{i})
. Clearly, each
\mathbf{D}_{s}
is fixed by
\tau^{D}_{t}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, from (
2.23
) we see that the standard generators
\mathbf{F}_{s}
are preserved under conjugation by
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{it}
up to a scalar. Similarly, the operators
\mathbf{E}_{s}
are preserved under conjugation by
\mathbf{Q}^{it}
up to a scalar. We conclude that
M
is invariant under the scaling group.
∎

According to
[
BV05
, Proposition A.5]
we conclude that
M=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
for a uniquely determined locally compact quantum group
\mathbb{G}
.

Definition 8.2
.

We call
\mathbb{G}=SL_{\hbar}^{+}(N,\mathbb{R})
the totally positive quantum
SL(N,\mathbb{R})
-group.

The general theory from
[
BV05
]
allows one to describe the basic structure of
D\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{-}
and
SL_{\hbar}^{+}(N,\mathbb{R})
in terms of the quantized Borel groups
\mathbb{B}_{N,\hbar}^{\pm}
.
A more detailed study of
SL_{\hbar}^{+}(N,\mathbb{R})
and its representation theory will be left to future work.

Appendix A
The quantum dilogarithm function

For
a_{+},a_{-}\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, put

(A.1)

W_{a_{+},a_{-}}(z)=-\frac{\pi i}{2}\int_{\Omega}\frac{dy}{y}\,\frac{e^{-2iyz}}{\sinh(a_{+}y)\sinh(a_{-}y)},\qquad z\in\mathbb{R},

with
\Omega
any curve in
\mathbb{C}
going from
-\infty
to
+\infty
with a small bump in the upper half plane around the origin. Gathering leading terms, this can be reduced to the alternative expression

(A.2)

W_{a_{+},a_{-}}(z)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}\left(\frac{a_{+}}{a_{-}}+\frac{a_{-}}{a_{+}}\right)+\frac{\pi^{2}z^{2}}{a_{+}a_{-}}-2\pi\widetilde{W}_{a_{+},a_{-}}(z),

where
\widetilde{W}_{a_{+},a_{-}}(z)
is defined through the real integral

(A.3)

\widetilde{W}_{a_{+},a_{-}}(z)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dy}{y}\left(\frac{\sin(2yz)}{2\sinh(a_{+}y)\sinh(a_{-}y)}-\frac{z}{a_{+}a_{-}y}\right).

Now recalling (
1.19
), we see that

(A.4)

W_{a_{+},a_{-}}(z)=W_{a_{+}/a_{-}}(2\pi z/|a_{+}|).

Writing

(A.5)

\varphi_{a_{+},a_{-}}(z)=\exp\left(\frac{i}{2\pi}W_{a_{+},a_{-}}(z)\right)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Omega}\frac{dy}{y}\,\frac{e^{-2iyz}}{\sinh(a_{+}y)\sinh(a_{-}y)}\right),

the resulting function for
a_{+}=a_{-}^{-1}=:b
is the quantum dilogarithm as it appears in e.g.
[
Fad95
,
Kas01
,
FG09
]
. By (
A.4
), we have the transformation formula

(A.6)

\varphi_{a_{+},a_{-}}(z)=\varphi_{a_{-}/a_{+}}(2\pi z/|a_{+}|).

Finally,

(A.7)

G(a_{+},a_{-};z)=\exp(i\widetilde{W}_{a_{+},a_{-}}(z))

gives the
hyperbolic gamma function
as introduced in
[
Rui97
]
(see also
[
Rui05
, Appendix A]
).

Note that

W_{1/\hbar}(t)=\operatorname{sgn}(\hbar)W_{1/|\hbar|}(t),\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}.

We then have the following alternative expression for
W_{1/|\hbar|}
, see
[
GK21
, Proposition 2.4]
.

Lemma A.1
.

With
\theta>0
, we have

(A.8)

W_{\theta}(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\ln(1+s^{-\theta})}{s+e^{-t}}\mathrm{d}\!\,s,\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.

This is the expression for
W_{\theta}
as it appears in
[
Wor00
]
.

In the following, we recall some of the structural properties of the quantum dilogarithm that we will need.

Fix
\theta>0
. First note that from (
1.19
), we see that
W_{\theta}
extends holomorphically to the strip

\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid|\mathrm{Im}(z)|<\pi(1+\theta^{-1})\}.

It furthermore satisfies the functional identity

W_{\theta}(x+\pi i)-W_{\theta}(x-\pi i)=2\pi i\ln(1+e^{\theta x}),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}

(see
[
Wor00
, Lemma 1.1]
). Hence

(A.9)

V_{\theta}(z):=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\pi i}W_{\theta}(z)\right)=\varphi_{-\theta^{-1}}(z)=1/\varphi_{\theta^{-1}}(z)

admits a meromorphic extension

V_{\theta}\colon\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\hat{\mathbb{C}}.

(We use the notation (
A.9
) to be in agreement with
[
Wor00
]
.) Note that writing

(A.10)

\chi=\pi(\theta+\theta^{-1})/24,

we get from (
A.2
) the identity

(A.11)

V_{\theta}(z)=e^{-i\chi}e^{-i\theta z^{2}/8\pi}G(2\pi,2\pi/\theta;z)

as claimed in
[
Rui05
, Equation (A.18)]
.

The following Lemma gathers results from
[
Wor00
, Section 1 and Appendix A]
and
[
Rui05
, Appendix A]
(the latter reference extends certain results of
[
Wor00
]
to a larger domain for the deformation parameter).

Proposition A.2
.

Let
\theta>0
. The meromorphic function
V_{\theta}
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

Its zeroes are located at

Z=\{\pi i\left((2k+1)+(2l+1)\theta^{-1}\right)\mid k,l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\},

while its poles are located at
-Z
. The multiplicity of a zero
z
is counted by the number of couples
(k,l)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
for which
z=\pi i((2k+1)+(2l+1)\theta^{-1})
, and similarly for poles. In particular, all zeroes and poles are simple if
\theta
is irrational.

(2)

It satisfies the following functional equations: for
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

(A.12)

V_{\theta}(z+2\pi i)=(1+e^{i\pi\theta}e^{\theta z})V_{\theta}(z),

(A.13)

V_{\theta}(z+2\pi i/\theta)=(1+e^{\pi i/\theta}e^{z})V_{\theta}(z).

(3)

It satisfies the following functional equation: for
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

(A.14)

V_{\theta}(z)V_{\theta}(-z)=V_{\theta}(z)/\overline{V_{\theta}(-\overline{z})}=e^{-2i\chi}e^{-i\theta z^{2}/4\pi}.

(4)

The functions
V_{\theta}
and
V_{1/\theta}
are related through

(A.15)

V_{1/\theta}(z)=V_{\theta}(z/\theta),\qquad z\in\mathbb{R}.

(5)

For fixed
0<\rho<\min\{1,\theta\}
and with
y
uniformly varying over a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}
, we have

(A.16)

V_{\theta}(x+iy)=1+\mathcal{O}(e^{\rho x}),\qquad x\rightarrow-\infty,

(A.17)

e^{i\theta(x+iy)^{2}/4\pi}V_{\theta}(x+iy)=e^{-2i\chi}+\mathcal{O}(e^{-\rho x}),\qquad x\rightarrow+\infty.

In particular, for any strip
S_{b}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid-b\leq\mathrm{Im}(z)\leq b\}
, there exists
a\geq 0
such that, for any
\lambda>0
, the function
z\mapsto V_{\theta}(z)e^{-\lambda z^{2}}
is bounded on
\{z\in S_{b}\mid|\mathrm{Re}(z)|\geq a\}
.

To prove the modularity of particular multiplicative unitaries, we will need some information about the (
\theta
-rescaled) Fourier dual of
V_{\theta}
and its inverse.

Let
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
be the class of Schwartz space functions with its natural Fréchet topology, and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
its dual, the space of tempered distributions. We write the pairing of a distribution and a function alternatively as

\langle f,g\rangle=\langle f(x),g(x)\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)g(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,x,\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}),g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}).

We will use below rescaled Fourier transforms, so

(A.18)

\hat{g}(x)=\frac{\sqrt{\theta}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\theta xy/2\pi}g(y)\mathrm{d}\!\,y,\qquad\check{g}(y)=\frac{\sqrt{\theta}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\theta xy/2\pi}g(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,x,\qquad g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}),

and similarly for their extension to the space of tempered distributions,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{f}(y)g(y)\mathrm{d}\!\,y:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\hat{g}(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,x,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\check{f}(y)g(y)\mathrm{d}\!\,y:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)\check{g}(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,x,\\
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}),g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}).

In particular, we consider
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, and as such can interpret Fourier duals of elements in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
as tempered distributions.

Since
V_{\theta}
, restricted to
\mathbb{R}
, has values on the unit circle and hence
1/V_{\theta}=\overline{V}_{\theta}
, its complex conjugate, we can consider the inverse Fourier duals of
V_{\theta}
and
\overline{V}_{\theta}
in the above sense of tempered distributions.

Proposition A.3
.

Let
\theta>0
, and put

a=\pi(1+\theta^{-1}).

Then

(A.19)

\hat{\overline{V}}_{\theta}(y)=\alpha_{\theta}(y)\hat{V}_{\theta}(y),

where

(A.20)

\alpha_{\theta}(y)=e^{a\theta y/2\pi}e^{-i\theta y^{2}/4\pi}.

Here the right hand side in (
A.19
) is a priori defined on the Fréchet dense domain
\mathscr{D}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
of
f
such that
\alpha_{\theta}f
again lies in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. What the result says in particular, is that the resulting functional uniquely extends to a tempered distribution, i.e.
\alpha_{\theta}
is a multiplier for
\hat{V}_{\theta}
.

The result in Proposition
A.3
again appears in
[
Wor00
, Appendix B]
, but under the restriction
\theta>2
. We use the results of
[
Rui05
, Appendix A]
to extend this result to arbitrary
\theta>0
.

Proof..

With
\chi
as in (
A.10
), write

\widetilde{V}_{\theta}(y)=e^{-i\pi/4}e^{-2i\chi}e^{i\theta y^{2}/4\pi}V_{\theta}(y-ia).

Then
\widetilde{V}_{\theta}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
except for a simple pole at
0
, and extends to a meromorphic function that is holomorphic on the upper half plane. By the estimates (
A.16
) and (
A.17
), we know that
\widetilde{V}_{\theta}
is bounded at infinity, and we can hence make sense of the tempered distribution

(A.21)

D(y)=P.V.(\widetilde{V}_{\theta}(y))-\pi i\mathrm{Res}_{0}(\widetilde{V}_{\theta})\delta_{0}(y).

We claim that this is exactly the distribution
\hat{V}_{\theta}(y)
. Indeed, as both distributions are tempered, it is enough to establish equality on

\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R})=\{g\colon x\mapsto P(x)e^{-x^{2}/2}\mid P\textrm{ polynomial}\},

which is a Fréchet dense subspace of
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
(
[
RS81
, Theorem V.13]
), stable under Fourier duality.

Now from
[
Rui05
, (A.21)]
we get that, for any
0<\delta<a
,

V_{\theta}(z)=\frac{\sqrt{\theta}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}+i\delta}e^{i\theta yz/2\pi}\widetilde{V}_{\theta}(y)\mathrm{d}\!\,y,\qquad-a<\mathrm{Im}(z)<-\delta,

where we note that the integrand is
L^{1}
on
\mathbb{R}+i\delta
by the behaviour of
x\mapsto V_{\theta}(x+iy)
at infinity in Proposition
A.2
.(
5
). We then find for
g\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R})
and
\Omega
as before that, again by Proposition
A.2
.(
5
), the following string of equalities is justified for
0<\varepsilon
small:

\displaystyle\langle D(y),g(y)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\widetilde{V}_{\theta}(y)g(y)\mathrm{d}\!\,y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}+i\delta}\widetilde{V}_{\theta}(y)g(y)\mathrm{d}\!\,y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\theta}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}-i\varepsilon}\hat{g}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}+i\delta}\widetilde{V}_{\theta}(y)e^{i\theta xy/2\pi}\mathrm{d}\!\,y\mathrm{d}\!\,x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}-i\varepsilon}\hat{g}(x)V_{\theta}(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\hat{V}_{\theta}(y),g(y)\rangle.

In the following, we can hence write

(A.22)

\hat{V}_{\theta}(y)=D(y).

To finish the proof, it is now sufficient to show that
\hat{V}_{\theta}
is in the definition domain of multiplication with
\alpha_{\theta}
, and that (
A.19
) holds. But clearly from (
A.21
) and (
A.22
) we have

\hat{\overline{V}}_{\theta}(y)=P.V.(\overline{\widetilde{V}}_{\theta}(-y))+\pi i\overline{\mathrm{Res}_{0}(\widetilde{V}_{\theta})}\delta_{0}(y).

So (
A.19
) follows by observing that (
A.14
) gives

\overline{\widetilde{V}}_{\theta}(-y)=\alpha_{\theta}(y)\widetilde{V}_{\theta}(y),\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}^{\times},

and that
\alpha_{\theta}(0)=1
.
∎

Appendix B
Pentagon equation

In this section, we give some details on the proof of Theorem
1.11
