. There are basically two arguments in the literature that we are aware of: the abstract operator algebraic approach in
[
Wor00
]
(whose proof however has a restriction on the parameter range), and the approach in
[
FKV01
]
obtained via considering the concrete operators
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{P}
and proving (
1.11
) via an integral transform with the deformation parameter extended to a particular region in the complex domain. In this section, we argue that, with a small modification, the approach in
[
Wor00
]
can be completed to give a full proof of the theorem, and then comment on how this result is equivalent to the one obtained in
[
FKV01
]
.

Fix
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, and let
\mathbf{A}=e^{\mathbf{a}},\mathbf{B}=e^{\mathbf{b}}
be two strictly positive
\hbar
-commuting operators on a Hilbert space, i.e. satisfying (
1.27
). Put
\mathbf{a}\dotplus\mathbf{b}
the unique self-adjoint operator such that

e^{it(\mathbf{a}\dotplus\mathbf{b})}=(\mathbf{A}\star\mathbf{B})^{it},\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},

with
\mathbf{A}\star\mathbf{B}
as in (
1.28
). It can indeed easily be shown that
\mathbf{a}\dotplus\mathbf{b}
is the unique self-adjoint extension of the sum
\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}
defined on the intersection of their domains, by considering the concrete presentation (
B.3
) below and arguing that
\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{P}
is essentially self-adjoint on
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
by means of the identity

(B.1)

(e^{-\pi i\mathbf{X}^{2}}\mathbf{P}e^{\pi i\mathbf{X}^{2}})f=\mathbf{X}f+\mathbf{P}f,\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}).

We have to prove that, with
\varphi_{\hbar}
as in (
1.20
), it holds that

(B.2)

\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{a})\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{b})=\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{b})\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{a}\dotplus\mathbf{b})\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{a}),\qquad\forall\hbar>0

(the case
\hbar<0
follows by symmetry, using
\overline{\varphi}_{\hbar}=\varphi_{-\hbar}
).

Alternatively, pick
b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with
b^{2}=\hbar
. Then by the Stone-von Neumann theorem, we may as well take

(B.3)

\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad\mathbf{a}=2\pi b\mathbf{P},\qquad\mathbf{b}=2\pi b\mathbf{X},

with
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{P}
as in (
3.7
). Then using the notation (
A.5
) and writing

e_{b}(z)=\varphi_{b^{-1},b}(z)=\varphi_{b^{2}}(2\pi bz),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C},

we get that (
B.2
) is equivalent to proving

(B.4)

e_{b}(\mathbf{P})e_{b}(\mathbf{X})=e_{b}(\mathbf{X})e_{b}(\mathbf{X}\dotplus\mathbf{P})e_{b}(\mathbf{P}).

Now (
B.2
) is proven in
[
Wor00
, Theorem 6.1]
for
0<\hbar<1/2
. In fact, a careful inspection shows that the proof still works for
0<\hbar<1
. Indeed, the only property that is used crucially for that part of the argument is that the function

x\mapsto\frac{1-\varphi_{\hbar}(\ln(x))}{x}

stays bounded in the neighborhood of
0
, which is guaranteed for
0<\hbar<1
by Proposition
A.2
.(4).

Now from (
1.19
) we see that

(B.5)

W_{1/\theta}(x)=W_{\theta}(x/\theta),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

Hence (
B.2
) holds for all
\hbar\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\setminus\{1\}
, or alternatively, (
B.4
) holds for all
b\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\setminus\{1\}
. The missing case
b=1
is now easily derived from the fact that
b\mapsto e_{b}(z)
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for each
z\in\mathbb{R}
, which (by the dominated convergence theorem) is enough to guarantee that
b\mapsto e_{b}(\mathbf{a})
is strongly continuous for each strictly positive operator
\mathbf{a}
. Hence both sides of (
B.4
) converge strongly as
b\rightarrow 1
to their instances at
b=1
, giving that (
B.4
) holds for all
b>0
.

Let us now comment on the proof of
[
FKV01
]
. First note that
W_{b^{-1},b}(z)
as in (
A.1
) is still well-defined for any
b\in\mathbb{C}\setminus i\mathbb{R}
(taking the half-circle in the contour
\Omega
of radius
\delta<|b|^{\pm 1}
), and then defines on the halfplane
H_{+}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\mathrm{Re}(z)>0\}
a function

W\colon H_{+}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad(b,z)\mapsto W_{b,b^{-1}}(z)

which is holomorphic in
b
for each
z
.

In fact, we easily see from (
A.1
) that
W(b,x)
remains bounded for
b
ranging over a compact region, uniformly over all
x
on the negative axis, and that we have there continuity in
b
, uniformly over all
x
. We hence obtain a Banach space valued holomorphic function

W^{-}\colon H_{+}\rightarrow C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}),\qquad W^{-}(b)(x)=W_{b,b^{-1}}(x),

and consequently also a holomorphic function

e^{-}\colon H_{+}\rightarrow C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}),\qquad e^{-}(b)(x)=e_{b}(x).

On the other hand, the identity (
A.14
), the renormalisation (
A.6
) and holomorphicity in
b
lead to the identity

e_{b}(-x)=e^{-2\pi i(b^{2}+b^{-2})/24}e^{ix^{2}/2}\overline{e_{\overline{b}}(x)},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},b\in H_{+},

from which it is immediately concluded that we obtain in fact a Banach space valued holomorphic function

e\colon H_{+}\rightarrow C_{b}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad e(b)(x)=e_{b}(x).

It is hence sufficient to show that (
B.4
) holds on any subset of
b
’s with a cluster point in
H_{+}
. From the result in
[
Wor00
, Theorem 6.1]
, one can take
(0,1/\sqrt{2})
as such a set. In
[
FKV01
, Section 6]
, one rather shows (
B.4
) directly on the quadrant
\mathbb{R}_{>0}+i\mathbb{R}_{>0}\subseteq H_{+}
.

Appendix C
Modularity of the rank
1
Fock–Goncharov flip

In this section we present a proof of Theorem
3.4
. It is simply the proof of
[
WZ02
, Proposition 2.3]
with no restriction on the deformation parameter.

We will need the notion of spectral measure: recall that if
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space,
\mathbf{H}
a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
and
\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
, the associated
spectral measure
is the (absolutely) bounded (complex) measure
\mu_{\xi^{\prime},\xi}
on
\mathbb{R}
determined by

\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,\mu_{\xi^{\prime},\xi}(x)=\langle\xi^{\prime},g(\mathbf{H})\xi\rangle,\qquad g\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}).

We also recall that if
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space and
\mathbf{H}
a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
, then for any
\xi\in\cap_{z\in\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{D}(e^{z\mathbf{H}})
the map

\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\qquad z\mapsto e^{z\mathbf{H}}\xi

is automatically a Banach space valued holomorphic function.

Proposition C.1
.

Consider on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
the operators

\mathbf{F}=e^{\mathbf{X}},\qquad\mathbf{L}=e^{-4\pi^{2}\hbar\mathbf{P}},\qquad\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{L}\star\mathbf{F}^{-1},\qquad\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{F}^{-1}

as in the statement of Theorem
3.4
. Then there exists a subspace
V\subseteq L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

•

V\subseteq\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{F}^{z})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{L}^{z})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{E}^{z})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{K}^{z})
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}
,

•

V
is stable under each
\mathbf{F}^{z},\mathbf{L}^{z},\mathbf{E}^{z},\mathbf{K}^{z}
, and

•

for each
z\in\mathbb{C}
the space
V
is a core for any of the operators
\mathbf{F}^{z},\mathbf{L}^{z},\mathbf{E}^{z}
and
\mathbf{K}^{z}
.

Proof..

We can take
V
to be the linear span of all functions of the form
x\mapsto e^{\alpha x+\beta x^{2}}
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
and
\mathrm{Re}(\beta)<0
. Indeed,
V
is stable under Fourier duality, and is easily seen to form a core of
e^{z\mathbf{X}}
for each
z\in\mathbb{C}
, left stable by each such operator. By Fourier duality this then also holds for
e^{z\mathbf{P}}
for each
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Finally, by (
B.1
) it also follows that
V
is a core for each such
\mathbf{E}^{z}
, and stable under all
\mathbf{E}^{z}
.
∎

Consider now the setting and notation of Theorem
3.4
. In the terminology of Definition
3.6
, it is sufficient to show that the triple
(\mathbb{W},\mathbf{L}^{\frac{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}{2}},\mathbb{W}_{c})
is a weakly modular datum, since clearly
\mathbb{W}
commutes with

\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\otimes\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{L}^{\frac{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}{2}}\otimes\mathbf{K}^{-\frac{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}{2}}.

We slightly rewrite (
3.3
) as in (
5.27
) to be more in line with the setup of
[
WZ02
]
. Namely, put

(C.1)

Z=\mathbb{W}_{21}^{*}=F_{\hbar}(\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathbf{F})\exp\left(-\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}\ln(\mathbf{K})\otimes\ln(\mathbf{L})\right)

and put

\widetilde{Z}={\mathbb{W}_{c}}_{,21}=\exp\left(\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}\ln(\mathbf{K})\otimes\ln(\overline{\mathbf{L}})\right)F_{\hbar}(\mathbf{K}^{-1}\star\mathbf{E}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}}).

Then with
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}=\mathbf{L}^{\frac{1+|\hbar|^{-1}}{2}}
, we need to show that

(C.2)

\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}},\widetilde{Z}^{*}(\xi\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})\rangle=\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,Z(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime})\rangle,\qquad\forall\xi,\xi^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}),\eta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}).

We start with the following easy lemma, which follows immediately by functional calculus.

Lemma C.2
.

Assume
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}
are selfadjoint operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then for all
\xi,\xi^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have

(C.3)

\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\eta^{\prime}},\exp\left(\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}\mathbf{a}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{b}}\right)(\xi\otimes\overline{\eta})\rangle\\
=\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,\exp\left(\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}\mathbf{a}\otimes\mathbf{b}\right)(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime})\rangle.

We now show (
C.2
).
Note first that in this identity, we may restrict to
\xi,\xi^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in V
,
with
V
as in Proposition
C.1
, as this space forms a core for
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{\pm 1}
. In the following, we fix such
\eta,\eta^{\prime},\xi,\xi^{\prime}
.

Put

\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{E}\otimes\mathbf{F},\qquad\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}=\mathbf{K}^{-1}\star\mathbf{E}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}},\\
E_{\hbar}=\exp\left(-\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}(\ln(\mathbf{K})\otimes\ln(\mathbf{L})\right),\quad\widetilde{E}_{\hbar}=\exp\left(-\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}(\ln(\mathbf{K})\otimes\ln(\overline{\mathbf{L}})\right),

and for
y\in\mathbb{R}
write

\varphi(y)=\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,\mathbf{C}^{iy/2\pi\hbar}E_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime})\rangle,\qquad\psi(y)=\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}},\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{iy/2\pi\hbar}\widetilde{E}_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})\rangle.

By our choice of
\xi^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}
we have that

z\mapsto\mathbf{C}^{iz}(\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta),\qquad z\mapsto\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{iz}(\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}})

are holomorphic maps
\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, which implies that
\varphi,\psi
extend to holomorphic functions.

From the skew commutation between
\mathbf{L}
and
\mathbf{F}
, resp.
\overline{\mathbf{L}}
and
\overline{\mathbf{F}}
, we get

(C.4)

\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{L}^{-\overline{z}}\eta,\mathbf{C}^{iy/2\pi\hbar}E_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\mathbf{L}^{z}\eta^{\prime})\rangle=e^{yz}\varphi(y),

(C.5)

\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{L}}^{-\overline{z}}\,\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}},\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{iy/2\pi\hbar}\widetilde{E}_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\overline{\mathbf{L}}^{z}\,\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})\rangle=e^{-yz}\psi(y).

Since the left hand sides and their derivatives are bounded in
y\in\mathbb{R}
(for
z\in\mathbb{R}
fixed), we find that
\varphi,\psi
and their derivatives have faster than exponential decay at infinity, and so in particular
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
. Moreover, if we denote by
\mu
the spectral measure for
\ln(\mathbf{C})
associated to
\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta
and
E_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime})
, and by
\nu
the spectral measure for
\ln(\widetilde{\mathbf{C}})
associated to
\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}}
and
\widetilde{E}_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})
, we see that

\varphi(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ixy/2\pi\hbar}\mathrm{d}\!\,\mu(x),\qquad\psi(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ixy/2\pi\hbar}\mathrm{d}\!\,\nu(x),

so it follows that
\mu,\nu
are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Then, using the notation (
A.18
) with
\theta:=|\hbar|^{-1}
, and putting
\varepsilon=\operatorname{sgn}(\hbar)
, we see that

(C.6)

\mathrm{d}\!\,\mu(x)=(4\pi^{2}|\hbar|)^{-1/2}\hat{\varphi}(\varepsilon x)\mathrm{d}\!\,x,\qquad\mathrm{d}\!\,\nu(x)=(4\pi^{2}|\hbar|)^{-1/2}\hat{\psi}(\varepsilon x)\mathrm{d}\!\,x.

Now from (
C.5
) we get in particular, putting
z=-(1+\theta)/2
, that

\psi(y)=e^{-(1+\theta)y/2}\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\eta^{\prime}},\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{iy/2\pi\hbar}\widetilde{E}_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\overline{\eta})\rangle

But

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\eta^{\prime}},\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}^{iy/2\pi\hbar}\widetilde{E}_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\overline{\eta})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(\mathbf{K}^{-1}\star\mathbf{E})^{-iy/2\pi\hbar}\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}}^{-iy/2\pi\hbar}\overline{\eta^{\prime}},\widetilde{E}_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\overline{\eta})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(\mathbf{K}^{-1}\star\mathbf{E})^{-iy/2\pi\hbar}\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{F}^{iy/2\pi\hbar}}\overline{\eta^{\prime}},\exp\left(-\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}(\ln(\mathbf{K})\otimes\ln(\overline{\mathbf{L}})\right)(\xi\otimes\overline{\eta})\rangle

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqAntiSwitch}}{=}\langle(\mathbf{K}^{-1}\star\mathbf{E})^{-iy/2\pi\hbar}\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,\exp\left(-\frac{i}{2\pi\hbar}(\ln(\mathbf{K})\otimes\ln(\mathbf{L})\right)(\xi\otimes\mathbf{F}^{iy/2\pi\hbar}\eta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,((\mathbf{K}^{-1}\star\mathbf{E})^{iy/2\pi\hbar}\otimes 1)E_{\hbar}(1\otimes\mathbf{F}^{iy/2\pi\hbar})(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqGausEqInv}}{=}\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,((\mathbf{K}^{-1}\star\mathbf{E})^{iy/2\pi\hbar}\otimes 1)(\mathbf{K}\otimes\mathbf{F})^{iy/2\pi\hbar}E_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqStarProd}}{=}e^{i\pi\hbar(y/2\pi\hbar)^{2}}\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,(\mathbf{E}^{iy/2\pi\hbar}\otimes\mathbf{F}^{iy/2\pi\hbar})E_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{iy^{2}/4\pi\hbar}\varphi(y).

We hence conclude that

(C.7)

\psi(y)=e^{-(1+\theta)y/2}e^{iy^{2}/4\pi\hbar}\varphi(y).

Now recall again the notation and results of Proposition
A.3
. Then from (
C.7
) we see that

(C.8)

\alpha_{\theta}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon y)\psi(y)=\varphi(y).

We can now compute that

\displaystyle\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}},\widetilde{Z}^{*}(\xi\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{-1}\eta^{\prime}},\overline{F}_{\hbar}(\widetilde{\mathbf{C}})\widetilde{E}_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\overline{\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\eta})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}V_{\theta}^{-\varepsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,\nu(x)

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqSpecMeasQD}}{=}

\displaystyle(4\pi^{2}|\hbar|)^{-1/2}\langle V_{\theta}^{-\varepsilon}(x),\hat{\psi}(\varepsilon x)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(4\pi^{2}|\hbar|)^{-1/2}\langle(V^{-\varepsilon}_{\theta})^{\wedge}(\varepsilon y),\psi(y)\rangle

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqDefNeedEqual}}{=}

\displaystyle(4\pi^{2}|\hbar|)^{-1/2}\langle(V^{\varepsilon}_{\theta})^{\wedge}(\varepsilon y),\alpha_{\theta}^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon y)\psi(y)\rangle

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqIdPHPS}}{=}

\displaystyle(4\pi^{2}|\hbar|)^{-1/2}\langle(V_{\theta}^{\varepsilon})^{\wedge}(\varepsilon y),\varphi(y)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(4\pi^{2}|\hbar|)^{-1/2}\langle V_{\theta}^{\varepsilon}(x),\hat{\varphi}(\varepsilon x)\rangle

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{EqSpecMeasQD}}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}V_{\theta}^{\varepsilon}(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,\mu(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,F_{\hbar}(\mathbf{C})E_{\hbar}(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\xi^{\prime}\otimes\eta,Z(\xi\otimes\eta^{\prime})\rangle.

Appendix D
Multiplier C
∗
-algebras and affiliated elements

Definition D.1
.

A
multiplier
for a C
∗
-algebra
A
is a linear map
m\colon A\rightarrow A
for which there exists a linear map
m^{*}\colon A\rightarrow A
(necessarily unique) such that

(m^{*}b)^{*}a=b^{*}(ma),\qquad\forall b,a\in A.

By the closed graph theorem, multipliers are automatically norm-continuous. The space
M(A)
of multipliers then becomes a unital C
∗
-algebra under composition and the above
*
-structure. Identifying

A\subseteq M(A),\qquad a\mapsto(a^{\prime}\mapsto aa^{\prime}),

we have that
M(A)
contains
A
as a norm-closed
2
-sided ideal.

If
B
is a C
∗
-algebra containing
A
as a
*
-ideal, we get a
*
-homomorphism

B\rightarrow M(A),\qquad b\mapsto(a\mapsto ba).

If
A
is an
essential
ideal, so
b\in B
and
bA=0
implies
b=0
, we get a
*
-embedding

B\hookrightarrow M(A),

acting as identity on
A
. So,
M(A)
is the largest C
∗
-algebra containing
A
as an essential ideal.

For example, if
X
is a locally compact Hausdorff space, we have an identification

C_{b}(X)\cong M(C_{0}(X)),\qquad f\mapsto(g\mapsto fg).

As another example, let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. Then

\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\cong M(\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})),\qquad x\mapsto(y\mapsto xy).

If
A,B
are C
∗
-algebras, a
*
-homomorphism

\pi\colon A\rightarrow M(B)

is called
non-degenerate
if

B=[\pi(A)B].

If
A
is a
*
-algebra and
\pi\colon A\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a
*
-representation, one calls
\pi

non-degenerate
if

\mathcal{H}=[\pi(A)\mathcal{H}].

Viewing
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})=M(\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}))
, this is then the same as asking that
\pi\colon A\rightarrow M(\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}))
is non-degenerate.

Whenever
\pi\colon A\rightarrow M(B)
is a non-degenerate
*
-homomorphism between C
∗
-algebras, there exists a unique extension of
\pi
to a unital
*
-homomorphism

\pi\colon M(A)\rightarrow M(B).

We will also have need of the affiliation relation and affiliated elements
[
Wor91
]
(see also
[
WN92
,
Wor95
]
).

Definition D.2
.

Let
A
be a C
∗
-algebra. Let
z\in M(A)
be such that
\|z\|\leq 1
and

(D.1)

A=[(1-z^{*}z)^{1/2}A].

Then we define
T_{z}
to be the unbounded, densely defined operator

(D.2)

\mathscr{D}(T_{z}):=(1-z^{*}z)^{1/2}A\rightarrow A,\qquad(1-z^{*}z)^{1/2}a\mapsto za.

It is easily seen from (
D.1
) that
T_{z}
is well-defined.

Definition D.3
.

A densely defined linear operator
T\colon\mathscr{D}(T)\rightarrow A
is said to be
affiliated to
A
if
T=T_{z}
for some
z
as above. Notation:

T\eta A,\qquad A^{\eta}=\{T\mid T\eta A\}.

Remark D.4
.

•

One can show that
T_{z}=T_{w}
if and only if
w=z
, see the remark following
[
Wor91
, Proposition 2.2]
. Hence if
T\eta A
, it makes sense to write
z=z_{T}\in M(A)
for the unique contraction satisfying (
D.1
) and
T=T_{z}
.

•

Any
T\in M(A)
is affiliated to
A
, by taking

z=T(1+T^{*}T)^{-1/2}.

Conversely, if
T\eta A
and
T
is norm-bounded, then
T\in M(A)
.

For example, if
X
is a locally compact Hausdorff space, one shows (
[
Wor91
, Example 2]
) that

C_{0}(X)^{\eta}=C(X)=\{\textrm{continuous functions }X\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\},

where we view
f\in C(X)
as an operator
T=M_{f}
on
C_{0}(X)
via multiplication:

\mathscr{D}(M_{f})=\{g\in C_{0}(X)\mid fg\in C_{0}(X)\},\qquad M_{f}g=fg,\qquad g\in C_{0}(X).

As another example one shows (
[
Wor91
, Example 3]
) that, for
\mathcal{H}
a Hilbert space,

\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})^{\eta}=\{\textrm{closed, densely defined operators on }\mathcal{H}\},

by sending
T
to
z_{T}(1-z_{T}^{*}z_{T})^{-1/2}
(the latter in the sense of unbounded operators).

We will need the following simple observations on affiliated elements, which we gather in the following lemmas. See
[
Wor91
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
WN92
, Theorem 6.1]
for the proof of the respective statements.

Lemma D.5
.

Let
A,B
be C
∗
-algebras.

•

If
\pi\colon A\rightarrow M(B)
is a non-degenerate
*
-homomorphism, then for any
T\,\eta\,A
there exists a unique
\pi(T)\eta B
such that

z_{\pi(T)}=\pi(z_{T}).

•

If
T\,\eta\,A
and
S\,\eta\,B
, there exists a unique
(T\otimes S)\,\eta\,(A\otimes B)
such that

(D.3)

\mathscr{D}(T)\odot\mathscr{D}(S)\subseteq\mathscr{D}(T\otimes S),\quad(T\otimes S)(a\otimes b)=Ta\otimes Sb,\qquad a\in\mathscr{D}(T),b\in\mathscr{D}(S).

If
A
is a C
∗
-algebra, we call
an affiliated element
T\,\eta\,A

normal
if
z_{T}
is normal. We will then need the following form of functional calculus (see
[
Wor91
, Theorem 1.5, Theorem 1.6]
and the discussion following it). Note first that if

\alpha\colon\{z\mid|z|<1\}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad z\mapsto z(1-|z|^{2})^{-1/2}

and
g\in C_{0}(\mathbb{C})
, then
g\circ\alpha
is a
C_{0}
-function on the open unit disk, and in particular extends to a continuous function on the closed unit disk.

Lemma D.6

(Functional calculus)
.

Assume
A
is a C
∗
-algebra and
T\,\eta\,A
normal. Then there exists a unique map

C(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow A^{\eta},\qquad f\mapsto f(T)

such that
f(T)=\pi(f)
for
\pi
the non-degenerate
*
-homomorphism

\pi\colon C_{0}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow M(A),\qquad g\mapsto(g\circ\alpha)(z_{T}).

For example, if
A\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a non-degenerate faithful
*
-representation of
A
, then any normal
T\,\eta\,A
can be seen as an (unbounded) normal operator
\mathbf{T}
on
\mathcal{H}
, and the operator corresponding to
f(T)
will be
f(\mathbf{T})
as obtained through the usual functional calculus for normal operators on
\mathcal{H}
. In particular, we only need to have
f
defined as a continuous function on the closed set

\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{T})=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\mid\mathbf{T}-\lambda\textrm{ bijective as a map }\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}\}.

We need this observation in particular in case
\mathbf{T}
is a self-adjoint positive operator on
\mathcal{H}
, so

\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{T})\subseteq[0,\infty).

Appendix E
Rieffel’s deformation theory

Although the material in Section
4.1
only refers to the deformation of very specific function algebras, we recall some of the general framework of
[
Rie93
]
to have easy access to the results obtained there.

Let
A
be any C
∗
-algebra with a (not necessarily norm-continuous) action
\alpha
of
\hat{V}
by
*
-automorphisms. We then let
A^{u}\subseteq A
be the C
∗
-subalgebra of norm-continuous elements, i.e. those
a\in A
for which

(E.1)

\hat{v}\mapsto\alpha_{\hat{v}}(a)

is norm-continuous. We let
A^{\infty}\subseteq A^{u}
be the
*
-algebra of smooth functions, i.e. those for which the Banach-space valued functions (
E.1
) have infinite partial derivatives.

If now
A
is any C
∗
-algebra, we can endow the C
∗
-algebra
C_{b}(\hat{V},A)
of bounded functions
\hat{V}\rightarrow A
with the translation action
5
5

5

We change the direction of the translation action with respect to
[
Rie93
]
, to be in line with the other conventions in our paper. The resulting sign changes that appear are of course inconsequential.

(\tau_{\hat{v}}f)(\hat{w})=f(\hat{w}-\hat{v}),\qquad\hat{v},\hat{w}\in\hat{V},

and consider the associated
*
-algebra
C^{\infty}_{b}(\hat{V},A)
as above. Let
\mathcal{S}(\hat{V},A)\subseteq C^{\infty}_{b}(\hat{V},A)
be the corresponding space of Schwartz functions
f
, i.e. those functions such that for any iterated partial derivative
g
of
f
, the function
\hat{v}\mapsto p(\hat{v})g(\hat{v})
is bounded for any polynomial function
p
on
\hat{V}
. Then we obtain a well-defined map

\times_{\hbar}\colon C^{\infty}_{b}(\hat{V},A)\times\mathcal{S}(\hat{V},A)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(\hat{V},A),\qquad(f\times_{\hbar}g)(\hat{v})=\int_{V}f(\hat{v}-\mathcal{J}u)\hat{g}(u)e^{2\pi i\hat{v}\cdot u}\mathrm{d}\!\,u,

see
[
Rie93
, Proposition 3.3]
. There then exists a unique associative algebra structure
\times_{\hbar}
on
C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V},A)
which turns the above map into a module, and
C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V},A)
becomes a pre-C
∗
-algebra through the pointwise
*
-structure and the norm

\|f\|=\sup_{\|g\|_{A}\leq 1}\|f\times_{\hbar}g\|_{A},\qquad\|g\|_{A}^{2}=\left\|\int_{\hat{V}}g(\hat{v})^{*}g(\hat{v})\mathrm{d}\!\,\hat{v}\right\|,\qquad f\in C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V},A),g\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V},A),

see
[
Rie93
, Chapter 4]
. We denote

C_{b,\hbar}^{\infty}(\hat{V},A)=\textrm{ norm-completion of }C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V},A).

If then
A
is
any
C
∗
-algebra with a norm-continuous action
\alpha
of
\hat{V}
, we denote

\widetilde{\alpha}\colon A^{\infty}\rightarrow C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V},A),\qquad a\mapsto(\hat{v}\mapsto\alpha_{\hat{v}}(a)),

and we endow
A^{\infty}
with the pre-C
∗
-algebra structure inherited from
C_{b}^{\infty}(\hat{V},A)
through his map. We write
A_{\hbar}
for the completion of
A^{\infty}
, and we denote the resulting emedding of
A^{\infty}
into this completion as

\mathcal{Q}_{A}\colon A^{\infty}\rightarrow A_{\hbar},\qquad a\mapsto a_{\hbar}.

For example, if
A=C_{0}(\hat{V})
, it is not hard to show that
A^{\infty}=\mathcal{S}(\hat{V})
, and that the resulting quantization
C_{0,\hbar}(\hat{V})
is precisely
C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)
as constructed before.

What will be important for us, are the structural properties of the assignment

A\mapsto A_{\hbar}.

For this, we first recall the following result, which is
[
Rie93
, Proposition 5.9]
.

Proposition E.1
.

Let
B
be a C
∗
-algebra with a norm-continuous
\hat{V}
-action. Consider
C=M(B)
with its natural (not necessarily norm-continuous)
\hat{V}
-action. Then there exists a unique injective
*
-homomorphism

B_{\hbar}\rightarrow C_{\hbar},\qquad b_{\hbar}\mapsto b_{\hbar},\qquad b\in B^{\infty},

realizing
B_{\hbar}
as an essential two-sided
*
-ideal of
C_{\hbar}
.

In particular, we may view
C_{\hbar}\subseteq M(B_{\hbar})
.

Together with the previous proposition, the following theorem now follows from combining
[
Rie93
, Theorem 5.7, Proposition 5.8 and Proposition 5.10]
.

Theorem E.2
.

Let
A,B
be C
∗
-algebras with norm-continuous
\hat{V}
-actions, and assume that
\theta\colon A\rightarrow M(B)
is a non-degenerate
\hat{V}
-equivariant
*
-homomorphism. Then there exists a unique non-degenerate
*
-homomorphism

\theta_{\hbar}\colon A_{\hbar}\rightarrow M(B_{\hbar}),\qquad a_{\hbar}\mapsto\theta(a)_{\hbar},\qquad a\in A^{\infty}.

Moreover, if
\theta
is faithful, also
\theta_{\hbar}
is faithful, and if
\theta
has range in
B
(resp. is surjective with range
B
), then also
\theta_{\hbar}
has range in
B_{\hbar}
(resp. is surjective with range
B_{\hbar}
).

The above construction is functorial, in the sense that if
\theta\colon A\rightarrow M(B)
and
\kappa\colon B\rightarrow M(C)
are non-degenerate
*
-homomorphisms, then

(\kappa\circ\theta)_{\hbar}=\kappa_{\hbar}\circ\theta_{\hbar}.

We will apply this as follows.

First assume that we have a subspace
W\subseteq V
. Then the corresponding projection map
q\colon\hat{V}\twoheadrightarrow\hat{W}
leads to the non-degenerate
*
-homomorphism

\pi\colon C_{0}(\hat{W})\rightarrow C_{b}(\hat{V})=M(C_{0}(\hat{V})),\qquad f\mapsto f\circ q.

Moreover, this
*
-homomorphism is
\hat{V}
-equivariant when endowing
\hat{W}
with the translation action of
\hat{V}
through the quotient map. This leads to a non-degenerate
*
-homomorphism

C_{0,\hbar}(W)\rightarrow M(C_{0,\hbar}(V))=M(C_{\hbar}(V)).

A priori, the left hand side could be different from the canonical quantization
C_{\hbar}^{*}(W)
of
C_{0}(\hat{W})
viewing
W
as a skew-symmetric space in its own right. However, one checks that the above map, considered on elements of the form
f_{\hbar}
for
f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{W})
, coincides with the following map as defined on these elements:

C_{\hbar}^{*}(W)\rightarrow M(C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)),\qquad f_{\hbar}\mapsto\int_{W}\hat{f}(w)e_{\hbar}^{2\pi iw}\mathrm{d}\!\,w,\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(W),

using notation as in (
4.24
). Hence we can unambiguosly interpret
C_{0,\hbar}(W)=C^{*}_{\hbar}(W)
.

In particular, if
v\in V
and we take
W=\mathbb{R}v
, then we can canonically identify

(E.2)

\hat{W}\cong\mathbb{R},\qquad\hat{w}\mapsto\hat{w}\cdot v

with associated quotient map

(E.3)

\hat{V}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad\hat{w}\mapsto\hat{w}\cdot v.

We then find a canonical non-degenerate inclusion

(E.4)

\pi_{v,\hbar}\colon C_{0}(\mathbb{R})=C_{0}(\hat{W})=C_{0,\hbar}(\mathbb{R})=C_{\hbar}^{*}(\mathbb{R}v)\hookrightarrow M(C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)).

So, if
f\in C(\mathbb{R})
, then we can consider
f\,\eta\,C_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, and through Lemma
D.5
we obtain a corresponding element

f_{\hbar}=\pi_{v}(f)\in C^{*}_{\hbar}(V).

If in particular the function

z=z_{f}\colon\mathbb{R}\hat{v}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad\hat{w}\mapsto z(\hat{w})=\frac{f(\hat{w})}{(1+|f(\hat{w})|^{2})^{1/2}}

lies in
C_{b}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\hat{v})
, then the functoriality of our constructions gives concretely that

f_{\hbar}=T_{z_{\hbar}}.

For example, applying this to the identity function
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}}\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we find (after applying the functional calculus as in Lemma
D.6
to the exponential map) the element
e_{\hbar}^{v}
affiliated to
C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)
as in (
4.26
).

Resume now the setting as at the end of Section
4.1
, where we have chosen dual bases
\{f_{i}\},\{\hat{f}_{i}\}
of
V
and
\hat{V}
, together with a partitioning
I=I_{0}\sqcup I_{1}
. Then the translation action of
\hat{V}
on itself extends to a continuous action of
\hat{V}
on
\hat{V}_{+}
(where
-\infty
acts as a fixed point). Hence from the theory developed above, we see that (
4.31
) quantizes into a sequence of inclusions

(E.5)

C_{0,\hbar}(\hat{V})\subseteq C_{0,\hbar}(\hat{V}_{+})\subseteq C_{u,\hbar}(\hat{V}_{+})\subseteq C_{u,\hbar}(\hat{V})\subseteq M(C_{0,\hbar}(\hat{V}))

(where
C_{u}(\hat{V})
, resp.
C_{u}(\hat{V}_{*})
denotes the norm-continuous elements for the translation action in
C_{b}(\hat{V})
, resp.
C_{b}(\hat{V}_{+})
). By construction, this already gives that
C^{*}_{\hbar}(V_{+})
, as defined by (
4.32
), is indeed a C
∗
-algebra, equal to
C_{0,\hbar}(\hat{V}_{+})
in the notation above.

Let us now conclude the remainder of the proof of Theorem
4.7
. Assume that
v\in V_{+}
. Then (
E.3
) extends to a quotient map

\hat{V}_{+}\rightarrow[-\infty,\infty),\qquad\hat{w}\mapsto v\cdot\hat{w}.

The resulting non-degenerate
*
-homomorphism

C_{0}([-\infty,\infty))=C_{0,\hbar}([-\infty,\infty))\rightarrow M(C^{*}_{\hbar}(V_{+}))

is then simply the restriction of the corresponding map

\pi_{v,\hbar}\colon C_{b}(\mathbb{R})=M(C_{0}(\mathbb{R}))\rightarrow M(C^{*}_{\hbar}(V)).

Hence it is sufficient to observe that the exponential function
x\mapsto e^{x}
extends to a continuous function on
[-\infty,\infty)
, and so determines an element affiliated to
C_{0}([-\infty,\infty))
.

Theorem
4.8
follows similarly through functoriality. Indeed, assume first that
I_{0}=I_{0}^{\prime}
. Then
\iota_{I^{\prime}}
as in (
4.35
) is a
\hat{V}
-equivariant closed embedding when endowing
\hat{V}^{\prime}
with the translation action of
\hat{V}
under the quotient map
\hat{V}\twoheadrightarrow\hat{V}^{\prime}
. Hence (
4.34
) is provided through Theorem
E.2
. The formula (
4.36
) then follows from the fact that, again through functoriality,
\pi_{I^{\prime}}(e_{\hbar}^{f_{i}})
must coincide with
e_{\hbar}^{f_{i}\circ\iota_{I^{\prime}}}
.

For the general theorem, we may now already assume that
I_{1}^{\prime}=I_{1}
, and that
I_{0}^{\prime}
is such that
(f_{i},f_{j})=0
for each
i\in I_{0}^{\prime}\setminus I_{0}
. But in this case the
f_{i}
with
i\in I_{0}\setminus I_{0}^{\prime}
lie in the kernel of
\epsilon
, and the inclusion map
V^{\prime}\subseteq V

does
allow a splitting as a map between skew-symmetric spaces, through the section

s\colon V\rightarrow V^{\prime},\qquad\sum_{i\in I}v_{i}f_{i}\mapsto\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}}v_{i^{\prime}}f_{i}^{\prime}.

It follows that any unitary
\hbar
-representation
\pi
of
V^{\prime}
leads to a unitary
\hbar
-representation
\pi
of
V
through

\pi(v):=\pi(s(v)),

and we hence obtain a surjective
*
-homomorphism

\pi_{I^{\prime}}\colon C_{\hbar}^{*}(V)\rightarrow C_{\hbar}^{*}(V^{\prime}),\quad f_{\hbar}\mapsto(f\circ\hat{s})_{\hbar},\qquad f\in\mathcal{S}(\hat{V}).

Extending this map to
M(C_{\hbar}^{*}(V))
and restricting to
C_{\hbar}^{*}(V_{+})
leads to the map
\pi_{I^{\prime}}
as in Theorem
4.8
. As we can then also apply the extended map
\pi_{I^{\prime}}
to the element
e_{\hbar}^{v}
as affiliated to
C^{*}(V)
, this then also immediately proves (
4.36
) in this case.

Appendix F
von Neumann algebras and their weight theory

As a main reference for von Neumann algebras and their weight theory, we refer to
[
Tak02
,
Tak03
]
.

F.1.
von Neumann algebras

A von Neumann algebra
M
is any unital C
∗
-algebra allowing a predual as a Banach space. The resulting weak
∗
-topology on
M
is independent of the choice of predual, and called the
\sigma
-weak
or
ultraweak
topology. Canonically, one can then choose the predual of
M
to be the space
M_{*}\subseteq M^{*}
of
\sigma
-weakly continuous functionals.

For example, if
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space, then
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a von Neumann algebra with
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{*}
the functionals of the form

\omega(x)=\sum_{i}\langle\xi_{i},x\eta_{i}\rangle,\qquad\sum_{i}\|\xi_{i}\|^{2}<\infty,\sum_{i}\|\eta_{i}\|^{2}<\infty.

The following are equivalent for a unital
*
-subalgebra
M\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
:

•

M\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a von Neumann algebra whose
\sigma
-weak topology is induced by the one on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
.

•

M
is
\sigma
-weakly closed in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
.

•

M=M^{\prime\prime}
, where for a subset
S\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
we put

S^{\prime}=\{x\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\mid\forall y\in S:xy=yx\}.

Usually, one only calls
M\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
a von Neumann algebra if the above conditions are satisfied, i.e.
M\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is what is called
normally embedded
.

More generally, a unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi\colon M\rightarrow N
between von Neumann algebras is called
normal
if it is
\sigma
-weak-
\sigma
-weak continuous, which is the same as asking that it is dual to a norm-continuous linear map
\pi_{*}\colon N_{*}\rightarrow M_{*}
. For example, any
*
-isomorphism between von Neumann algebras is automatically normal.

A
normal unital
*
-representation
of a von Neumann algebra
M
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is any normal unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. One can show that any von Neumann algebra
M
has a faithful normal unital
*
-representation
\pi
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and then necessarily
\pi(M)\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a (normally embedded) von Neumann algebra with
M\cong\pi(M)
.

If
\pi\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a normal unital
*
-representation and
\mathcal{G}
is another Hilbert space, then we obtain another normal
*
-representation

\pi\otimes 1\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{G}),\quad x\mapsto\pi(x)\otimes 1,

called
amplification
of
\pi
.

If on the other hand
\pi\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a normal unital
*
-representation and
p\in\pi(M)^{\prime}
is a self-adjoint projection, then we obtain another normal
*
-representation

\pi_{p}\colon M\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(p\mathcal{H}),\quad x\mapsto\pi(x)p,

called
cut-down
of
\pi
(by
p
). One can then show:

Lemma F.1
.

Given a faithful normal unital
*
-representation
\pi
of a von Neumann algebra
M
, every other normal unital
*
-representation of
M
is, up to unitary equivalence, a cut-down of an amplification of
\pi
.

F.2.
General theory of weights

Fix
M\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
a von Neumann algebra on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and let
M_{+}
be the cone of positive elements in
M
.

Definition F.2
.

A
weight
on
M
is any map

\varphi\colon M_{+}\rightarrow[0,\infty]

such that

\varphi(x+y)=\varphi(x)+\varphi(y),\qquad\varphi(0)=0,\qquad\varphi(ax)=a\varphi(x),\qquad a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},x,y\in M_{+}.

One calls the weight

•

normal
if for any strongly converging increasing net
x_{i}\nearrow x
in
M_{+}
, it holds that

\varphi(x)=\lim_{i}\varphi(x_{i}),

•

faithful
if
\varphi(x)=0
for
x\in M_{+}
implies
x=0
,

•

semi-finite
if the linear span
\mathfrak{m}_{\varphi}
of

\mathfrak{m}_{\varphi}^{+}=\{x\in M_{+}\mid\varphi(x)<\infty\}

is
\sigma
-weakly dense in
M
, and

•

tracial
if

\varphi(xx^{*})=\varphi(x^{*}x),\qquad\forall x\in M.

We denote by
\mathfrak{W}_{M}
the set of all weights, and by
\mathfrak{W}_{M}^{\mathrm{nsf}}
the set of all normal, semi-finite, faithful (=nsf) weights (we also use the obvious variations).

Clearly any weight which is a pointwise supremum of normal states is normal, and conversely for any normal weight
\varphi
one has

\varphi(x)=\sup_{\omega\in\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}}\omega(x),\qquad\forall x\in M_{+},

where

\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}=\{\omega\in M_{*}^{+}\mid\omega\leq\varphi\},

and where for
\omega\in M_{*}^{+}
we write
\omega\leq\varphi
if this holds pointwise on
M_{+}
.

Example F.3
.

Assume
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space. Then we obtain a normal, semi-finite, faithful, tracial weight on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
via the usual trace

(F.1)

\mathrm{Tr}(x)=\sum_{i}\langle e_{i},xe_{i}\rangle,\qquad x\geq 0,

where
\{e_{i}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}
. Any other orthonormal basis gives the same trace.

Example F.4
.

More generally, if
\mathbf{T}
is a (strictly) positive operator on
\mathcal{H}
, one defines a normal semi-finite (faithful) weight

(F.2)

\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{T}}(x)=\sup_{r\geq 0}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathbf{T}_{r}^{1/2}x\mathbf{T}_{r}^{1/2})=\sup_{r\geq 0}\mathrm{Tr}(x^{1/2}\mathbf{T}_{r}x^{1/2}),\qquad x\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{+},

where
\mathbf{T}_{r}=\mathbf{T}\chi_{[0,r)}(\mathbf{T})
for
\chi_{Y}
the indicator function on a set
Y
. In fact, any semi-finite normal weight on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is of this form, for a unique
\mathbf{T}
.

Example F.5
.

The construction in Example
F.4
can be modified to an arbitrary von Neumann algebra
M\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
with an nsf trace
\tau
. Namely, assume that
\mathbf{T}
is a positive operator on
\mathcal{H}
which is
affiliated
to
M
in the sense of von Neumann algebras:

(F.3)

u\mathbf{T}u^{*}=\mathbf{T},\qquad\forall u\textrm{ unitary in }M^{\prime}.

Then
\mathbf{T}_{r}\in M
for all
0\leq r
, and one can deform
\tau
into a semifinite normal weight
\tau_{\mathbf{T}}
, exactly as before:

(F.4)

\tau_{\mathbf{T}}(x)=\sup_{r\geq 0}\tau(\mathbf{T}_{r}^{1/2}x\mathbf{T}_{r}^{1/2})=\sup_{r\geq 0}\tau(x^{1/2}\mathbf{T}_{r}x^{1/2}),\qquad x\in M_{+}.

We now recall the GNS-construction: with
\varphi
an nsf weight, put

\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}=\{x\in M\mid x^{*}x\in\mathfrak{m}_{\varphi}^{+}\}.

Then
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
is a left-sided ideal in
M
, and in case
\varphi
is tracial also a two-sided ideal with
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}^{*}=\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
. In general, one has

\mathfrak{m}_{\varphi}=\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}^{*}\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}.

By polarisation, it follows that
\varphi
can be extended to a linear functional on
\mathfrak{m}_{\varphi}
, and
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
becomes a Hilbert space with respect to the inner product

\langle x,y\rangle_{\varphi}:=\varphi(x^{*}y),\qquad x,y\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}.

One writes
L^{2}(M,\varphi)
for the completion, or simply
L^{2}(M)
if
\varphi
is understood. The natural embedding of
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}
into
L^{2}(M)
is written

\Lambda=\Lambda_{\varphi}\colon\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}\rightarrow L^{2}(M).

One further shows that there exists a unique normal faithful unital
*
-representation of
M
on
L^{2}(M)
such that

x\Lambda(y)=\Lambda(xy),\qquad x\in M,y\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}.

The celebrated
Tomita–Takesaki
theorem states that there exist a unique anti-linear involutive isometry
J_{\varphi}
(the modular conjugation) and strictly positive operator
\nabla_{\varphi}
(the modular operator) on
L^{2}(M)
such that

\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}^{*}\subseteq\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{\varphi}^{1/2}),\qquad J_{\varphi}\nabla_{\varphi}^{1/2}\Lambda_{\varphi}(x)=\Lambda_{\varphi}(x^{*}),\qquad x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}^{*}.

Moreover, one has

\nabla_{\varphi}^{it}M\nabla_{\varphi}^{-it}=M,\qquad J_{\varphi}MJ_{\varphi}=M^{\prime}.

The resulting one-parameter group of automorphisms of
M
is called the
modular

one-parameter-group
:

\sigma_{t}^{\varphi}\colon M\rightarrow M,\quad x\mapsto\nabla_{\varphi}^{it}x\nabla_{\varphi}^{-it},\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}.

It compensates for the non-traciality of arbitrary nsf weights: whereas an nsf weight
\varphi
is tracial if and only if
\sigma_{t}^{\varphi}=\mathrm{id}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, a general nsf weight satisfies

\varphi\circ\sigma_{t}^{\varphi}=\varphi,\qquad\varphi(xx^{*})=\varphi(\sigma_{-i/2}^{\varphi}(x)^{*}\sigma_{-i/2}^{\varphi}(x)),\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R},x\in\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}^{*},

where the notation indicates that
\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}^{*}\cap\mathfrak{n}_{\varphi}\subseteq\mathscr{D}(\sigma_{-i/2}^{\varphi})
. Here
x\in\mathscr{D}(\sigma_{z}^{\varphi})
for a
z\in\mathbb{C}
if there exists a (necessarily unique)
\sigma
-weakly continuous function
w\rightarrow\sigma_{w}^{\varphi}(x)
on the closed strip

\{w\mid\mathrm{Im}(w)\textrm{ between }0\textrm{ and }\mathrm{Im}(z)\}

which is holomorphic on its interior (as a Banach-space valued function) and extends
\sigma_{t}^{\varphi}(x)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Example F.6
.

Resume the setting of Example
F.4
. Then if
\mathbf{T}
is strictly positive and
\varphi=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{T}}
, one can identify

L^{2}(\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}),\varphi)\cong\mathcal{H}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{H}}

in a unique way such that

\Lambda(\theta_{\xi,\eta})=\xi\otimes\overline{\mathbf{T}^{1/2}\eta},\qquad\xi\in\mathcal{H},\eta\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{T}^{1/2}).

The modular conjugation and modular operator are given in this case by

J_{\varphi}(\xi\otimes\overline{\eta})=\eta\otimes\overline{\xi},\qquad\nabla_{\varphi}^{it}(\xi\otimes\overline{\eta})=\mathbf{T}^{it}\xi\otimes\overline{\mathbf{T}^{it}\eta},\qquad\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{H},

so in particular the modular one-parameter group is

\sigma_{t}^{\varphi}(x)=\mathbf{T}^{it}x\mathbf{T}^{-it},\qquad x\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}),t\in\mathbb{R}.

We also briefly comment on how affiliated positive operators (as in (
F.3
)) can be seen as being
dual
to the set of weights. Namely, define the
extended positive cone

M_{+}^{\mathrm{ext}}
of a von Neumann algebra
M
as the set of all

x\colon M_{*}^{+}\rightarrow[0,\infty],\qquad\omega\mapsto\omega(x)

such that there exists an increasing net
x_{i}\in M_{+}
with

\omega(x)=\sup_{i}\omega(x_{i}),\qquad\omega\in M_{*}^{+}.

Necessarily one then has

(\omega+\omega^{\prime})(x)=\omega(x)+\omega^{\prime}(x),\qquad 0(x)=0,\qquad(a\omega)(x)=a\omega(x),\qquad a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\omega\in M_{+}^{*},

and
x
satisfies the normality condition

\omega(x)=\lim_{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}(x)

whenever
\omega_{\alpha}
is an increasing net in
M_{*}^{+}
converging in norm to
\omega
. One then calls
x\in M^{\mathrm{ext}}_{+}

semi-finite
if

M_{*}^{+}=\textrm{closure of }\{\omega\in M_{*}^{+}\mid\omega(x)<\infty\},

and
faithful
if
\omega(x)=0
implies
\omega=0
for
\omega\in M_{*}^{+}
.

Then if
M\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between the (faithful) semi-finite
x\in M^{\mathrm{ext}}_{+}
and the (strictly) positive operators
\mathbf{x}
on
\mathcal{H}
which are affiliated to
M
, determined through

(F.5)

\omega_{\xi,\xi}(x)=\begin{cases}\|\mathbf{x}^{1/2}\xi\|^{2}&\text{ if }\xi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{x}^{1/2}),\\
\infty&\text{ if }\xi\notin\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{x}^{1/2}).\end{cases}

The abstract notion of the extended cone makes it easy to directly evaluate weights on its elements: for a general von Neumann algebra, we have an
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-bilinear pairing

\mathfrak{W}_{M}^{\mathrm{nsf}}\times M_{+}^{\mathrm{ext}}\rightarrow[0,\infty],\qquad\varphi(x)=\sup_{\omega,y}\omega(y),

where
\omega
ranges over
\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}
and
y
ranges over

\mathcal{G}_{x}=\{y\in M_{+}\mid y\leq x\},

where for
y\in M_{+}
we write
y\leq x
if this holds pointwise on
M_{*}^{+}
.

F.3.
Form sums

If
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space and
\mathbf{T},\mathbf{S}
are positive operators on
\mathcal{H}
, then

\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{T}}+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{S}}

is again a normal weight on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
, which is faithful if
\mathbf{T},\mathbf{S}
are strictly positive. By the result in Example
F.4
, we can make the following definition:

Definition F.7
.

If
\mathbf{T},\mathbf{S}
are strictly positive operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
such that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{T}}+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{S}}
is semi-finite, their
form sum
6
6

6

The notation
\mathbf{T}\dotplus\mathbf{S}
is more common, but we prefer to use the latter notation for the closure of the operator
\mathbf{T}+\mathbf{S}
with domain
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{T})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{S})
.

\mathbf{T}\boxplus\mathbf{S}
is the unique strictly positive operator such that

\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{T}\boxplus\mathbf{S}}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{T}}+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{S}}.

We have that
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{T}}+\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{S}}
is semi-finite if and only if
\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{T})\cap\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{S})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
.

Consider
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{P}
as in (
3.7
), as well as the rescaled operator

(F.6)

\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}=4\pi^{2}\hbar\mathbf{P}.

By the Stone-von Neumann theorem, Proposition
1.14
will follow once we prove the following:

Proposition F.8
.

The following identity of positive operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
holds:

(F.7)

e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}}\boxplus e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}\dotplus\mathbf{X}}=\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}}\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})^{*}.

We will need some preparations.

For
r>0
we consider the strips

(F.8)

S_{r}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid 0<\mathrm{Im}(z)<r\},\qquad S_{-r}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid-r<\mathrm{Im}(z)<0\},

(F.9)

T_{r}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid 0\leq\mathrm{Im}(z)\leq r\},\qquad T_{-r}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid-r\leq\mathrm{Im}(z)\leq 0\}.

For a general Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with self-adjoint operator

\mathbf{T}\colon\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow\mathcal{H},\qquad\mathscr{D}(\mathbf{T})\subseteq\mathcal{H},

and any
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, we define

\mathrm{Hol}(T_{r},\mathcal{H})=\{F\colon T_{r}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}\mid F\textrm{ continuous on $T_{r}$ and holomorphic on }S_{r}\}

and

H_{r}(\mathbf{T})=\{F\in\mathrm{Hol}(T_{r},\mathcal{H})\mid\forall z\in T_{r},t\in\mathbb{R}:F(z+t)=e^{it\mathbf{T}}F(z)\}.

The following is
[
Tak03
, Lemma VI.2.3]
.

Lemma F.9
.

The map

(F.10)

H_{r}(\mathbf{T})\rightarrow\mathscr{D}(e^{-r\mathbf{T}}),\qquad F\mapsto F(0)

is a well-defined linear isomorphism, with

e^{-r\mathbf{T}}(F(0))=F(ir),\qquad F\in H_{r}(\mathbf{T}).

We will write the inverse of (
F.10
) as

(F.11)

\mathscr{D}(e^{-r\mathbf{T}})\rightarrow H_{r}(\mathbf{T}),\qquad\xi\mapsto F_{\xi},

so that

e^{-r\mathbf{T}}\xi=F_{\xi}(ir),\qquad\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-r\mathbf{T}}).

Moreover, then clearly
\mathscr{D}(e^{-r\mathbf{T}})\subseteq\mathscr{D}(e^{z\mathbf{T}})
for all
z\in T_{r}
, with

(F.12)

e^{iz\mathbf{T}}\xi=F_{\xi}(z),\qquad\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-r\mathbf{T}}).

We now return to the specific case of the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and gather some extra information on the domain of
e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2}
with
\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}
as in (
F.6
).

For
r\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, write

\mathrm{Hol}(S_{r})=\{F\colon S_{r}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\mid F\textrm{ holomorphic}\},

and

H(S_{r})=\{f\in\mathrm{Hol}(S_{r})\mid\sup_{0<b<1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x+irb)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x<\infty\}\subseteq\mathrm{Hol}(S_{r}).

Lemma F.10
.

If
0<b
and
f\in H(S_{\pi b\hbar})
, there exists
\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-b\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})
such that

(F.13)

\xi(x)\overset{\textrm{a.e.}}{=}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f(x+i/n^{2}),

and we obtain in this way a linear isomorphism

(F.14)

H(S_{\pi b\hbar})\cong\mathscr{D}(e^{-b\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2}),\quad f\mapsto f_{\xi}.

Moreover, in this case we have

(F.15)

(e^{-b\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2}\xi)(x)\overset{\textrm{a.e.}}{=}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f(x+\pi ib\hbar-i/n^{2})

and, for all
z\in H(S_{\pi b\hbar})
,

(F.16)

(e^{-z\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2}\xi)(x)\overset{\textrm{a.e.}}{=}f(z+x).

Proof..

This is just a reformulation of
[
Sch94
, Lemma 1.1]
(which is an application of the Paley–Wiener theorem). More precisely,
[
Sch94
, Lemma 1.1]
states (
F.13
), the surjectivity of (
F.14
), and (
F.15
). The identity (
F.16
) and the injectivity of (
F.14
) then follow by applying this first part to
f
restricted to
S_{\pi b^{\prime}\hbar}
with
0<b^{\prime}<b
.
∎

If
f\in H(S_{\pi\hbar})
and
\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})
are related through (
F.13
), we write

f=f_{\xi}.

Similarly, if
f\in H(S_{\pi\hbar})
, we write
F\in H_{\pi\hbar}(2\pi\mathbf{P})
for the unique element related to
f
via (
F.16
), so

(F.17)

F(z)(x)\overset{\textrm{a.e.}}{=}f(z+x).

Then indeed
f_{\xi}
and
F_{\xi}
are related under this correspondence for
\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})
. We then also extend the definition domain of
f\in H(S_{\pi\hbar})
to
T_{\pi\hbar}
as

f(x+i\pi\hbar)=F(i\pi\hbar)(x),\qquad f(x)=F(0)(x),

with the understanding that this is only well-defined almost everywhere.

Lemma F.11
.

Let
\chi
be a holomorphic function on a domain
\Omega\supseteq T_{\hbar\pi}
, and assume there exist
C,D>0
with

(F.18)

|\chi(x+iy)|\leq Ce^{D|x|},\qquad\forall x+iy\in T_{\pi\hbar}.

Assume that
f\in H(S_{\pi\hbar})
, and define
g\in\mathrm{Hol}(S_{\pi\hbar})
by

g(z)=\chi(z)f(z),\qquad z\in S_{\pi\hbar}.

Then

(F.19)

\sup_{0<b<1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x+ib)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x=\max\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x,\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x\right\}.

Proof..

For any
\eta\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R})
with
\|\eta\|_{2}\leq 1
we obtain a holomorphic function

z\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(z+t)\overline{\eta(t)}\mathrm{d}\!\,t=\langle\overline{\chi}(z+\mathbf{X})\eta,F(z)\rangle.

Using (
F.18
), together with the uniform boundedness of
\|F(z)\|_{2}
, this function satisfies the conditions for the Phragmen-Lindelöf theorem, by which it then follows that

\sup_{0<b<1}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(t+\pi\hbar b)\overline{\eta(t)}\mathrm{d}\!\,t\right|^{2}\leq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}\cup(\mathbb{R}+\pi i\hbar)}|\langle\overline{\chi}(z+\mathbf{X})\eta,F(z)\rangle|^{2}\\
\leq\max\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x,\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x\right\}.

Taking the supremum over all such
\eta
, we find

\sup_{0<b<1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(t+\pi\hbar b)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,t\leq\max\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x,\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x\right\}.

The converse direction follows from (
F.13
),(
F.15
) and Fatou’s lemma.
∎

Lemma F.12
.

We have

(F.20)

\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2\dotplus\mathbf{X}/2})=e^{i\mathbf{X}^{2}/4\pi\hbar}\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2}),

with

(F.21)

e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2\dotplus\mathbf{X}/2}=e^{i\mathbf{X}^{2}/4\pi\hbar}e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2}e^{-i\mathbf{X}^{2}/4\pi\hbar},

Proof..

The identity (
F.21
) follows from (
B.1
), and (
F.20
) is then an immediate consequence.
∎

Lemma F.13
.

We have
\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})\cap\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2\dotplus\mathbf{X}/2})
if and only if there exists
f\in H(S_{\pi\hbar})
with
f_{\mid\mathbb{R}}=\xi
and

(F.22)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+e^{x})|f(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x<\infty.

Proof..

Assume first that
\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})\cap\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2\dotplus\mathbf{X}/2})
. By Lemma
F.10
and equation (
F.20
), we can then find
f,g\in H(S_{\pi\hbar})
with associated
F,G\in H_{\pi\hbar}(2\pi\mathbf{P})
such that

F(0)=\xi,\qquad G(0)=e^{-i\mathbf{X}^{2}/4\pi\hbar}\xi.

We claim that then

(F.23)

g(z)=e^{-iz^{2}/4\pi\hbar}f(z),\qquad\forall z\in S_{\pi\hbar}.

Indeed, pick
\eta\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R})
. Then we obtain the holomorphic function

S_{\pi\hbar}\ni z\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(z+t)\overline{\eta}(t)\mathrm{d}\!\,t=\langle\eta,G(z)\rangle.

On the other hand, also

S_{\pi\hbar}\ni z\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(z+t)e^{-i(z+t)^{2}/4\pi\hbar}\overline{\eta}(t)\mathrm{d}\!\,t=\langle e^{i(\overline{z}+\mathbf{X})^{2}/4\pi\hbar}\eta,F(z)\rangle

is holomorphic. Since these functions extend continuously to the same function on
\mathbb{R}
, they must be equal. This immediately implies (
F.23
).

Since now
g\in H(S_{\pi\hbar})
and
\chi(z)=e^{-iz^{2}/4\pi\hbar}
satisfies the conditions in Lemma
F.11
, we can conclude from equation (
F.19
) that (
F.22
) holds.

Conversely, if
\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})
with associated function
f=f_{\xi}
that satisfies (
F.22
), define
g\in\mathrm{Hol}(S_{\pi\hbar})
via (
F.23
). Applying again Lemma
F.11
, we find
g\in H(S_{\pi\hbar})
. But through the identity (
F.13
), this implies precisely

\xi\in e^{i\mathbf{X}^{2}/4\pi\hbar}\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})=\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2\dotplus\mathbf{X}/2})

as required.
∎

For the next lemma, we recall that
\varphi_{\hbar}
is unimodular on the real line, so that

(F.24)

\overline{\varphi_{\hbar}(\overline{z})}\varphi_{\hbar}(z)=1,\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}.

On the other hand, by the identity (
A.13
) we have

(F.25)

\varphi_{\hbar}(z-\pi i\hbar)=(1+e^{z})\varphi_{\hbar}(z+\pi i\hbar),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C},

so combined with (
F.24
) we find

(F.26)

|\varphi_{\hbar}(x+\pi i\hbar)|=(1+e^{x})^{-1/2},\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}.

Lemma F.14
.

We have

(F.27)

\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})\cap\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2\dotplus\mathbf{X}/2})=\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2}).

Proof..

Assume first that
\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})\cap\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2\dotplus\mathbf{X}/2})
, and put
f=f_{\xi}
. Then by holomorphicity of
\varphi_{\hbar}^{-1}
on the strip
S_{\pi\hbar}
we get that

z\mapsto g(z):=f(z)/\varphi_{\hbar}(z)

defines a function in
\mathrm{Hol}(S_{\pi\hbar})
. Now by the growth estimates in (
A.16
) and (
A.17
), the function

\chi(z)=1/\varphi(z)
satisfies the conditions in Lemma
F.11
. Then since by Lemma
F.13
the inequality (
F.22
) holds, we conclude from the identity (
F.26
) and Lemma
F.11
that
g\in H(S_{\pi\hbar})
. But then
\xi\in\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})
, since

\xi(x)/\varphi_{\hbar}(x)\overset{\textrm{a.e.}}{=}\lim_{n\rightarrow 0}g(x+\frac{i}{n^{2}}).

Assume now conversely that
\xi=\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})\eta
for
\eta\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})
. Let
g=f_{\eta}
be its associated function in
H(S_{\pi\hbar})
, and
G=F_{\eta}\in H_{\pi\hbar}(2\pi\mathbf{P})
. Put

z\mapsto f(z):=\varphi_{\hbar}(z)g(z)

as a function in
\mathrm{Hol}(S_{\pi\hbar})
. Then we can extend
f
to a measurable function on
\mathbb{R}+\pi i\hbar
such that

f(x+\pi i\hbar)\overset{\textrm{a.e.}}{=}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}f(x+\pi i\hbar-i/n^{2}),

and using again (
F.26
) we compute

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+e^{x})|f(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|f(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}}{|\varphi_{\hbar}(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\!\,x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|g(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x=\|G(\pi i\hbar)\|^{2}<\infty.

Moreover, we then also have that
f\in H(S_{\pi\hbar})
, by another application of Lemma
F.11
. Since
\xi
realizes the boundary value of
f
on
\mathbb{R}
, we can hence conclude by Lemma
F.13
that
\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})\cap\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2\dotplus\mathbf{X}/2})
.
∎

We can now prove the identity (
F.7
). Indeed, it is enough to show that with
\theta_{\xi}
the projection onto a unit vector
\xi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

(F.28)

\mathrm{Tr}_{e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}}}(\theta_{\xi})+\mathrm{Tr}_{e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}\dotplus\mathbf{X}}}(\theta_{\xi})=\mathrm{Tr}_{\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}}\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})^{*}}(\theta_{\xi}).

Now the left hand side is finite if and only if

(F.29)

\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})\cap\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2\dotplus\mathbf{X}/2}),

while the right hand side is finite if and only if
\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})^{*}\xi\in\mathscr{D}(e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2})
. By Lemma
F.14
, these are the same sets of vectors. Moreover, for a vector in this set, we then easily compute, using again (
F.26
) and the computations in the above lemmas, that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}}\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})^{*}}(\theta_{\xi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}/2}\varphi_{\hbar}(\mathbf{X})^{*}\xi\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|f_{\xi}(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}}{|\varphi_{\hbar}(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}}\mathrm{d}\!\,x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+e^{x})|f_{\xi}(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f_{\xi}(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x+\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{x}|f_{\xi}(x+\pi i\hbar)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\!\,x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}}}(\theta_{\xi})+\mathrm{Tr}_{e^{-\mathbf{P}_{\hbar}\dotplus\mathbf{X}}}(\theta_{\xi}).

F.4.
Actions on von Neumann algebras

Assume that
G
is a locally compact Hausdorff group. Let
M
be a von Neumann algebra. Then we say
G
acts on
M
, and write
G\underset{\alpha}{\curvearrowright}M
, if we are given
*
-automorphisms
\alpha_{g}
of
M
for
g\in G
giving an algebraic action and satisfying the continuity condition that for all
x\in M
, the map

\alpha(x)\colon G\rightarrow M,\qquad g\mapsto\alpha_{g}(x)

is
\sigma
-weakly continuous. Then the
G
-fixed points of
M
form a von Neumann subalgebra

M^{\alpha}=\{x\in M\mid\forall g\in G:\alpha_{g}(x)=x\}.

Then with
\lambda
a left Haar integral on
M
, one can define on
M
a unique map

(F.30)

T_{\alpha}\colon M_{+}^{\mathrm{ext}}\rightarrow M_{+}^{\alpha,\mathrm{ext}}

such that

\omega_{M^{\alpha}}(T_{\alpha}(x))=\int_{G}(\omega\circ\alpha_{g})(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,\lambda(g),\qquad\omega\in M_{*}^{+},x\in M_{+}^{\mathrm{ext}}.

One refers to
T_{\alpha}
as an
operator valued weight
. It again satisfies the normality condition that if
x_{i}\nearrow x
strongly, then
T_{\alpha}(x_{i})\nearrow T_{\alpha}(x)
strongly.

In particular, if
\alpha
is ergodic, that is,
M^{\alpha}=\mathbb{C}1_{M}
, then
T_{\alpha}
is itself a normal weight, which we write as
\varphi_{\alpha}
. It hence satisfies that, for
\omega
an
arbitrary
normal state, on
M
,

(F.31)

\varphi_{\alpha}(x)=\int_{G}(\omega\circ\alpha_{g})(x)\mathrm{d}\!\,\lambda(g),\qquad x\in M_{+}^{\mathrm{ext}}.

This weight is necessarily faithful. One then calls an ergodic action
integrable
if
\varphi_{\alpha}
is also semi-finite.

Appendix G
Proof of Theorem
2.33

Recall that

(G.1)

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B^{b,r,i}.

By similar direct calculations to those in Lemma
2.28
, one shows the following result.

Lemma G.1
.

Assuming
1\leqslant i\leqslant r\leqslant b\leqslant n
and similarly for the triple
(b^{\prime},r^{\prime},i^{\prime})
we have

(G.2)

\left[B^{b,r,i},B^{b^{\prime},r^{\prime},i^{\prime}}\right]

whenever any of the following conditions is satisfied:

(1)

r^{\prime}=r
;

(2)

\left|b^{\prime}-i^{\prime}-b+i\right|>1
;

(3)

b^{\prime}-i^{\prime}=b-i+1
,
b^{\prime}<b
,
b^{\prime}-r^{\prime}\leqslant b-r
;

(4)

b^{\prime}-i^{\prime}=b-i+1
,
b^{\prime}\geqslant b
,
b^{\prime}-r^{\prime}>b-r
;

(5)

b^{\prime}-i^{\prime}=b-i
,
(b^{\prime}-b)(b^{\prime}-r^{\prime}-b+r)>0
.

Corollary G.2
.

Relation (
G.2
) holds whenever
r^{\prime}>r
and any of the following conditions is satisfied:

(1)

b^{\prime}<b
;

(2)

r^{\prime}-i^{\prime}<r-i
;

(3)

r^{\prime}-i^{\prime}=r-i
and
b^{\prime}-r^{\prime}>b-r
.

Proof..

We prove part (1), the other two cases are analogous. First, note that
b^{\prime}<b
and
r^{\prime}>r
imply
b^{\prime}-r^{\prime}<b-r
. By Lemma
G.1
, we only need to consider possibilities

\displaystyle b^{\prime}-i^{\prime}

\displaystyle=b-i,

\displaystyle b^{\prime}-i^{\prime}

\displaystyle=b-i\pm 1.

In all three case, the result follows from Lemma
G.1
.
∎

Let us set

B^{b,r}=\prod_{i=1}^{r}B^{b,r,i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad B^{b}=\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}B^{b,r}.

This way we have

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}B^{b,r}.

Applying part (1) of Corollary
G.2
, we see that that the flip
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
can be written as

(G.3)

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}B^{b}.

In what follows, we shall also use the following expressions for
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
. The first one is

(G.4)

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant j\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant c\leqslant n-r+1}^{\longrightarrow}B^{n-c+1,r,r-j+1}.

and can be obtained from (
G.1
) by using part (1) of Lemma
G.1
to write

(G.5)

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longleftarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longleftarrow}B^{b,r,i},

and re-parametrizing
j=r-i+1
,
c=n-b+1
.

The second one is

(G.6)

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B^{n-r+i,b+i-1,i},

and can be obtained from (
G.5
) by commuting factors
B^{b^{\prime},r^{\prime},i^{\prime}}
past
B^{b,r,i}
whenever
r^{\prime}>r
and conditions (2) or (3) of Corollary
G.2
hold.

Finally, the third one is derived in an analogous fashion and reads

(G.7)

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B^{b+r-1,b+i-1,i}.

Now the entire proof of Theorem
2.33
only consists of spelling out a picture in
[
DGG16
]
. (Here we feel obliged to remark that in our opinion mathematical thought ought to go the opposite way — from formulas to pictures).

We start by using formulas (
G.3
) and (
G.6
) to rewrite the left hand side as

\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,b+i-1,i}.

We will now be transforming this expression step by step, so that after
s
steps we arrive at

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant s}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-i+1,b,b-r+1}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i}\cdot

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant a\leqslant s+1}^{\longrightarrow}\Bigg(\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n+a-s-1,r,i}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,s+i,s+i-a+1}\cdot

\displaystyle\prod_{n-s+1\leqslant r\leqslant n-a+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[13]}^{a+r-1,a+i-1,i}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\prod_{s+2\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,b+i-1,i}\prod_{n-s+1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i}.

After
n
steps we arrive at expression

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-i+1,b,b-r+1}\cdot

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant a\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-a+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[13]}^{a+r-1,a+i-1,i}\cdot

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i},

which equals the right hand side, as the three factors are respectively by formulas (
G.4
), (
G.7
), and (
G.1
). Performing the
s
-th step consists of applying Lemma
G.3
and shuffling commuting factors. Namely, after applying the lemma, inside the parenthesis, where the product over
a
is taken, we get

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+1,s+1,c+2}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,s+i+1,c+i+2}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[13]}^{n-c-1,s+r-c-1,r}

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n-c-1,r,i}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n-c-1,n-s,i}\cdot\prod_{n-s+1\leqslant r\leqslant n-a+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[13]}^{a+r-1,a+i-1,i}.

Then we observe that the factors
B_{[23]}
in the second line commute with the factors
B_{[13]}
. This way
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[23]}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[12]}
equals

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant s}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-i+1,b,b-r+1}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i}\cdot

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant a\leqslant s+1}^{\longrightarrow}\Bigg(\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+1,s+1,s-a+2}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,s+i+1,s+i-a+2}\cdot

\displaystyle\prod_{n-s\leqslant r\leqslant n-a+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[13]}^{a+r-1,a+i-1,i}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n+a-s-1,r,i}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n+a-s-1,n-s,i}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\prod_{s+2\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,b+i-1,i}\prod_{n-s+1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i}.

Using (
2.2
), one can now easily verify the following. First, factors

\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+1,s+1,s-a+2}

can be commuted all the way to the left past the product

\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i},

so that the first big factor in our expression becomes

\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant s}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-i+1,b,b-r+1}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant a\leqslant s+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+1,s+1,s-a+2}=\\
=\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant s+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-i+1,b,b-r+1}.

Second, the factors

\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n+a-s-1,n-s,i}

can be similarly commuted all the way to the right past the factor

\prod_{s+2\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,b+i-1,i},

so that the very last factor becomes

\prod_{1\leqslant a\leqslant s+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n+a-s-1,n-s,i}\cdot\prod_{n-s+1\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i}=\prod_{n-s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i}.

At this point we have

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant s+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-i+1,b,b-r+1}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant b\leqslant n-s-2}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant b}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i}\cdot

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant a\leqslant s+2}^{\longrightarrow}\Bigg(\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n+a-s-2,r,i}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,s+i+1,s+i-a+2}\cdot

\displaystyle\prod_{n-s\leqslant r\leqslant n-a+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[13]}^{a+r-1,a+i-1,i}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\prod_{s+3\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-b+1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,b+i-1,i}\prod_{n-s\leqslant r\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{r\leqslant b\leqslant n}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{b,r,i},

which is precisely the expression
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[23]}\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{[12]}
after
s+1
steps. This completes the proof.

Lemma G.3
.

The following equality holds (here
c=s-a
):

\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n-c-1,r,i}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,s+i,c+i+1}=\\
\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+1,s+1,c+2}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,s+i+1,c+i+2}\\
\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[13]}^{n-c-1,s+r-c-1,r}\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n-c-1,r,i}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n-c-1,n-s,i}.

Proof..

As always using (
2.2
), we see that the left hand side is equal to

\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}B_{[23]}^{n-c-1,r,i}B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,s+i,c+i+1},

which in turn equals

\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}\varphi\left(0\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{n-c-i,n-c-r-1}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{c+i+1,c+1}\right)\varphi\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{n-c-r,n-s-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{c+r+1,r-i}\oplus 0\right)

Applying the pentagon relation for quantum dilogarithms (one easily verifies that (
1.26
) is valid), we get

\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}^{\longrightarrow}C_{[12]}^{r,i}C_{[13]}^{r,i}C_{[23]}^{r,i}.

where

\displaystyle C_{[12]}^{r,i}

\displaystyle=B_{[12]}^{n-r+i,s+i,c+i+1}=\varphi\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{n-c-r,n-s-r}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{c+r+1,r-i}\oplus 0\right)

\displaystyle C_{[13]}^{r,i}

\displaystyle=\varphi\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{n-c-r,n-s-r}\oplus\left(\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{c+r+1,r-i}+\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{n-c-i,n-c-r-1}\right)\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{c+i+1,c+1}\right)

\displaystyle C_{[23]}^{r,i}

\displaystyle=B_{[23]}^{n-c-1,r,i}=\varphi\left(0\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\nearrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\nearrow$}}}{e}_{n-c-i,n-c-r-1}\oplus\overset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.7}{$\displaystyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\textstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptstyle\searrow$}}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\searrow$}}}{e}_{c+i+1,c+1}\right).

Note that

C_{[13]}^{r,r}=B_{[13]}^{n-c-1,s+r-c-1,r}.

Using our favorite commutation relations (
2.2
) we then arrive at

\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}C_{[12]}^{r,1}\prod_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\left(C_{[13]}^{k,k}\prod_{1\leqslant j\leqslant k}^{\longrightarrow}\left(C_{[23]}^{k,j}C_{[13]}^{k+1,j}C_{[12]}^{k+1,j+1}\right)\right)C_{[13]}^{n-s,n-s}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}C_{[23]}^{n-s,i}.

Using the pentagon relation once again, we obtain

\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}C_{[12]}^{r,1}\prod_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\left(C_{[13]}^{k,k}\prod_{1\leqslant j\leqslant k}^{\longrightarrow}\left(C_{[12]}^{k+1,j+1}C_{[23]}^{k,j}\right)\right)C_{[13]}^{n-s,n-s}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}C_{[23]}^{n-s,i},

and further commute it to the form

\prod_{1\leqslant r\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}C_{[12]}^{r,1}\prod_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant j\leqslant k}^{\longrightarrow}C_{[12]}^{k+1,j+1}\prod_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}C_{[13]}^{k,k}\prod_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n-s-1}^{\longrightarrow}\prod_{1\leqslant j\leqslant k}^{\longrightarrow}C_{[23]}^{k,j}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n-s}^{\longrightarrow}C_{[23]}^{n-s,i}.

This in turn equals the desired expression.
∎
